## Instructions

You are a research assistant performing **Stage 2 of Thomas & Harden (2008) thematic synthesis**: grouping coded evidence into **descriptive themes** for a systematic literature review on quantum computing in financial services.

The evidence has already been open-coded at the text level (Stage 1 = A1). You receive the coded evidence in the form of analytical memos (A2) and, for multi-silo papers, silo-specific overlays. You DO NOT see the raw papers. Every theme you generate must be grounded in specific code IDs from the memos so a reviewer can trace back to the exact text span.

## Inputs

### Silo context
Silo: Credit & Lending (CL)
Batch ID: 1 of 1
Papers in this batch: 63
Minimum supporting papers per theme: 3

### Evidence blocks (one per paper)
Two block types appear; treat both uniformly as evidence of what that paper contributes:

- `type: central` — 9-dimension analytical memo (problem framing, quantum method, results, classical comparison, limitations, future work, novelty, methodological notes). Each dimension cites `support_codes` = A1 code IDs.
- `type: central+overlay` — same central memo PLUS a silo-specific overlay highlighting `key_points` most relevant to THIS silo. Multi-silo papers use this form; the overlay emphasises silo-X-specific readings but the central memo remains the evidence spine.

Both block types carry code IDs (e.g., `C-001`). You must cite these IDs in your themes.

=== PAPER 01e7ba36bcfc (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper studies a hybrid quantum–classical neural network for realistic classification tasks, specifically including a credit-scoring (finance) dataset and MNIST, motivated by the need to handle datasets with up to ~1000 features, more than 1000 samples, and potentially unbalanced targets. The finance problem is framed as a supervised binary classification task on credit-default data, with both balanced and unbalanced versions of the dataset constructed to test performance under realistic class imbalance.
    [codes: C-001, C-005, C-017, C-018, C-027]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors employ a hybrid quantum–classical network in which a classical neural network front-end partially performs the encoding and downscales high-dimensional features into parameters for a variational quantum circuit, allowing the number of qubits to be chosen independently of the number of input features and output classes. The quantum circuit uses angle encoding with a per-qubit sequence of Ry(φ1)·Rz(φ2)·Ry(φ3) rotations followed by entangling CNOT layers, repeated in multiple variational layers whose parameter count scales as n = 3·nqubits·nlayers, and integrates a data re-uploading scheme to improve expressivity. Training is implemented in TensorFlow Quantum to enable seamless optimization of classical and quantum parameters within the same framework.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-015, C-028]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Across finance and MNIST experiments, the hybrid network (QNet) is reported to show an advantage over a purely classical network on several performance measures, including cases where it outperforms the classical benchmark on a balanced credit-scoring sample and exhibits faster learning progress and superior performance on MNIST test data. The study finds that using data re-uploading yields better classification performance than repeating only the variational block, and that for MNIST there is a threshold in network complexity: increasing qubits initially improves accuracy with a noticeable jump between two and four qubits followed by a performance plateau and a weakening dependence on the number of layers as qubit count grows. Regularization experiments show that adding a dropout layer between the quantum and second classical network can bring training and test accuracies into alignment around 0.75, while L2 regularization on measurement outputs can be tuned to reduce overfitting.
    [codes: C-002, C-016, C-022, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-037, C-039]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The hybrid quantum–classical model is systematically compared against a fully classical neural network baseline (NNet) with a comparable number of trainable parameters. On the balanced finance sample, the hybrid approach is explicitly reported to achieve better performance than this classical benchmark, and on MNIST the authors state that the hybrid QNet learns faster and maintains its performance advantage on independent test data, indicating a potential predictive benefit of including the quantum layer in this specific hybrid architecture.
    [codes: C-001, C-014, C-019, C-022, C-025, C-026, C-029, C-030]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors repeatedly acknowledge overfitting as a central limitation: they note that overfitting arises as a recurring issue in their hybrid network, explicitly observe overfitting when applying trained models to unseen finance data, and detect overfitting for the MNIST task where near-perfect training confusion matrices do not translate to equally strong test performance. They caution that accuracy can be a misleading metric on unbalanced datasets and hence emphasize the need to consider additional measures like AUC to avoid misinterpreting performance. In their regularization study, they report that L2 regularization on the angles fed into the quantum network harms performance to the extent that the network loses its ability to classify sensibly, indicating a significant limitation of that regularization strategy.
    [codes: C-003, C-019, C-020, C-024, C-032, C-038]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The paper calls for more detailed, data-specific investigations to understand for which kinds of datasets particular hybrid architectures are suitable, indicating that network performance may depend strongly on dataset characteristics. It also highlights that regularization of the quantum-parameter part of the network has not been fully explored and warrants a more thorough investigation. Finally, the authors note that all experiments are performed on a simulator and propose future work to examine how realistic error rates on physical quantum devices would affect their results.
    [codes: C-041, C-042, C-043]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is positioned as an analysis of a hybrid quantum–classical network capable of handling non-trivial, realistically sized classification problems, including a finance credit-scoring dataset with up to ~1000 features and class imbalance, by leveraging a classical front-end that decouples qubit count from feature dimensionality. The authors additionally present a comparative study of data re-uploading versus simpler layer repetition in the quantum circuit, explore how performance depends on the number of qubits and layers, and propose and empirically evaluate several regularization strategies—particularly showing that regularizing only classical measurements can mitigate overfitting without directly constraining quantum parameters.
    [codes: C-001, C-005, C-007, C-008, C-023, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-039, C-040]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study emphasizes realism in dataset design by targeting settings with many features, sufficient training samples, and unbalanced targets, including constructing both balanced and unbalanced samples from a credit-scoring dataset and carefully discussing appropriate performance metrics such as AUC alongside accuracy for unbalanced data. The hybrid model architecture and hyperparameterization are described in detail, including explicit formulas for parameter counts, and a classical baseline network is defined for fair comparison. The implementation uses TensorFlow Quantum to allow joint training of classical and quantum components, with the authors noting that circuits up to 10 qubits and 10 layers can be simulated on modest hardware in a few hours, but they also acknowledge that all experiments are simulator-based and that the impact of hardware noise remains to be tested. Their regularization study is documented with specific techniques (dropout, L2 on angles or measurements) and quantitative outcomes in some cases, though the paper also concedes that quantum-parameter regularization requires more systematic study.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-008, C-011, C-012, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-027, C-028, C-033, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-042, C-043]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None; all verified codes could be assigned to the prior dimensions.
    [codes: —]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it directly evaluates a credit-scoring dataset and shows how a hybrid quantum-classical model can handle realistic loan-default prediction settings, including class imbalance and high-dimensional borrower features. Its most useful takeaways for lending are the comparison against a classical baseline, the emphasis on AUC over accuracy for unbalanced default data, and the regularization results that address overfitting risk in credit models.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: The paper directly targets credit-scoring/default prediction and tests both balanced and unbalanced loan-default samples.
    [codes: C-001, C-017, C-018  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The hybrid model is designed to downscale many borrower features into a quantum circuit, making high-dimensional credit data more tractable.
    [codes: C-006, C-008, C-028  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: On the balanced credit sample, the hybrid model outperforms the classical benchmark.
    [codes: C-014, C-022  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: For unbalanced default data, accuracy alone is misleading and AUC is needed to distinguish models.
    [codes: C-019, C-020, C-021, C-025, C-026  dim_source: dimension_8]

    [codes: C-003, C-024, C-036, C-037, C-039, C-040  dim_source: dimension_5]

=== PAPER 0757b9aa9a3b (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper tackles feature selection for financial risk assessment tasks—specifically fraud and credit default detection—by formulating feature selection as a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) problem to be solved on quantum annealers. The motivation is that high-dimensional datasets with irrelevant or redundant features are computationally expensive to train on and can cause overfitting, while feature selection is NP-hard for classical computers, suggesting potential benefits from quantum optimization in these finance applications.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use quantum annealing as the quantum computing paradigm, arguing it is well-suited to solving QUBO formulations with relatively strong reliability despite being less versatile than gate-based devices. They implement feature selection by building QUBO matrices from information-theoretic and correlation-based criteria, then solve them on D-Wave hardware via a hybrid solver that decomposes larger, densely connected QUBOs into smaller subproblems due to current QPU connectivity and size limits; feature subsets are then evaluated by training ML models and measuring F1 and Gini.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-024, C-025]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Experiments indicate that QUBO-based feature selection on quantum computers can, in aggregate, perform better than classical feature-selection methods on the evaluated fraud and default datasets, though the gains are often modest and formulation- and k-dependent. For small fraud data with k=13, parametric-MI QUBO yields the best F1 while MI-Pearson gives the best Gini, whereas at k=25 a classical MI-Spearman method dominates F1 and Pearson-QUBO dominates Gini; MIQUBO is the worst technique on the small fraud dataset but performs best on the larger fraud dataset when only a small number of features are selected. Across datasets, MIQUBO, MI-Pearson, and parametric-MI slightly outperform a full-feature baseline and, on the default dataset, MIQUBO, parametric-MI QUBO, and MI-Pearson QUBO show similar ML performance for smaller feature subsets. The authors also find that the parametric scaling (alpha) materially affects performance when only a small number of features are selected but becomes largely irrelevant as k increases.
    [codes: C-003, C-018, C-019, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study explicitly compares quantum QUBO feature selection against classical approaches including Random Forest feature importance and mutual-information plus Spearman correlation feature selection, as well as a classical full-feature baseline. The authors state that QUBO-based quantum feature selection consistently performs better than classical methods overall, but detailed results show a mixed picture: on the small fraud dataset, classical MI-Spearman dominates F1 at k=25 while QUBO formulations lead for some other k and metrics, MIQUBO can be worst in certain regimes, and on larger datasets the main quantum QUBO methods only slightly outperform the baseline. They also report that simulated annealing, a classical optimizer, achieves similar performance to quantum annealing for parametric-MI QUBO, which they attribute to analogous mechanisms for escaping local minima.
    [codes: C-003, C-008, C-012, C-028, C-029, C-031, C-032, C-035]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that current quantum annealers have limited connectivity and can only handle small or sparsely connected QUBOs directly, necessitating the use of a hybrid solver that decomposes larger problems, which constrains scalability and may dilute any pure quantum contribution. They further note that one of their parametric formulations may introduce an artificial increase in distances, and suggest that the scaling factor might be compensating for this artifact, indicating a modeling limitation. The discussion also concedes that quantum annealing and simulated annealing exhibit comparable performance on their instances, implying that any quantum advantage is not observed under current hardware and problem settings.
    [codes: C-024, C-025, C-037, C-035]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors propose that quantum advantage for these QUBO-based feature-selection methods is more likely to emerge on problem instances whose energy landscapes have tall, narrow barriers, provided that coherence in quantum annealers improves substantially. They thus suggest future work on both hardware development and targeting harder optimization landscapes where quantum tunneling could yield performance beyond classical techniques.
    [codes: C-036]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims to introduce three new QUBO formulations for feature selection that improve efficiency and flexibility of downstream ML models: a parametric mutual-information-based QUBO grounded in the mRMR framework, a hybrid MI-Pearson QUBO that combines linear and nonlinear dependence measures with lower computational complexity than traditional MIQUBO, and a parametric distance-correlation-based QUBO designed to efficiently handle continuous variables. They also emphasize that parametric control (via alpha) in these formulations allows practitioners to adjust the trade-off between relevance, redundancy, and cardinality without explicitly specifying the number of selected features, especially affecting performance when only a few features can be chosen.
    [codes: C-002, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-018, C-019, C-033]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the authors construct QUBO matrices from various dependence measures (mutual information, Pearson, Spearman, distance correlation) and then evaluate the selected feature subsets by training ML models and reporting F1 and Gini scores on held-out test sets. They explicitly include classical feature selection baselines such as Random Forest importance and MI+Spearman, and describe preprocessing steps that remove non-numeric, zero-variance, and all-NaN features before QUBO construction. The paper also discusses computational complexity and notes that the newly proposed parametric-MI and MI-Pearson QUBOs have lower complexity than MIQUBO, making them more practical for large datasets, and it provides some analysis of how the parametric scaling influences performance as a function of the number of selected features.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-020, C-022, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-018, C-019, C-033]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper offers additional interpretive insights into why certain formulations behave differently: for example, it suggests that MIQUBO underperforms on the small fraud dataset because it emphasizes higher-order interactions at the expense of linear relationships, and notes that parameterization effects are more pronounced when constrained to small feature sets; it also highlights that similar performance between quantum and simulated annealing likely stems from analogous local-minima escape mechanisms. These points contextualize when specific QUBO formulations and solvers may or may not be advantageous in practice.
    [codes: C-029, C-035, C-037, C-038]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it studies feature selection for credit default detection, a core task in credit scoring and loan underwriting, and evaluates whether quantum annealing can improve model performance under high-dimensional feature constraints. Its most useful takeaways for lending are the QUBO formulations that balance relevance, redundancy, and feature-count limits, plus the mixed evidence on whether they outperform classical methods in default-prediction settings.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: QUBO feature selection is directly applied to credit default detection, making the setup relevant to credit scoring and underwriting workflows.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The parametric-MI formulation is especially relevant for lending because it lets practitioners tune the relevance/redundancy trade-off and effectively control how many borrower features are selected.
    [codes: C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019  dim_source: dimension_7]
  KEY_POINT 3: The paper’s best lending-relevant results are on default prediction, where the top QUBO methods show similar performance on smaller feature subsets and only slight gains over the full-feature baseline.
    [codes: C-031, C-032, C-003  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: The comparison against classical feature selection is important for lending because the study shows mixed outcomes: some classical methods beat QUBO in certain feature-count regimes, and simulated annealing can match quantum annealing.
    [codes: C-012, C-028, C-029, C-035  dim_source: dimension_4]
  KEY_POINT 5: The lower-complexity MI-Pearson and parametric-MI QUBOs may be more practical for lending datasets with many borrower variables, especially when preprocessing removes unusable features first.
    [codes: C-020, C-021, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-033  dim_source: dimension_8]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-001, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-012, C-028, C-029, C-035

=== PAPER 0a824fa99e38 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit risk analysis, focusing on estimating risk metrics such as Value at Risk (VaR) and the loss distribution for portfolios of multiple counterparties, a complex problem the authors argue could notably benefit from quantum computation. They frame the task as estimating the cumulative distribution function (CDF) of total portfolio loss and then defining VaR via the usual CDF-based threshold formulation for a given confidence level. The current classical state of the art is described as Monte Carlo simulation for these risk metrics, which quantum amplitude estimation could in principle accelerate.
    [codes: C-001, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-015]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The approach is based on Quantum Amplitude Estimation (QAE) / Iterative QAE for VaR and CDF estimation, with a redesigned state-preparation operator built using Quantum Singular Value Transformation (QSVT) and amplitude loading. The original state-preparation operator A, composed of sub-operators U, S, and C, is replaced by a QSVT-based scheme that encodes total loss in the amplitudes of a target qubit via additive rotations, then applies a polynomial-approximation-based threshold filter compatible with QSVT constraints (including rescaling by a factor k). The method also includes a linear choice of the angle mapping θ to allow reuse of the same polynomial across different loss thresholds and an end-to-end quantum circuit integrated into a hybrid workflow for VaR search.
    [codes: C-003, C-009, C-010, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-016, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-027, C-030, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors state that their QSVT-based state preparation achieves circuit depth scaling as O(d n), i.e., linear in the number of counterparties for fixed polynomial degree d, in contrast to earlier exponential-growth constructions. They analytically show that with suitable angle and threshold polynomial choices, the probability of measuring a particular subspace equals the CDF evaluated at the target loss, up to a constant k² scaling factor, and that their rescaling only increases the number of QAE samples by a constant factor. Simulation studies on an anonymized real-data-derived instance validate that the end-to-end algorithm, from CDF estimation through bisection-based VaR search, converges towards the expected VaR values and that CDF estimates closely track classical benchmarks.
    [codes: C-024, C-025, C-026, C-028, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-036, C-038]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Classically, Monte Carlo simulation is identified as the state-of-the-art method for estimating credit risk metrics such as VaR and CVaR, serving as the main conceptual baseline. The paper emphasizes that QAE promises a quadratic speed-up over these classical Monte Carlo approaches for estimating such risk metrics, though this advantage remains theoretical in their work. In the experiments, the estimated CDF and the VaR obtained by the quantum algorithm are compared against classically reconstructed loss distributions from the same anonymized dataset, providing a benchmark for correctness rather than runtime.
    [codes: C-001, C-007, C-008, C-032, C-033, C-036, C-038]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that a major challenge for quantum risk analysis is the need for costly and restrictive arithmetic within quantum circuits, which motivated their QSVT-based redesign. They explicitly note that a prior multivariate extension that avoided explicit arithmetic led to exponential growth in gate counts with the number of counterparties and that their own improvement depends on a polynomial degree d whose dependence on precision and problem size remains unproven. They also point out that direct polynomial approximations of the threshold function can exceed the |1| bound required by QSVT and hence must be rescaled, which introduces an extra k² factor in the amplitude and correspondingly increases the required number of QAE samples by a constant multiplicative factor. Finally, they state that proving the independence (or detailed scaling) of the polynomial degree d and completing a full T-depth analysis for assessing benefits in the fault-tolerant regime are outstanding issues left unresolved in the present work.
    [codes: C-002, C-008, C-011, C-017, C-022, C-029, C-031, C-041]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions highlighted by the authors include further improving and analyzing the resource scaling of the state-preparation circuit, particularly the behavior of the polynomial degree d as a function of precision and the number of counterparties. They also propose conducting a thorough T-depth analysis to evaluate the effective advantages of their amplitude-loading scheme in a fault-tolerant setting. Beyond these technical aspects, they suggest extending the methodology to more sophisticated uncertainty models relevant to risk management beyond the specific credit risk model studied here.
    [codes: C-029, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims as its main contribution a novel end-to-end implementation of a quantum algorithm for VaR estimation in credit risk that uses amplitude loading and QSVT to avoid costly arithmetic while achieving improved, nominally linear scaling in the number of counterparties. This extends prior multivariate quantum credit risk models that either relied on expensive arithmetic or incurred exponential gate growth, by introducing a QSVT-based state-preparation operator and threshold-polynomial filtering scheme that maintain the theoretical quadratic speed-up potential of QAE while aiming to reduce circuit depth. The work is presented as among the first to provide an end-to-end code implementation and simulation study for QSVT-enhanced quantum credit risk analysis.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-028, C-032]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper implements an end-to-end simulation workflow, from encoding anonymized and perturbed real-data-derived credit risk parameters through CDF estimation to VaR computation, and makes the corresponding code publicly available. The authors describe their use of the QSPPACK library to synthesize the threshold-polynomial approximations and phase sequences needed for QSVT, and they report specific parameter choices such as a relatively high polynomial degree (d = 1000) in their experiments. The experimental evaluation is conducted entirely in simulation, benchmarking quantum-estimated CDF values and VaR against classical reconstructions from the same underlying dataset, which supports correctness but does not address hardware noise or real-time performance.
    [codes: C-004, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-038]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper notes that QSVT has been broadly studied as a way to enhance the efficiency of algorithms involving costly arithmetic and reiterates the general motivation that quantum computation could offer advantages for complex financial problems such as credit risk analysis. It also emphasizes that QAE offers a theoretical quadratic speed-up for estimating risk metrics like VaR and CVaR, even though practical realization of this speed-up is hindered by implementation overheads.
    [codes: C-005, C-008, C-018]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it targets credit risk metrics directly, especially VaR/CDF estimation over portfolios of counterparties, which is closely aligned with default-risk and loss estimation tasks used in lending decisions. Its main value for this silo is the proposed QSVT-based state preparation that aims to make quantum credit-risk workflows more scalable for multi-borrower portfolios, though the evidence is still simulation-based.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Directly models portfolio loss and VaR for credit risk estimation
    [codes: C-006, C-015, C-001  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: Uses QSVT and amplitude loading to reduce circuit cost for multi-counterparty credit risk
    [codes: C-003, C-012, C-013, C-019, C-020  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: Claims linear scaling in the number of counterparties, which is important for larger lending portfolios
    [codes: C-028, C-011, C-017  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: Benchmarks against Monte Carlo, the classical baseline for credit-risk estimation
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-032, C-036, C-038  dim_source: dimension_4]
  KEY_POINT 5: Highlights implementation limits and future work that matter for deployability in lending settings
    [codes: C-002, C-022, C-031, C-029, C-041  dim_source: dimension_5]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-001, C-003, C-007, C-008, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-015, C-019, C-020, C-022, C-028, C-029, C-031, C-032, C-036, C-038, C-041

=== PAPER 0c49909a73ca (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets few-shot credit risk assessment in a setting where data scarcity and class imbalance undermine conventional models, focusing on a 279-sample, imbalanced retail/wholesale borrower subset from a larger loan dataset to study default prediction under constrained data conditions.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-028, C-029, C-030]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors propose a hybrid quantum-classical neural network in which an ensemble of classical models generates a three-dimensional feature representation that is angle-encoded into a quantum neural network implemented as a parametrized quantum circuit trained via the parameter-shift rule; the PQC serves as the core trainable unit, with simulations using RX/RY-based layers and hardware runs using only RY rotations with reduced depth to suit the NISQ device.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-005, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-031, C-032, C-035, C-037, C-038]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: In numerical simulations on the 279-sample dataset, the QNN achieved an average test AUC around 0.85 with stable performance across repeated stratified splits and training loss converging after roughly 20 epochs while AUC peaked by epoch 6. On Quafu hardware, a simplified circuit achieved an AUC of 0.88, with the authors noting strong recall and interpreting the consistent cross-validation performance as evidence of robustness.
    [codes: C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-039, C-040, C-042, C-043, C-044]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study reports that the hybrid QNN surpasses a suite of classical benchmarks such as logistic regression, random forest, XGBoost, SVM, decision trees, and neural networks on the few-shot credit dataset, with particular gains highlighted on recall while classical models are also used as part of the feature-engineering ensemble feeding the quantum classifier.
    [codes: C-004, C-010, C-011, C-031, C-032]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that on such a small dataset the QNN shows rapid training AUC saturation and strong risk of overfitting, requiring early stopping/model selection, and they explicitly caution that the current evidence is limited to a single, narrow dataset and needs broader validation before claiming generalizability. They also imply that near-term advantage may not manifest as end-to-end speedup and that performance remains constrained by NISQ-era noise and hardware capabilities.
    [codes: C-002, C-030, C-040, C-041, C-047, C-048, C-027]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include validating the proposed hybrid QNN approach on more diverse and realistic financial datasets to establish broader generalizability, and pursuing hardware–software co-design where improvements in quantum hardware (e.g., reduced noise) are coupled with further theoretical investigation of hybrid quantum-classical models.
    [codes: C-047, C-048]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims to contribute a novel hybrid quantum-classical workflow for few-shot credit risk assessment that uses classical ensemble models for feature engineering and a QNN as the core classifier, and it positions this as a pragmatic NISQ-era blueprint for applying quantum computing to data-constrained financial scenarios while emphasizing a nuanced view of quantum advantage focused on model expressivity rather than end-to-end speedups.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-012, C-025, C-027, C-046]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses an open-source anonymized loan dataset, selecting a 279-sample, imbalanced retail/wholesale subset and compressing eight original features to three ensemble-derived features before quantum encoding. Evaluation relies on repeated random stratified 7:3 train–test splits with multiple metrics (AUC, KS, recall, precision), and both noiseless simulations and real-hardware runs are conducted via the PyQuafu–PyTorch stack with hardware-aware circuit simplifications (reduced depth, restricted gate set). The authors also discuss convergence behavior, possible regularization from hardware noise, and the observation that simpler, hardware-aware models can be highly expressive, but they do not report code release, limiting direct reproducibility.
    [codes: C-004, C-006, C-007, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper includes general educational explanations of QNN components—such as the role of variational circuits, the trade-off between qubit efficiency and circuit complexity in state preparation, and basic descriptions of quantum measurement and wave-function collapse—which support reader understanding but are not central to the empirical credit-risk study.
    [codes: C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-023]

=== PAPER 0cfd33edfd1a (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses credit-scoring for consumer loans, specifically predicting 12‑month default risk using transactional banking data, in a context where smaller financial institutions must operate with scarce and skewed datasets and imbalanced default rates. The authors frame this as a binary classification problem on a proprietary loan dataset (4,763 loans, 3 months of transactions, >350 engineered features) for Fintonic/Wanna, with the goal of improving performance under limited-data conditions that challenge classical models. They hypothesize that quantum kernel methods and quantum feature spaces may be particularly suitable for such data‑scarce, noisy, imbalanced scenarios and could provide marginal gains valued as “last‑mile” innovation in financial services.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-007, C-017, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-050]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors propose Systemic Quantum Score (SQS), a quantum-kernel-based classifier that uses an evolutionary algorithm to search over families of quantum feature maps built from n‑qubit Pauli-word operators applied after Hadamard initialization, with free parameters optimized at the end of each generation. Kernel target alignment (via a normalized eigenvalue criterion) is used as the fitness signal to select feature maps that best separate classes, and the workflow embeds a heavily preprocessed feature space—10 mutual-information-selected features further reduced by LDA and standardization—into quantum circuits of up to 10 qubits. The design is informed by prior work on QSVC as a quantum-enhanced classifier, and the evolutionary search explores different initial population sizes and circuit structures, while prioritizing lower-qubit mappings for tractability.
    [codes: C-009, C-012, C-015, C-016, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-036]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The paper reports that SQS shows an increased capacity to extract patterns from fewer data points and, in the lowest-sample regimes, outperforms all compared models, including data-hungry algorithms like XGBoost, in terms of AUC. In downsampling experiments, SQS and other quantum kernel models appear promising under low data availability, with SQS achieving the highest AUC when the training set is reduced to 10% of the original size, and some less intuitive, evolutionarily discovered feature maps performing particularly well. However, the authors also note that SQS performance decays as dataset size approaches the full sample, converging towards classical baselines, and that increasing qubit count does not yield significant improvements in kernel scaling, with diversity in the initial population contributing more to fitness gains.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-005, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-047, C-048]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: SQS is empirically compared against classical Support Vector Classifiers and XGBoost, with XGBoost chosen as the production baseline due to its strong performance in the existing risk model pipeline. The authors report that while XGBoost improves with more data, simpler models like SVC and QSVC show better generalization than XGBoost when training data are scarce, and SQS outperforms all three models in the lowest-sample regime and in a 10%‑training generalization test (in terms of AUC). They also characterize XGBoost as a data-hungry algorithm that is less competitive than quantum-kernel-based approaches under extreme data scarcity, although SQS’ advantage diminishes when the full dataset is used.
    [codes: C-005, C-023, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-045, C-047, C-048]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly acknowledge that the advantage of quantum-enhanced models in the NISQ regime is not yet fully understood and that any observed quantum advantage should be regarded as opportunistic rather than a general solution for industrial use. They stress that the entire workflow is executed on simulators due to current device latency, with quantum feature maps limited to 10 qubits for tractability, and note that many-body gate constructions like XXY rotations would significantly increase circuit depth after transpilation, posing hardware challenges. They also observe inefficiencies in some evolved circuits (e.g., redundant Hadamard gate sequences), and admit that the apparent sweet spot for scarce, imbalanced, and noisy data requires further validation on additional production-grade datasets before drawing confident conclusions.
    [codes: C-010, C-028, C-031, C-037, C-038, C-049, C-050, C-051]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The paper calls for further analysis of quantum-kernel-based models on more production-grade datasets to confirm whether the observed benefits in scarce, imbalanced, noisy settings hold more generally. The authors also propose extending this line of research to other application areas where similar data challenges are common, explicitly mentioning healthcare and additional finance-sector use cases as promising domains.
    [codes: C-051, C-052]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors claim novelty in introducing the Systemic Quantum Score (SQS), a quantum-kernel approach that leverages evolutionary algorithms to design feature maps aiming to surpass traditional classical models in real-world financial credit scoring. They further highlight that SQS provides preliminary empirical evidence of potential advantage over purely classical models in a production-grade finance use case, particularly in the low-data regime where quantum feature spaces are projected to capture better links between features and target classes.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-012, C-013]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a proprietary Fintonic/Wanna risk dataset comprising 4,763 loans with three months of transactional history and over 350 engineered variables per customer–loan pair, fed into a production-like pipeline where a classifier is triggered after bank-account aggregation and transaction categorization. The authors perform extensive classical preprocessing, including feature engineering, mutual-information-based selection down to 10 features, and LDA plus standardization to fit quantum circuits of up to 10 qubits, and evaluate models via downsampled datasets and a 10%‑training generalization test to probe performance under varying data scarcity. The experimental setup for the evolutionary search is described with varying initial population sizes and generations, and they note practical insights such as diverse initial populations improving kernel fitness and some non-intuitive evolved feature maps performing well, while also disclosing that all experiments are simulator-based and that increased qubit counts do not yield clear scaling benefits.
    [codes: C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-039, C-046, C-047]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper situates its work in an industry context where large financial institutions pursue marginal ‘last‑mile’ gains in model performance while smaller neobanks and fintechs must compete with limited data, and argues that quantum computing is attracting interest for specific near-term financial applications. It also emphasizes that quantum kernels should employ feature maps that are hard to replicate classically to be meaningful, and offers the broader observation that quantum-kernel-based models seem particularly promising in low-data regimes with scarce, imbalanced, and noisy data.
    [codes: C-006, C-008, C-011, C-014, C-044, C-050]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is highly relevant to credit_lending because it studies default prediction for consumer loans using transactional banking data and explicitly tests whether quantum kernels help when labeled loan data are scarce, skewed, and noisy. Its main value for this silo is the comparison against standard credit-risk baselines and the evidence that the proposed method can improve AUC in the lowest-data regimes used in loan default modeling.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Directly targets loan default prediction from banking transactions, matching the core credit_lending task.
    [codes: C-020, C-021, C-022, C-018, C-019  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The method is designed for scarce, skewed data, which is especially relevant when lenders have limited historical defaults or thin-file borrowers.
    [codes: C-001, C-007, C-011, C-050  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 3: SQS is compared against XGBoost and SVC, with XGBoost as the production baseline, making the benchmark set directly useful for credit-risk model selection.
    [codes: C-023, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-045, C-048  dim_source: dimension_4]
  KEY_POINT 4: The strongest gains appear in the lowest-sample regime and in a 10% training test, which is the most relevant setting for thin-data lending segments.
    [codes: C-039, C-042, C-046, C-047  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 5: The paper’s limitations are important for deployment in lending: the workflow is simulator-only, capped at 10 qubits, and the authors say the advantage may be opportunistic rather than general.
    [codes: C-028, C-031, C-037, C-049, C-051  dim_source: dimension_5]

=== PAPER 10be86fe1795 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper studies a binary credit default prediction task, asking whether integrating a variational quantum circuit into a neural network can yield a quantum advantage in accuracy or time for financial classification. The authors motivate this by arguing that finance is an early candidate for quantum benefit and that classical methods struggle with large-scale, complex, non-linear financial modeling, including overfitting on non-convex problems such as portfolio optimization and option pricing.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-007, C-008, C-019]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The approach is a hybrid quantum–classical neural network (QCNN) where classical layers handle non-linear transformations and quantum layers perform complex linear transformations using NISQ devices and variational quantum algorithms. The quantum part is a continuous-variable variational circuit composed of interferometer, squeezer, interferometer, displacement, and Kerr gates, repeated in five blocks, with classical data embedded via amplitude embedding and parameters optimized with Adam.
    [codes: C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-044]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the credit default dataset, the hybrid QCNN achieved substantially higher predictive performance than the classical baseline: at 30 epochs, training/validation accuracies of 96.34%/95.18% with lower losses (0.0354/0.0487) versus the classical model’s 91.22%/91.11% and 0.2627/0.3128. Extending QCNN training to 40 epochs further improved accuracy to 97.63% (training) and 96.21% (validation). The authors also note that LDA-based feature reduction retained about 97.67% of variability. However, the quantum model showed higher variability in accuracy and loss, with its loss stabilizing only after around the 15th epoch.
    [codes: C-002, C-029, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-040, C-042]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The classical baseline is a feedforward neural network with one hidden layer of 10 nodes using ReLU and sigmoid activations, binary cross-entropy loss, Adam optimizer, and an exponentially decaying learning rate. Compared on the same task and (for the primary comparison) the same 30-epoch budget, the QCNN returns markedly higher accuracy (about 97–96% vs ~91%) and lower loss, but each QCNN training epoch is approximately 10 times slower (70 seconds vs 7 seconds) and exhibits greater training variability, with the classical loss remaining in a narrow 0.2–0.4 band. The authors interpret this as a quantum advantage in classification performance but not in runtime.
    [codes: C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-002, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly acknowledge that the QCNN is significantly slower per epoch than the classical neural network, requiring about ten times more runtime, and that its training dynamics show higher variability in both accuracy and loss, with loss only stabilizing after mid-training. They also concede that further work is needed to enhance results on contemporary quantum hardware, implying that current hardware constraints limit direct deployment of their simulated approach.
    [codes: C-039, C-040, C-042, C-048]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include exploring more advanced feature maps or ansätze for the variational quantum circuits, conducting more thorough hyperparameter optimization (especially learning rate and batch size), and designing larger and more intricate hybrid neural network architectures to handle more demanding classification problems and improve performance on actual quantum hardware.
    [codes: C-045, C-046, C-047, C-048]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions itself as a proof-of-concept study showing that integrating variational quantum circuits into classical neural networks can provide a quantum advantage in financial classification tasks, specifically credit default prediction. It aims to bridge the gap in the literature on whether such quantum integration genuinely improves processing of financial tasks and argues that the hybrid design effectively allocates linear transformations to quantum layers and non-linear transformations to classical layers.
    [codes: C-001, C-012, C-016, C-043, C-044]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a 2023 public credit default dataset with 20 predictors and a binary target indicating next-month default, removing null columns and client IDs before modeling. It addresses a severe initial class imbalance (10.32:1 normal-to-default) via undersampling to a 3:1 ratio and applies StandardScaler standardization. For the hybrid model, it reduces dimensionality with LDA from 20 to 10 features while retaining about 97.67% variability, then feeds these into a two-hidden-layer classical network before amplitude encoding into the quantum circuit. Both classical and hybrid models use binary cross-entropy and Adam, with a dynamic learning rate for the classical baseline. These details support reproducibility but the paper does not report multiple independent runs or statistical significance testing for its performance gains.
    [codes: C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The introduction and related work sections emphasize broader claims that quantum machine learning can offer exponential speed-ups for certain learning tasks and more efficient vector operations (O(log N) vs O(N)), and they cite prior studies where hybrid or continuous-variable quantum architectures slightly improved ROC or accuracy and cost over classical baselines in financial or related domains. These contextual claims motivate the present work but are not directly tested within this study.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-013, C-014, C-015]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to credit_lending because it evaluates a hybrid quantum-classical model on credit default prediction, a core lending risk task. Its main value for the silo is the reported accuracy gain over a classical baseline, alongside clear evidence about preprocessing, class imbalance handling, and runtime tradeoffs that matter for deployable credit scoring systems.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: The study targets credit default prediction and explicitly asks whether a hybrid quantum model can improve lending-risk classification.
    [codes: C-001, C-016, C-043  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The hybrid QCNN outperformed the classical baseline on the default dataset, with higher accuracy and lower loss at 30 epochs and further gains at 40 epochs.
    [codes: C-002, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 3: The dataset and preprocessing choices are highly relevant to lending data, especially the severe class imbalance and the use of undersampling plus standardization.
    [codes: C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 4: The hybrid model uses LDA feature reduction before the quantum circuit, retaining most variability while reducing 20 features to 10.
    [codes: C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 5: The main deployment caveat is runtime and stability: the quantum model was about 10x slower per epoch and showed higher variability than the classical network.
    [codes: C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-048  dim_source: dimension_4]

=== PAPER 1993ad0237f3 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames finance as fundamentally concerned with uncertainty, volatility and the trade‑off between risk and return, highlighting portfolio optimization, option pricing and risk estimation as core problems often cast as optimization or Monte Carlo estimation tasks. It notes that many financial tasks, including portfolio construction and dynamic portfolio selection, can be expressed as optimization over portfolios for a given risk/return profile, while derivatives and risk measures like VaR/CVaR often rely on computationally costly Monte Carlo simulation. The authors also emphasize that machine‑learning style pattern discovery in past data underpins applications such as credit scoring and fraud detection, but stress that widely accepted models may fail in qualitatively new market conditions.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-012, C-037, C-040, C-049]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors survey both gate‑model and quantum‑annealing hardware families and describe how finance problems are mapped into quantum form, e.g., encoding optimization as QUBO Hamiltonians for adiabatic optimization/quantum annealing and using amplitude encoding plus reversible quantum circuits for Monte Carlo and machine‑learning tasks. They outline generic quantum algorithm structure relying on superposition, entanglement and reversibility, and discuss specific subroutines such as Grover‑based search, quantum Fourier transform, HHL‑style linear‑system solvers, quantum PCA, and amplitude amplification/estimation that underlie quantum Monte Carlo, regression and classification methods. Quantum classification and SVM approaches focus on efficient projection operations and amplitude‑encoded inner products, while adiabatic/annealing methods attempt to find ground states corresponding to combinatorial minima under adiabatic conditions, albeit only approximately realized in current annealers.
    [codes: C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-033, C-035, C-038, C-042, C-050, C-051]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The review reports several conceptual and proof‑of‑principle results: summarized speedups for various quantum machine‑learning subroutines, including Grover‑based O(√N) search and exponential scaling claims for quantum SVMs and quantum PCA, and quadratic speedups for amplitude amplification and quantum amplitude estimation leading to quantum Monte Carlo with quadratically reduced sample complexity. On the empirical side, the authors describe D‑Wave annealing experiments for optimal trading trajectory and arbitrage detection where the annealer matched classical optimal solutions on small instances, a Boltzmann machine trained on a present‑day D‑Wave device, and quantum risk‑analysis experiments on IBM hardware that estimated VaR and CVaR with excellent accuracy and theoretically quadratic speedup. They also note that neural networks have already proved highly successful in financial prediction classically and that small IBM experiments could already show quantum speedups in limited settings.
    [codes: C-017, C-018, C-031, C-032, C-034, C-039, C-043, C-045, C-047, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-055, C-056]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Comparisons to classical methods are mostly at the level of asymptotic complexity and small‑scale benchmarks. For optimization on annealers, the trading‑trajectory experiments on 512‑ and 1152‑qubit D‑Wave chips showed performance similar to classical hardware, and in the arbitrage example the annealer recovered the same optimal solutions as an exhaustive classical solver on a five‑asset graph. For sampling‑based methods, the authors argue that amplitude amplification and quantum amplitude estimation provide quadratic improvements over classical Monte Carlo in terms of required samples for a given accuracy, which translates into faster pricing and risk‑metric estimation, though the review does not report detailed runtime benchmarks. For machine learning, they highlight theoretical exponential or polynomial‑in‑log‑N scaling advantages for quantum SVMs and quantum PCA relative to classical counterparts, while noting that classical neural networks and related models are already highly successful in finance.
    [codes: C-032, C-034, C-039, C-043, C-045, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-055]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper is explicit about multiple limitations. On the modelling side, it cautions that even widely accepted financial models may make erroneous predictions in qualitatively new situations, and that least‑squares regression can be computationally expensive in classical settings. On the quantum side, decoherence is said to destroy quantum behavior and any potential advantage, while fault‑tolerant error correction demands thousands of physical qubits per logical qubit and practical qRAM capable of efficient data loading does not yet exist. The authors stress that quantum annealing is only an approximate realization of adiabatic computing, and that quantum hidden Markov models and related ideas for quantum AI are still in their infancy with their power not yet well understood. They further emphasize that numerous experimental breakthroughs are still required before universal quantum processors can surpass present‑day supercomputers, making most of the claimed speedups contingent and largely theoretical at this stage.
    [codes: C-005, C-024, C-025, C-027, C-030, C-041, C-048, C-057]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors suggest that near‑term efforts should concentrate on algorithms for Noisy Intermediate‑Scale Quantum (NISQ) devices, exploring how faulty quantum computers might still deliver useful financial applications before full fault tolerance is available. They highlight quantum‑accelerated Monte Carlo for derivative pricing and the use of quantum amplitude estimation for broader risk‑metric computation as promising directions, as well as deploying quantum annealers for optimal feature selection in credit analysis and potentially speeding up neural‑network training. They also remark that many financial applications remain unexplored in the paper, explicitly mentioning blockchain and cryptocurrencies as areas where quantum technologies could become relevant in future work.
    [codes: C-026, C-036, C-044, C-046, C-054, C-058, C-059]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions itself primarily as an overview of quantum computing prospects for finance, rather than as a source of new algorithms, and emphasizes the breadth of potential quantum speedups across optimization, machine learning and Monte Carlo‑based risk and pricing tasks. It underscores the central role of certain subroutines—such as HHL‑style linear solvers, quantum PCA, and amplitude estimation—that have caused excitement due to their wide applicability in financial regression, dimensionality reduction, and risk estimation. The review also highlights that combining classical sampling improvements with quantum amplitude‑based methods may allow concatenated speedups, and argues that even imperfect, NISQ‑era quantum devices could already support meaningful proof‑of‑principle financial applications.
    [codes: C-017, C-020, C-043, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-056, C-058]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the work is largely conceptual and survey‑like, summarizing algorithms and earlier experiments rather than presenting detailed new empirical studies with full datasets or reproducible pipelines. The reported D‑Wave and IBM experiments for trading trajectories, arbitrage, credit‑feature selection, Boltzmann machine training, quantum SVM classification, and VaR/CVaR estimation are described at a high level without explicit dataset sizes, full problem instance specifications, or quantitative runtime and error metrics in this paper. Many of the speedup claims are asymptotic and depend on subroutines like qRAM that are not yet available in practice, and several cited approaches (such as quantum hidden Markov models) are acknowledged to be at an early stage where their true capabilities remain uncertain.
    [codes: C-031, C-032, C-034, C-036, C-041, C-047, C-048, C-051, C-052, C-055, C-056, C-057]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Some codes provide general conceptual background rather than mapping cleanly onto the other dimensions, such as the definition of volatility as variation in a trading price series, the identification of two main hardware families with gate‑model processors being closest to classical computers, and the observation that quantum and classical sampling advantages can concatenate when combined. These contextual points support the broader narrative about how quantum computing concepts intersect with financial modeling and hardware choices but are not themselves central to the problem framing, methods, results, or limitations.
    [codes: C-007, C-021, C-022, C-049, C-053]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it explicitly discusses credit scoring, feature selection for credit analysis, and classification/regression methods that map directly to default prediction and loan decision workflows. Its main value for this silo is as a survey of quantum approaches that could, in principle, speed up credit-risk modeling, while also emphasizing that current results are mostly proof-of-principle and hardware-limited.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Quantum annealing is discussed as a route to optimal feature selection in credit analysis.
    [codes: C-035, C-036, C-023, C-028  dim_source: dimension_6]
  KEY_POINT 2: Classification methods are tied to fraud detection and credit scoring, with quantum SVMs proposed as a faster classifier.
    [codes: C-037, C-038, C-039  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: Regression is presented as a core forecasting tool, but classical least-squares fitting is expensive, motivating quantum regression ideas.
    [codes: C-040, C-041, C-042  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 4: The paper highlights that neural networks are already successful in analyzing credit risk, and that quantum annealers may speed their training.
    [codes: C-045, C-046, C-047  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 5: The paper warns that models can fail in new conditions and that quantum methods remain limited by decoherence, qRAM, and immature hardware.
    [codes: C-005, C-024, C-025, C-027, C-057, C-058  dim_source: dimension_5]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-005, C-024, C-025, C-027, C-057, C-058

=== PAPER 1a0d37057236 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses multiclass credit risk classification, proposing an interpretable quantum neural network framework (IQNN-CS) for credit scoring on structured financial data, with an emphasis on regulatory demands for transparency rather than quantum supremacy or raw performance gains. It specifically targets the interpretability gap in structured, multiclass QML tasks such as credit scoring, where existing QML work has mostly treated the problem as binary and performance-focused, overlooking transparency requirements.
    [codes: C-001, C-007, C-010, C-011, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-019]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors propose a five-stage hybrid classical–quantum pipeline in which classical preprocessing (including standardization, PCA-based dimensionality reduction, and a Linear→ReLU block) prepares structured financial inputs that are then angle-encoded onto a fixed number of qubits for processing by a multi-layer entangling variational quantum circuit. The model is trained end-to-end via PennyLane’s TorchLayer using negative log-likelihood loss and AdamW with learning-rate scheduling, quantum gradients computed by the parameter-shift rule, and early stopping based on validation loss to control overfitting. The quantum component relies on StronglyEntanglingLayers-style variational circuits and Pauli-Z expectation values as quantum features, with subsequent classical post-processing to produce class logits.
    [codes: C-012, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Empirically, IQNN-CS is reported to show stable training dynamics and competitive predictive performance, achieving perfect 100% accuracy and F1 on Dataset 1, while attaining 77.3% accuracy with heterogeneous class-wise precision/recall on Dataset 2. Analysis reveals that for Dataset 2 the model has high recall but low precision for the Low-risk class and strong precision but lower recall for the High-risk class, reflecting class-specific misclassification patterns and highlighting the dataset’s complexity and imbalance. Interpretability analyses show focused, coherent attributions, well-separated quantum manifolds, sharp confidence drops under occlusion, and low inter-class attribution similarity on Dataset 1, contrasted with diffuse and noisy attributions, entangled latent clusters, gradual degradation under occlusion, significant ICAA overlap, mixed-class influential examples, broader predictive uncertainty, and generally degraded interpretability on Dataset 2; together these results support claims that IQNN-CS can reveal how the model distinguishes risk categories and that metrics like ICAA provide deeper insight into class-specific reasoning and dataset dependence of QNN success.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-014, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055, C-057, C-059, C-061, C-063, C-064, C-065, C-066, C-067, C-069]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors explicitly state that their goal is not to demonstrate quantum supremacy or outperform classical machine learning models, and no direct empirical comparison to classical baselines is reported. Instead, prior QML credit scoring work is critiqued primarily for focusing on binary formulation and performance rather than interpretability, positioning IQNN-CS as complementary and interpretability-first rather than as a performance challenger to classical models.
    [codes: C-011, C-015, C-016, C-017]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: Several limitations are acknowledged. Conceptually, the black-box nature of QNNs and the lack of standardized tools for reasoning about QNN decisions, particularly for structured multiclass tasks, are identified as current field-level constraints, compounded by the fact that most classical explainability methods do not directly extend to quantum models. Methodologically, prior QML work on credit scoring is noted to have overlooked interpretability by focusing on binary classification or optimization formulations. Within the proposed framework, early stopping is employed specifically to combat overfitting, hinting at sensitivity to data size and complexity, and Dataset 2’s complex, overlapping feature space is recognized as challenging for the model to disentangle. On the interpretability side, gradient-based explanations—especially SmoothGrad—are found to be inconsistent and noise-sensitive, with the authors cautioning that these methods should be applied carefully in quantum settings; they observe that model internal logic can break down despite apparently reliable output probabilities, and that certain instances (e.g., Sample 12 in Dataset 2) show high attribution variance, indicating unreliable class evidence and degraded utility of most interpretability methods on harder data. Overall, the authors conclude that robust interpretability is limited not only by explanation method design but also by the stability and semantic quality of the latent representations learned by the QNN.
    [codes: C-009, C-010, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-033, C-045, C-055, C-056, C-057, C-062, C-068, C-069, C-070]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors suggest that interpretability-first frameworks like IQNN-CS will be essential for deploying reliable AI in sensitive domains such as finance and healthcare, implying future work on extending and hardening such interpretable QML approaches for real-world deployment under regulatory constraints.
    [codes: C-060]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims novelty in proposing IQNN-CS, an interpretable quantum neural network framework tailored to multiclass credit risk classification on structured financial data, and in introducing Inter-Class Attribution Alignment (ICAA), a new metric for quantifying attribution divergence across predicted classes. It further contributes a hybrid classical–quantum pipeline combining a variational QNN with a suite of post-hoc interpretability techniques (gradient-based attributions, prototype matching, occlusion/perturbation analyses, and ICAA) applied specifically to structured multiclass credit scoring, which the authors argue has been largely unexplored in prior QML research.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-019, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-059]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Experimentally, IQNN-CS is implemented as a five-stage pipeline with clear data handling steps: numerical features are standardized, class imbalance is addressed via undersampling (Dataset 1) or SMOTE (Dataset 2), PCA is applied to ensure compatibility with qubit limits, and stratified splits preserve class distributions across train, validation, and test sets. The model is trained in an end-to-end differentiable fashion using PennyLane TorchLayer with NLL loss, AdamW optimizer, parameter-shift gradients for the quantum circuit, learning-rate scheduling, and early stopping to mitigate overfitting, and performance is reported using accuracy and macro F1. Interpretability is assessed via four gradient-based attribution methods, prototype matching in quantum feature space, the ICAA matrix, perturbation-based indecision region analysis, and visualization of quantum latent space with t-SNE, alongside occlusion and attribution stability studies; a method evaluation summary ranks occlusion and ICAA as the most reliable techniques and notes degradation of interpretability quality on the more complex dataset. These choices collectively reflect a relatively rigorous and transparent experimental setup for simulator-based hybrid QML in credit scoring, though no classical baselines are included for comparison.
    [codes: C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-061, C-067, C-069, C-070]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors advance broader conceptual points: they argue that as credit scoring operates in regulated environments, interpretability is not optional but mandatory for deployment, and that interpretability should be viewed as a diagnostic tool to probe model behavior rather than merely as a post-hoc explanation layer. They also stress that the success of QNNs is highly dataset-dependent, hinging on alignment between data structure and the model’s inductive bias, and that transparent and accountable QML models are a practical path forward for financial decision-making, with robust interpretability contingent on stable, semantically meaningful latent representations.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-054, C-058, C-070]

=== PAPER 2849bbcb864e (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit-risk assessment, where models must balance predictive accuracy with interpretability to satisfy regulatory and institutional demands for transparent, auditable reasoning. It formulates default prediction on the Taiwan credit card dataset as a supervised binary classification problem with 23 input attributes and a markedly imbalanced label distribution (~22.1% defaults).
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-015, C-016, C-017]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors propose a single-qudit variational quantum neural network in which both data features and trainable parameters are co-encoded into a unitary generated by the full su(d) Lie algebra, enabling exploration of the entire d-dimensional Hilbert space. The circuit uses a compact Hamiltonian where trainable weights appear as linear coefficients of input features, with data re-uploading across multiple layers to enhance nonlinearity and feature cross-talk, and all data and variational parts condensed into a single unitary operator. Training employs a bounded nonlinear remapping of x_j w_j, cross-entropy loss with L2 (ridge) regularization, and the Adam optimizer; su(d) generators are constructed algorithmically in symmetric, antisymmetric, and diagonal sets and implemented in matrix form.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-028, C-029]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the Taiwan credit card default dataset, the qudit QNN achieves a macro-averaged F1-score of about 0.667 with 384 parameters, consistently outperforming logistic regression and closely matching a classical neural network while reaching random-forest-level macro-F1. Its edit distance in feature ranking relative to LR (~20.9) is statistically indistinguishable from that of RF, despite using far fewer parameters (384 vs. 3927 for RF). Under feature poisoning (selected inputs replaced with noise), the QNN attains the highest classification performance, exhibiting superior robustness and competitive accuracy while maintaining moderate interpretability according to the introduced metrics; the authors argue these findings suggest quantum approaches may uncover complex financial patterns that may elude conventional methods.
    [codes: C-005, C-008, C-011, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-044, C-047]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The QNN is benchmarked against logistic regression, random forest, and a classical neural network. It consistently outperforms LR and reaches or closely matches RF and NN in macro-F1, while using substantially fewer parameters than RF. In feature-ranking interpretability, the QNN’s edit distance from LR’s ranking is similar to RF’s, and poisoning-based metrics (BIS/WIS) indicate RF preserves the strongest alignment with the LR reference, whereas the QNN attains the best poisoned-data F1 but only moderate interpretability. The authors also note that although the classical NN slightly exceeds the QNN’s F1 on clean data, it lacks an intrinsic feature-ranking mechanism, whereas LR and the QNN provide explicit parameter–feature relationships.
    [codes: C-005, C-010, C-018, C-019, C-033, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-044, C-045, C-046]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: A key methodological limitation acknowledged by the authors is that parameter-shift rules connecting partial derivatives to circuit observables are not yet established for their specific single-qudit Hamiltonian encoding, constraining gradient estimation techniques for hardware implementations. In terms of trade-offs, they characterize the QNN as offering only moderate interpretability relative to RF under poisoning tests, and as slightly lagging classical NNs in accuracy on uncorrupted data, indicating the approach does not dominate classical baselines on all criteria.
    [codes: C-027, C-042, C-044, C-045]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors propose extending their architecture to multi-qudit implementations as a direction for future work, presumably to increase expressivity and explore scalability beyond a single qudit setting.
    [codes: C-048]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is framed as a compact, single-qudit QNN that co-encodes data features and trainable parameters via the full su(d) generator set, thereby exploring the entire Hilbert space while preserving a transparent correspondence between learned weights and input feature importance. They emphasize an interpretability mechanism where feature importance is inferred from learned coefficient magnitudes, supported by multilayer expansions in which weights add linearly at first order and cumulative weight size relates to influence. Additionally, they introduce two custom interpretability metrics—edit distance between model and LR feature rankings, and a poisoning-based Weighted Interpretability Score—to quantitatively assess how faithfully a model’s learned weights focus on informative features.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-006, C-007, C-013, C-014, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The study uses the well-known Taiwan credit card default dataset with 30,000 records, 23 attributes, and a 22.1% default rate, providing a realistic imbalanced benchmark. The QNN ansatz is rigorously specified via the full su(d) generator construction, categorized into symmetric, antisymmetric, and diagonal sets and implemented in matrix form, and the training pipeline is clearly described (nonlinear remapping, cross-entropy plus ridge regularization, Adam optimizer). Interpretability is operationalized through two explicit metrics: edit distance to LR’s feature ranking and a poisoning-based Weighted Interpretability Score in which models are expected to downweight corrupted features. The authors also analyze how multilayer weight accumulation affects feature influence, arguing that small cumulative weights indicate negligible effect while large weights signal strong contributions, thereby grounding their feature-ranking procedure in the circuit’s series expansion.
    [codes: C-015, C-016, C-017, C-022, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors argue that finance applications, particularly credit risk, require models that reconcile strong predictive performance with explicit, auditable reasoning, and suggest that quantum approaches may have the potential to uncover complex patterns in financial behavior beyond conventional techniques. They further observe that increasing the number of QNN layers systematically enhances classification performance, indicating a depth–performance relationship in this qudit-based architecture.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-011, C-014, C-047]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to credit_lending because it studies default prediction on a credit card dataset and explicitly targets the accuracy–interpretability trade-off that matters for lending decisions. Its QNN is evaluated against standard credit-scoring baselines and includes feature-ranking and corruption tests that speak to model transparency and robustness in default-risk settings.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
