=== PAPER 288305d31b7d (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit risk analysis (CRA) for portfolios, focusing on estimating Value at Risk (VaR) and Economic Capital (EC) for default losses using a quantum formulation of a portfolio loss model. It starts from an existing quantum CRA algorithm that offers a quadratic speedup over classical Monte Carlo methods and seeks to make it compatible with more realistic multi-factor risk models used in practice, where expected loss is additive across assets and EC is defined as VaR minus expected loss.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-005, C-006, C-016, C-017, C-019, C-021, C-024]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The approach is based on Quantum Amplitude Estimation (QAE) and its iterative variant to estimate loss distribution tail probabilities, by mapping CRA to a quantum operator A acting on n+1 qubits and using bisection on P[L ≤ x] to obtain VaR. The original circuit decomposes A into operators U, S, and C under a Basel II–style Gaussian conditional independence model, and the paper proposes two multi-factor uncertainty encodings (multiple-rotations with one register per factor, and single-rotation with a multivariate normal register) plus a modified comparator using LinearAmplitudeFunction and a piecewise linear function on amplitudes that reads default qubits directly and removes the S (sum) operator to allow flexible LGD inputs.
    [codes: C-009, C-010, C-014, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-028, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-039, C-040]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On a toy example with two assets and two systemic risk factors, using the multiple-rotations scheme and non-integer LGDs, the authors simulate their circuit with iterative QAE and bisection, setting target precision ε = 0.002 and a 99% confidence interval, which required on average about 50,000 quantum samples and 9 qubits on a Qiskit simulator. They report that, for small portfolios, the modified multiple-rotations variant without a sum register offers a qubit advantage, and they emphasize that the use of non-integer LGD values empirically demonstrates increased input flexibility compared to the original integer-only design.
    [codes: C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The work is framed against classical Monte Carlo–based CRA and EC estimation, where convergence is O(√n) and pseudo-random number generation can degrade simulation quality due to duplicates. The authors position QAE as providing a quadratic speedup for VaR estimation over these classical Monte Carlo methods and note that quantum hardware’s ability to generate true random samples addresses limitations of pseudo-random simulation, though the speedup is presented as a theoretical advantage rather than an empirically demonstrated one in this paper.
    [codes: C-001, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several limitations. Classically, Monte Carlo CRA suffers from slow O(√n) convergence and pseudo-random duplication issues that can compromise simulation quality. On the quantum side, enriching the model with multiple risk factors via multiple-rotations significantly increases gate count, while the single-rotation design reduces depth at the cost of forcing all assets to share the same alpha vector, which is a modeling restriction. The original WeightedAdder-based approach restricts LGD inputs to integers and, for realistic LGD magnitudes, requires many qubits in the sum register. Their more flexible LGD encoding using LinearAmplitudeFunction adds one qubit per asset, effectively doubling per-asset qubit growth and causing circuit width to scale as O(2K) instead of O(K); although removing the sum register helps at small K, its overhead becomes negligible at larger scales. The number of risk factors also increases required qubits linearly with n_Z, and overall, the authors conclude that further scalability improvements are needed and that current real quantum hardware noise completely destroys the output, making meaningful results dependent on significant improvements in qubit coherence times.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-012, C-027, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-050, C-051]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work highlighted by the authors includes the need for significantly improved qubit coherence times to obtain meaningful results beyond ideal simulations, and the use of their enhanced algorithm—featuring flexible LGD inputs and a multi-factor uncertainty model aligned with large financial institutions’ practice—to enable realistic benchmarking studies against classical CRA models using real-world data.
    [codes: C-052, C-053, C-054]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims methodological contributions in extending an existing QAE-based CRA algorithm to support a more realistic multi-factor default model that accounts for several systemic risk factors, while preserving the theoretical quadratic speedup, and in increasing input flexibility by removing the constraint that LGD values be integers through a redesigned comparator that avoids the S operator. Although these enhancements increase circuit depth and width, the authors argue they demonstrate a path toward a more realistic quantum CRA software solution that better matches the custom multi-factor models used by large banks.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-004, C-011, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-024, C-026, C-028, C-033, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Experimentally, the paper uses a simulator-based evaluation on IBM’s Qiskit qasm_simulator with a clearly specified toy problem (two assets, two risk factors, non-integer LGDs) and reports concrete resource counts such as 9 qubits and roughly 50,000 quantum samples under stated precision and confidence targets, enhancing transparency. The authors employ iterative QAE with a standard bisection VaR search and explicitly discuss scalability of qubits and gates with respect to asset and risk-factor counts, but the study is limited to a very small synthetic instance without detailed classical benchmark results or large-scale empirical tests.
    [codes: C-016, C-018, C-019, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-047, C-049]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper notes that quantum finance more broadly seeks to exploit quantum computing to outperform classical methods across a wide range of financial problems and reiterates that its more realistic multi-factor uncertainty model aligns with the models commonly used by large entities such as Intesa Sanpaolo, supporting external relevance of the chosen modeling assumptions.
    [codes: C-005, C-013, C-021, C-022]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it targets portfolio credit risk estimation, including VaR and economic capital, which are directly tied to default prediction and loss modeling in lending. Its main value for this silo is the move toward more realistic multi-factor default models and more flexible LGD handling, both of which matter for loan portfolios with heterogeneous borrowers and exposures.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Extends credit risk analysis from a single-factor setup to a multi-factor default model.
    [codes: C-002, C-011, C-013, C-024, C-025  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 2: Introduces a more flexible LGD encoding that removes the integer-only restriction.
    [codes: C-003, C-015, C-031, C-033, C-034, C-035  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: Keeps the core credit-risk outputs focused on VaR and economic capital.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-016, C-017, C-019  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 4: Highlights the main tradeoff for lending use cases: better realism comes with higher quantum resource costs.
    [codes: C-004, C-027, C-029, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-050  dim_source: dimension_5]
  KEY_POINT 5: The paper’s toy experiment demonstrates the method on a small credit portfolio with two assets and two risk factors.
    [codes: C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043  dim_source: dimension_3]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-019, C-024, C-025, C-031, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-037, C-039, C-041, C-042, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-050

=== PAPER 2896abff2596 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit risk assessment for loan default prediction, using a real-world Kaggle credit risk dataset with 24 normalized features and binary default labels, and frames it as a supervised binary classification problem suitable for a hybrid quantum–classical pipeline. The authors emphasize the need for accurate, scalable, and computationally feasible methods under current quantum hardware limitations, positioning Quantum Transfer Learning as a way to improve prediction quality under these constraints.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-010, C-025]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study implements a hybrid Quantum Transfer Learning architecture in which a pretrained three-layer classical MLP acts as a frozen feature extractor producing a 4-dimensional latent vector that is then passed to a 4‑qubit variational quantum circuit. Each latent feature is angle-encoded onto a single qubit using RY gates, forming a four-qubit product state that is processed by a VQC composed of Hadamard and trainable unitary U(θq) gates, with only the quantum parameters optimized while the classical extractor remains fixed; measurement in the computational basis produces expectation values that are fed into a final sigmoid output layer for classification. The quantum circuit is implemented in PennyLane as a 4‑qubit VQC, trained with binary cross-entropy loss and the Adam optimizer on an 80/20 stratified split of the credit dataset.
    [codes: C-002, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The hybrid QTL model achieves an accuracy of about 91.08% and an AUC up to approximately 0.88–0.91 on the credit risk dataset, while also significantly improving recall for the minority defaulter class to around 0.62 compared with the standalone QNN’s recall of 0.16. The authors report improved AUC and overall accuracy relative to the QNN and highlight marked gains in recall and AUC for the hybrid architecture.
    [codes: C-012, C-013, C-030, C-031, C-036]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper benchmarks the hybrid QTL model against both a standalone QNN trained directly on the credit risk data and a purely classical neural network, reporting that QTL significantly outperforms the standalone QNN and achieves performance on par with or better than the classical network. The authors note that while the classical model performs well on the majority (non‑defaulter) class, it exhibits low sensitivity to the minority defaulter class, whereas the hybrid model improves minority-class recall and can outperform the classical NN according to their reported table.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-005, C-011, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly characterize the standalone QNN as suffering from poor recall on the minority class, convergence inefficiencies, and high training cost. They attribute these weaknesses to high computational demands, limited capacity to learn complex feature hierarchies without prior preprocessing, sensitivity to parameter initialization, and gradient noise from the parameter‑shift rule on simulated quantum circuits, positioning the hybrid QTL approach as a response to these limitations rather than claiming to have removed them entirely.
    [codes: C-034, C-035]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Not addressed in this paper
    [codes: —]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims as its main contribution an efficient application of a hybrid quantum–classical Quantum Transfer Learning model to improve credit risk classification, specifically demonstrating how such hybrid models can be effectively applied to real-world financial datasets. It further positions the work as providing a detailed benchmark comparison between a standalone QNN and a hybrid QTL model, and as showing that hybrid architectures can enhance minority-class (defaulter) recall and overall AUC while reducing quantum overhead, thus offering a promising path forward under current hardware constraints.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-006, C-008, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-037]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a public Kaggle credit risk dataset with 24 normalized features and 32,580 instances, split into stratified 80% training and 20% testing sets, and evaluates performance using accuracy, AUC, precision, recall, and related metrics. The classical component is a pretrained three-layer MLP feature extractor that is frozen during QTL training, and the hybrid model is trained with binary cross-entropy loss and Adam, with implementation on a 4‑qubit PennyLane VQC; the pipeline and mathematical formulation of feature extraction, encoding, and measurement are described in detail, which supports reproducibility of the architecture given access to similar tools and data.
    [codes: C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors frame transfer learning more broadly as reusing pretrained models to solve related tasks, arguing that this reduces the need to train models from scratch, thereby improving computational efficiency and lowering carbon emissions; they conclude that hybrid quantum–classical architectures offer a meaningful path that balances performance improvements with present quantum hardware limitations. These remarks position the work within a sustainability and near‑term feasibility narrative rather than purely algorithmic innovation.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-009, C-037]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to credit_lending because it tackles loan default prediction on a credit risk dataset and evaluates whether a hybrid quantum-classical model can improve defaulter detection. The most important lending-specific takeaway is the reported gain in minority-class recall, which matters for identifying likely defaults in credit scoring workflows.

=== PAPER 2a0770a1a995 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames financial forecasting as two supervised learning problems at Itaú Unibanco: predicting which customers will churn in the next three months based on the previous six months of behavior, with an operational focus on high‑precision intervention, and estimating SME credit default probabilities using a large tabular risk dataset evaluated via Gini score.
    [codes: C-001, C-005, C-017, C-019, C-039, C-040, C-041]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The work explores two quantum‑inspired/quantum approaches: (i) a DPP‑enhanced Random Forest that replaces uniform subsampling with Determinantal Point Process sampling, including a novel batched DPP-RF procedure and corresponding quantum circuits for DPP sampling with theoretically favorable gate complexity, and (ii) quantum neural networks using orthogonal and compound layers built from RBS‑based orthogonal circuits, compound matrices acting on fixed Hamming‑weight subspaces, and custom data loaders (unary, RY, H) plus an expectation‑per‑subspace loss to compress exponential output. For hardware experiments, the churn DPP pipeline is made hardware‑ready via PCA and small batches, while QNNs are trained classically and then deployed as quantum inference circuits on IBM hardware using the trained parameters.
    [codes: C-006, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-016, C-026, C-028, C-031, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-038, C-047]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: For churn prediction, the DPP‑RF model improves precision at low recall from about 71.6% to 77.5% (about a 6% absolute increase) and yields modest gains in withdrawals captured and maximum possible withdrawals captured relative to the baseline Random Forest. For credit risk, orthogonal and compound‑layer networks achieve Gini scores comparable to a classical residual baseline while using far fewer trainable parameters. On quantum hardware, the IBM processor reproduces classical/simulator DPP performance only for very small matrix dimensions, and for QNNs the OrthoResNN’s hardware Gini is close to the noiseless simulation and can be brought even closer with error mitigation, whereas the compound model’s hardware performance degrades substantially.
    [codes: C-002, C-007, C-020, C-023, C-024, C-029, C-036, C-043, C-044, C-049, C-050, C-052]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors report that classical DPP‑enhanced Random Forests outperform a standard Random Forest baseline on precision, efficiency, and business bottom‑line KPIs for churn, despite higher training time. For credit risk, the OrthoResNN with orthogonal layers matches the baseline residual network’s Gini with substantially fewer parameters, and OpenML benchmarks show only small performance differences between orthogonal/quantum‑inspired architectures and standard feed‑forward networks, suggesting parity rather than clear dominance. Quantum hardware results are compared mainly to classical/noiseless simulations rather than to separate classical baselines.
    [codes: C-007, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-043, C-044, C-046, C-029, C-049, C-050]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges several limitations. Classical DPP sampling remains computationally costly, with naive methods taking exponential time, and even practical batched DPP‑RF training is much slower than a standard Random Forest and sensitive to batch size choices. Quantum DPP hardware experiments require strong problem reduction (PCA to very low dimensions and small batches), and the IBM processor matches simulator performance only for very small matrices; as dimensions grow, hardware noise leads to degraded classifier performance. For QNNs, hardware inference was limited to a small subsample of 300 test points, and orthogonal versus compound layers show different robustness, with the compound architecture performing much worse on hardware. The authors also stress that error‑mitigation techniques, while helpful for OrthoResNN, add overhead, rely partly on heuristics, and may not scale efficiently to larger circuit sizes.
    [codes: C-014, C-021, C-022, C-027, C-028, C-030, C-048, C-050, C-053]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors suggest that quantum‑inspired methods already offer benefits as classical ML enhancements but anticipate greater impact as quantum hardware improves. They highlight that quantum DPP sampling can in principle achieve lower complexity than classical methods and that their quantum circuits for DPPs have favorable gate counts and depths, implying potential future runtime advantages once hardware noise and scale constraints are addressed. More broadly, they expect these quantum or quantum‑inspired architectures to run faster and achieve better forecasting performance with more advanced devices.
    [codes: C-004, C-015, C-026, C-054, C-055]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is positioned as demonstrating how quantum machine learning ideas—specifically DPP‑based sampling and orthogonal/compound QNN layers—can improve real‑world financial forecasting tasks at a major bank. Methodologically, the authors introduce quantum algorithms for DPP sampling coupled with a novel batched DPP-RF training procedure, and propose quantum neural architectures with orthogonal and compound layers that reduce redundancy, mitigate vanishing gradients, and, via a pyramid circuit, exactly represent the special orthogonal group while using significantly fewer parameters than classical layers. They further claim empirical evidence that these quantum‑inspired models can match or modestly improve classical performance on finance use cases and benchmarks, and that orthogonal layers are particularly NISQ‑friendly based on hardware experiments.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-009, C-010, C-012, C-016, C-032, C-035, C-036, C-038, C-043, C-051, C-054]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The study uses large, realistic proprietary datasets for churn (304k points, 153 features) and SME credit risk (~141.5k observations, 32 features), with clearly described temporal splits and business KPIs such as precision at low recall, withdrawals captured, and Gini scores. The authors explore multiple model architectures (residual, orthogonal, compound) and report results across internal use cases and public benchmarks, including ROC‑AUC comparisons that show small differences, suggesting careful benchmarking. For hardware experiments, they transparently describe dimensionality reduction via PCA, batch sizing, and the constrained test subsample of 300 points, as well as the hybrid setup where models are trained classically and then run on quantum processors with error mitigation. These details indicate attention to experimental realism and to making the methods feasible under current NISQ constraints.
    [codes: C-005, C-018, C-019, C-022, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-025, C-028, C-031, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-052]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper emphasizes structural properties of its circuits: RBS‑based orthogonal layers preserve Hamming weight, compound layers act blockwise on fixed Hamming‑weight subspaces, and orthogonality is claimed to reduce redundancy and help avoid vanishing gradients; the expectation‑per‑subspace loss is motivated as a way to capture information about the output distribution despite exponential Hilbert space size. The authors also interpret the hardware results as evidence that orthogonal layers are more NISQ‑friendly than compound layers.
    [codes: C-032, C-034, C-035, C-038, C-051]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it includes a direct credit default prediction use case on SME data, evaluated with Gini and compared against parameter-efficient quantum-inspired architectures. It also shows the practical tradeoff between model compactness and hardware feasibility, which matters for deploying credit scoring models under real-world constraints.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: The credit-risk task is explicitly probability-of-default prediction on an SME dataset, making it directly aligned with credit scoring and default modeling.
    [codes: C-039, C-040, C-041  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: Orthogonal QNNs match the classical residual baseline’s Gini while using far fewer parameters.
    [codes: C-003, C-042, C-043, C-044  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 3: The paper’s credit-risk results are benchmarked carefully, with only small differences versus standard feed-forward models, suggesting parity rather than clear superiority.
    [codes: C-025, C-046, C-044  dim_source: dimension_4]
  KEY_POINT 4: Hardware deployment is constrained: inference was run on only 300 test points, and the compound model degraded much more than the orthogonal model, while error mitigation helped the orthogonal model.
    [codes: C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-053  dim_source: dimension_5]
  KEY_POINT 5: The paper’s broader claim is that quantum ideas can already be turned into classical ML solutions today, with better hardware potentially improving speed and results later.
    [codes: C-004, C-054, C-055, C-015, C-026  dim_source: dimension_6]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-039, C-040, C-041, C-003, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-025, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-004, C-054, C-055, C-015, C-026

=== PAPER 2fe0d0f15604 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets a credit sales risk classification task: determining a customer’s reliability (whether goods can be issued or sales should be blocked) from selected decision variables, posed as a two-class problem that also contains additional subgroups of samples in each class and is not well separated in the original feature space.
    [codes: C-001, C-010, C-019]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors propose a hybrid variational quantum eigensolver (VQE)–based classifier for credit sales risk, in which classical data samples are first normalized and amplitude-encoded into normalized quantum states, then a parameterized ansatz circuit is trained via a VQE-style expectation minimization to produce class-specific pattern states, and finally a SWAP-Test–based similarity measurement plus a decision rule based on high |0⟩ probabilities on three qubits is used for classification. The method emphasizes suitability for NISQ-era hardware, using structurally simple VQA circuits, a low-complexity SWAP-Test, and ansatz designs explicitly constructed to recreate the entanglement structure observed in the encoded data, including a compact nine-qubit register layout with ancilla, VQE, and data sub-registers and two ansatz variants (a standard and a simplified, data-structure-aware circuit).
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-018, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-034]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors report that their VQE+SWAP-Test classifier achieves high training-set accuracy on the proprietary credit-risk dataset, with best performance reaching 100% classification efficiency when classification thresholds are properly tuned. They find that the three-qubit amplitude encoding of eight features (with one padded zero feature) not only reduces the number of qubits but also speeds up the optimization process compared to an eight-qubit encoding. Analysis of the encoded samples shows that in most cases all three qubits are entangled, with distinct entanglement partitions for the two classes, and the authors claim the overall algorithm has polynomial complexity and requires only a logarithmically increasing number of qubits in the feature dimension.
    [codes: C-005, C-016, C-017, C-035, C-036, C-038, C-039]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Using classical exploratory analysis, the authors observe that although correlations between variables are weak and the raw classes are not well separated, PCA reveals class-based clustering. They further report that several classical classifiers can fit the same training data extremely well, with some models attaining 100% fit but being described as quite complex, which they implicitly contrast with their compact, logarithmic-qubit quantum representation.
    [codes: C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-036]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors caution that the choice of acceptance threshold—the boundary distance from a class center to its most distant elements—is critical: if set too high it may cause class overlap and degrade classification quality. They also implicitly delimit the scope of their optimization findings by noting that the barren plateaus problem has not been observed for this problem instance, which should not be generalized beyond the studied setting.
    [codes: C-033, C-041]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future extensions, the authors suggest distributing observations across independent quantum circuits to realize parallel processing, which in principle could yield an additional linear speed-up for their classification procedure.
    [codes: C-037]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is positioned as a compact, NISQ-feasible hybrid VQE-based classifier for credit sales risk that uses amplitude encoding to achieve logarithmic qubit scaling in the feature dimension, explicitly designs the ansatz to reproduce empirically observed class-specific entanglement patterns, and employs a low-depth SWAP-Test for similarity-based classification, thereby offering a resource-efficient alternative to complex classical models on this dataset.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-009, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-035, C-036]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a real but proprietary dataset from a Polish pharmaceutical wholesaler covering a specific period in 2003 and documents a two-step normalization (classical min–max to [0,1] followed by quantum amplitude normalization, with an additional zero-valued feature to match the three-qubit encoding). The authors perform classical exploratory analyses (correlations, separability, PCA) before designing the quantum ansatz to match observed entanglement, and they note that barren plateaus were not observed in their optimization runs. Reproducibility is supported by public availability of the full source code for all numerical experiments and plot generation in an online repository.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-041, C-042]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None.
    [codes: —]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to credit_lending because it tackles a credit-risk classification problem using a compact quantum classifier for deciding whether a customer is reliable enough for goods/credit issuance [C-001]. Its main value for this silo is the combination of credit-risk framing, low-qubit encoding, and class-separation handling on a real proprietary dataset with reported high training performance [C-010, C-015, C-038].
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Directly models a credit-risk decision of customer reliability, matching credit scoring / default-style classification needs.
    [codes: C-001, C-010  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: Uses an 8-feature-to-3-qubit encoding with a padded zero feature, which is relevant for compact quantum representations of borrower attributes.
    [codes: C-015, C-036, C-039  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: Reports that the classes are not well separated and have weak correlations, but PCA reveals clusters, which is important for credit-risk data that may be hard to separate directly.
    [codes: C-019, C-020, C-021  dim_source: dimension_4]
  KEY_POINT 4: Claims high training-set classification efficiency, including 100% performance when thresholds are tuned, and notes that the reduced-qubit version speeds optimization.
    [codes: C-038, C-039, C-035  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 5: Highlights a thresholding caveat: acceptance thresholds can cause class overlap if set too high, which is directly relevant to approval/decline boundary setting in credit decisions.
    [codes: C-033, C-028, C-027  dim_source: dimension_5]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-001, C-010, C-015, C-019, C-021, C-033, C-038, C-039, C-036

=== PAPER 30afc00e8912 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets forecasting credit/financial defaults as a way to improve risk management and financial system resilience, formulating this as a binary classification problem where the model outputs a probability of default for each customer based on high‑dimensional time‑series financial data. The broader motivation is to explore whether integrating quantum computing with classical machine learning in a hybrid model can better capture complex financial patterns than purely classical systems, which the authors argue struggle with problems involving exponential solution spaces.
    [codes: C-001, C-009, C-010, C-012, C-013, C-018]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study employs a hybrid neural network ("dressed quantum circuit") that embeds a parameterized quantum circuit (PQC) as a quantum layer between classical input and output layers. The PQC uses angle encoding of features and consists of RY rotation gates followed by multi‑qubit controlled CNOT entangling gates, arranged in one or multiple layers and experimented with varying numbers of qubits; the quantum layer is explicitly intended to leverage superposition and entanglement to increase model expressibility for complex, large‑scale, high‑dimensional financial time series.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-011, C-013, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-019]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Across experiments, the hybrid model achieves around 0.88 accuracy on the credit default prediction task, with training loss decreasing and stabilizing as training proceeds and as quantum resources are increased. Specifically, a five‑qubit configuration plateaus at a loss of about 0.233 with 0.88 accuracy, while increasing to eight qubits yields a lower loss (~0.217); RMSprop gives the lowest reported loss (~0.213), and increasing the PQC depth from two to three layers slightly reduces loss further (~0.216 to ~0.216, then ~0.216 to ~0.216 according to the reported values) while maintaining similar classification metrics. The authors summarize that adding qubits and quantum layers generally reduces loss and that RMSprop performs marginally better than alternative optimizers on this hybrid setup.
    [codes: C-004, C-019, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-033, C-034]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper reports that the hybrid neural network trains significantly faster than state‑of‑the‑art classical gradient‑boosting algorithms on the same dataset, citing an HNN training time of 453.3 seconds versus 3,884.2–4,991.3 seconds for CatBoost, LightGBM, and XGBoost. The authors frame this as evidence of enhanced efficiency in training time relative to these classical baselines, but the comparison is limited to training duration rather than predictive performance differences.
    [codes: C-005, C-031, C-032]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that increasing the number of qubits and the depth of the quantum circuit, while improving loss, also leads to a notable rise in training time and computational demands, creating a trade‑off between performance gains and resource costs. They explicitly state that careful calibration of quantum circuit parameters is needed to balance enhanced model performance against computational overheads. Additionally, they clarify that no new datasets were generated or analyzed beyond the existing data source, implicitly limiting the scope of empirical validation to a single dataset.
    [codes: C-035, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors suggest that their hybrid model could be adapted to a range of other real‑world problems beyond credit default prediction, implying future work in extending and testing this architecture across additional financial and non‑financial domains.
    [codes: C-038]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims as its main contribution the design and empirical study of a hybrid neural network that tightly integrates classical and parameterized quantum circuit layers for financial default prediction, with a quantum circuit architecture tailored to complex, large‑scale, high‑dimensional financial time‑series data. It further presents contributions in exploring scalability via systematic variation of qubit count and circuit depth, in comparing multiple optimizers for training the hybrid model, and in demonstrating reduced training time relative to classical gradient‑boosting methods on a large credit default dataset.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-019, C-031, C-032]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a large real‑world credit card default dataset, applies extensive classical preprocessing including Chi‑score feature selection to retain the top 64 features, and structures the hybrid network with explicit classical input (sigmoid‑activated linear transformation) and output (sigmoid single‑neuron probability) layers around the quantum layer. Experiments follow a systematic design by varying three main hyperparameter axes—qubit count, optimizer choice, and PQC depth—and adopt a 60:40 random train‑test split with 275,347 training and 183,566 test examples, training for up to 20 epochs until the loss stabilizes. The work is conducted entirely in simulation rather than on physical quantum hardware (as indicated by the broader metadata), and no new datasets are generated, which has implications for reproducibility and external validity.
    [codes: C-014, C-015, C-016, C-018, C-019, C-021, C-022, C-037]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The introduction frames quantum computing as a fundamentally new information‑processing paradigm whose distinctive properties—superposition enabling parallelism and entanglement enabling correlated multi‑qubit states—are argued to underpin potential advantages in quantum machine learning models for finance. These conceptual statements motivate the hybrid approach but are more foundational background than specific to the implemented method.
    [codes: C-006, C-007, C-008, C-010]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to credit_lending because it tackles credit card default prediction, a core task for credit scoring and lending risk assessment, using a hybrid quantum-classical model. Its most useful takeaways for this silo are the reported default-prediction performance, the feature-selection and binary-probability setup, and the trade-off between improved loss and higher computational cost.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Directly models default risk as a binary probability output for credit decisioning.
    [codes: C-001, C-012, C-018  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: Uses a credit-card dataset with feature selection and a structured input/output pipeline suited to lending data.
    [codes: C-014, C-015, C-016, C-018, C-021, C-022  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 3: Reports strong default-prediction performance, including 0.88 accuracy and lower loss with more qubits or deeper circuits.
    [codes: C-023, C-024, C-025, C-033, C-034  dim_source: dimension_3]

    [codes: C-005, C-031, C-032  dim_source: dimension_4]

[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames quantum computing as a potentially transformative tool for financial portfolio optimization and broader financial decision-making, motivated by the limits of traditional approaches like Markowitz MPT and Monte Carlo simulations in handling vast datasets and highly complex, high-dimensional optimization problems. It argues that quantum parallelism and related phenomena could offer advantages for risk assessment, high-frequency trading, and other financial tasks. Classical scale limitations are presented as a key driver for exploring quantum approaches.
    [codes: C-001, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study is conceptual and case-based, surveying several quantum approaches used by major financial institutions rather than implementing algorithms itself. It discusses QAOA and quantum machine learning explored by JPMorgan for complex portfolio selection and forecasting, QMC and quantum linear algebra methods tested by Goldman Sachs for derivative pricing and portfolio risk assessment, and D-Wave quantum annealing used by BBVA for credit risk optimization. At a higher level, it groups these under quantum optimization, Monte Carlo, and annealing methods for financial tasks.
    [codes: C-003, C-016, C-017, C-020, C-021, C-024]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Across the cases, the authors report that quantum approaches show indicative benefits such as enhanced computational efficiency, richer scenario analysis, and improvements in optimization models and risk assessments. Specifically, JPMorgan is described as achieving faster scenario analysis for market fluctuations, Goldman Sachs as seeing promise in reduced computational time for derivative pricing and improved portfolio risk assessment, and BBVA as experiencing faster risk assessment leading to more efficient credit allocation, with early-stage success more generally. A comparison table summarizes that quantum methods demonstrated faster computation for portfolio optimization tasks, though without detailed quantitative metrics.
    [codes: C-004, C-018, C-021, C-022, C-025, C-026, C-027]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper contrasts quantum approaches with traditional methods such as MPT and classical Monte Carlo, arguing that these classical models face efficiency and scalability limits for large, complex portfolios. While a comparison table claims faster computation for some portfolio optimization tasks with quantum methods, the authors also explicitly hedge that current quantum algorithms may not significantly outperform classical techniques in practice, underscoring that any advantage is still speculative and problem- and hardware-dependent.
    [codes: C-008, C-010, C-027, C-028, C-034]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors emphasize multiple limitations: current quantum hardware suffers from qubit noise, decoherence, high error rates, and scalability challenges, as illustrated in the JPMorgan, Goldman Sachs, and BBVA cases. Algorithmic maturity is also cited as a constraint, with many methods remaining experimental and not yet practically scalable. They stress that, in their current form, quantum algorithms may not significantly outperform classical methods, so quantum advantage is immature. Beyond technical issues, they highlight integration complexity with existing systems, regulatory uncertainties, and associated ethical questions around quantum-driven financial decisions as barriers to widespread adoption.
    [codes: C-005, C-019, C-023, C-026, C-028, C-030, C-034, C-037, C-038]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work is framed along both technical and organizational lines. Technically, the authors anticipate that advances such as error-corrected quantum processors and more mature algorithms will be crucial, alongside further research on real-time implementation of quantum financial models and their regulatory implications. They see hybrid quantum–classical models as a key near- and medium-term direction for financial systems, and expect expansion of applications beyond portfolio optimization into fraud detection, high-frequency trading, cryptography, and quantum-enhanced machine learning for risk prediction. Organizationally, they argue institutions should invest in quantum literacy and develop quantum-ready strategies.
    [codes: C-006, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-036, C-039]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is primarily a qualitative, multiple-case synthesis of how leading financial institutions are experimenting with different quantum approaches in portfolio optimization and related tasks, using secondary evidence. The paper positions itself as offering case-based insights into operational benefits, implementation challenges, and long-term implications of quantum computing in finance, rather than providing new algorithms or empirical benchmarks. It also emphasizes the emerging importance of hybrid quantum–classical integration and quantum-readiness as conceptual contributions for practitioners and researchers.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-011, C-012, C-029, C-031, C-035]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a qualitative, multiple case study design focusing on JPMorgan, Goldman Sachs, and BBVA, selected based on early adoption of quantum R&D and availability of published case studies, white papers, and collaborations. It relies entirely on secondary data sources such as academic articles and institutional publications, without running quantum experiments or accessing proprietary datasets. As a result, its findings are interpretive and dependent on reported outcomes from these institutions, which may limit reproducibility and the ability to independently verify performance claims.
    [codes: C-002, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Beyond portfolio optimization, the paper repeatedly stresses broader, long-term implications of quantum computing for financial risk assessment, high-frequency trading, fraud detection, and financial cryptography, and argues that institutions must plan for quantum integration with existing classical infrastructures. It notes that quantum-enhanced machine learning could reshape risk prediction and fraud detection, while quantum cryptography may strengthen financial security, reinforcing a cross-cutting view of quantum computing as a strategic, system-level technology for financial services.

=== PAPER 32f6aa86e556 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames the problem as accelerating high-dimensional, noisy financial model testing—specifically backtesting and forward testing—by introducing finance-specialized qubits (QFOQs) tailored to financial data characteristics and aiming to reduce computational time while preserving or improving predictive accuracy under noisy market conditions.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-005, C-006]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The proposed quantum approach centers on Quantum Finance Optimization Qubits (QFOQs) with adaptive qubit states and specialized noise-resistant error correction for financial signals, combined with quantum amplitude amplification to increase the selection probability of high-value trading signals and quantum Monte Carlo/pathfinding-based techniques to speed up scenario analysis, implemented on platforms such as IBM Qiskit or Rigetti’s Forest.
    [codes: C-003, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-040]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors claim that QFOQs can theoretically reduce backtesting and forward testing time complexity from exponential O(2^n) to polynomial O(n^k) and empirically (in illustrative results) reduce execution time by 50–90% relative to classical systems, with a statistically significant time reduction (p < 0.001). They also report improved predictive accuracy (e.g., MAE reduced to 2.5%, a 52% reduction vs. baseline), greater noise resilience (only a 10% increase in error under noise), and an 82% adaptability score across changing market phases, along with general claims of enhanced model stability via noise-resistant protocols.
    [codes: C-020, C-024, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study explicitly benchmarks QFOQ-based testing against traditional classical computing approaches in finance, hypothesizing and then (in illustrative analyses) claiming at least 50% and up to 90% reductions in computational time while maintaining or improving prediction accuracy compared to classical models, with classical baselines used for execution time and error metrics.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-006, C-033, C-035, C-036]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that hardware limitations may affect performance outcomes of the QFOQ approach and further note that regulatory acceptance will require quantum models to satisfy transparency and explainability standards, constraining practical deployment in regulated financial environments.
    [codes: C-016, C-032]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include validating and extending the practical impact of QFOQs on financial modelling and risk management, applying accelerated forward testing to portfolio rebalancing under various future market conditions, using QFOQs for extensive stress testing, scenario simulation, and complex derivative pricing, and developing hybrid classical–quantum algorithms as well as scalable quantum hardware optimized for financial applications.
    [codes: C-025, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is positioned as the introduction of finance-specific Quantum Finance Optimization Qubits that specialize qubit behavior for high-dimensional, noisy financial data, integrating adaptive qubit states, tailored error correction/noise resistance, and quantum optimization components (including amplitude amplification and quantum pathfinding/Monte Carlo) to accelerate backtesting and forward testing while preserving model accuracy.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-033, C-034]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper outlines a classical–quantum comparative research design with explicit hypotheses on time reduction and accuracy preservation, detailed data selection (historical prices, economic indicators, sentiment) and preprocessing (normalization, noise filtering), and an experiment setup using platforms like IBM Qiskit or Rigetti with amplitude amplification and quantum Monte Carlo components. Statistical rigor is emphasized via plans for t-tests on execution time, ANOVA for accuracy across market conditions, chi-square-style error analyses, iterative calibration of QFOQs, repeated runs (at least five) for reliability, and testing across multiple market regimes (bull, bear, high volatility) to support external validity, along with a modeled efficiency equation that captures overheads from noise correction and dimensionality reduction via superposition.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-040]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper also links the adaptive qubit design of QFOQs to high-frequency trading applications, arguing that their dynamic response to changing market conditions is essential where millisecond-level timing is critical.
    [codes: C-026]
[SILO OVERLAY]

=== PAPER 36cd7ccae10c (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames the problem as the inability of traditional and classical AI-based risk modeling methods to capture intricate, high-dimensional, and real-time dynamics of modern financial systems, including portfolio optimization, fraud detection, and credit scoring. It identifies a specific gap: the lack of scalable, efficient hybrid AI–quantum systems capable of real-time prediction and mitigation of financial risks, and emphasizes the growing need for faster and more accurate risk models in increasingly complex markets. The overarching formulation is that AI–quantum convergence can reframe financial risk modeling and enterprise decision systems as high-dimensional optimization and pattern-recognition tasks amenable to quantum-enhanced computation.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-013, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-041, C-045, C-048, C-071]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors conceptually discuss a family of quantum and hybrid methods rather than implementing a specific algorithm, focusing on quantum-enhanced ML (Quantum Neural Networks and Quantum Support Vector Machines using quantum kernels and data encoded into quantum states), quantum optimization approaches (QAOA and quantum annealing for exploring large solution spaces in portfolio optimization), and quantum-inspired algorithms that simulate quantum phenomena on classical hardware as an intermediate step. They also describe generic quantum computing constructs—qubits, superposition, entanglement, quantum gates, and high-connectivity quantum processors—as the implementation substrate, and highlight mapping financial data into quantum states for credit risk analysis and other applications. Hybrid quantum–classical systems are positioned as the emerging architectural pattern where quantum subroutines handle parts of problems benefiting from quantum parallelism while classical components manage data preprocessing and post-processing.
    [codes: C-002, C-016, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-046, C-047, C-050, C-061, C-064, C-087]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: As a conceptual/review piece, the paper reports no new quantitative experiments but makes a series of performance claims about quantum-enhanced AI in finance: quantum optimization and parallelism purportedly handle high-dimensional datasets and optimization problems more efficiently, enabling real-time processing of data streams and larger solution spaces in portfolio optimization, fraud detection, and credit risk modeling. The authors claim that quantum-enhanced AI models can achieve superior accuracy, efficiency, scalability, more granular credit scoring, improved anomaly/fraud detection with reduced false positives, better default prediction, and more resilient portfolios under stress scenarios, and can support real-time algorithmic trading, scenario analysis, and decision support systems at scales infeasible for classical systems. They further state that cloud-based quantum access enables practical experimentation today, and conclude that AI–quantum synergy can enhance forecasting and optimization and ultimately transform enterprise decision systems and accelerate digital transformation.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-014, C-019, C-020, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-034, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-046, C-049, C-051, C-060, C-068, C-069, C-070, C-072, C-073, C-074, C-075, C-076, C-088, C-098, C-099]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors repeatedly contrast quantum-enhanced AI with classical approaches, arguing that classical models such as Monte Carlo simulations, traditional ML, Markowitz portfolio optimization, and standard fraud and credit scoring tools are limited by computational resources and struggle to scale with high-dimensional, real-time financial data and complex constraints. In contrast, they claim quantum or hybrid models can explore far larger solution spaces via quantum parallelism, analyze more asset configurations, uncover hidden correlations, capture non-linear interactions more effectively, and thus deliver faster asset allocation, improved fraud detection with fewer false positives, more granular credit scoring, and more accurate credit-risk and portfolio-resilience assessments than purely classical systems.
    [codes: C-003, C-015, C-019, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-034, C-036, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-048, C-049, C-071, C-072, C-074]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges extensive limitations and challenges. On the technical side, current quantum hardware is not yet scalable for large real-world financial models; available qubits are too few, noisy, and decohere quickly, requiring long coherence times, high-fidelity qubits, and substantial connectivity that existing devices do not yet provide. Error correction remains an unsolved problem whose overhead exceeds current resources, and NISQ-era noise and gate errors limit the robustness and trainability of complex quantum ML models. The authors also note that portfolio optimization with hundreds or thousands of assets would need an impractically large number of qubits, and that resource intensity and upfront costs can outweigh near-term benefits. Integration with legacy fintech and classical infrastructures is described as complex, compounded by immature quantum-specific regulatory oversight, demanding compliance with stringent financial regulation and data-privacy rules. Security is highlighted as a particular limitation because powerful quantum computers threaten current cryptographic schemes (RSA, ECC), while robust post-quantum and quantum-cryptographic solutions are still emerging. Ethically, the authors warn that quantum AI’s opacity amplifies existing black-box, explainability, and accountability issues, that high-dimensional processing may inadvertently magnify hidden biases and discriminatory patterns, and that tracing decision logic is especially difficult for quantum algorithms. Overall, they stress that scalability, integration, and governance challenges mean quantum supremacy in financial decision-making remains distant and contingent on substantial advances in hardware and algorithms.
    [codes: C-009, C-010, C-041, C-045, C-048, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055, C-056, C-058, C-059, C-062, C-063, C-065, C-066, C-078, C-081, C-083, C-084, C-091, C-094, C-100]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work is framed around making AI–quantum finance systems practical, robust, and governable. The authors call for advances in quantum hardware (improved coherence, lower error rates, better circuit design), and for quantum algorithms and QML techniques that are more robust, accurate, scalable, and tailored to noisy devices, including improved error-mitigation and error-correction strategies for QML. They highlight emerging hybrid quantum–classical architectures, novel quantum ML methods, and the integration of AI with quantum cryptography to enhance cybersecurity as promising research directions, while noting that achieving any form of quantum supremacy in financial decision-making will require substantial breakthroughs. On the institutional side, they foresee the need for regulatory evolution: methods to certify quantum-enhanced algorithms, the creation of international standards, and frameworks to ensure fairness, accountability, and compliance. They also argue that early adopters could gain strategic advantages and that ongoing digital transformation of financial services could be accelerated as these technologies mature.
    [codes: C-051, C-067, C-076, C-085, C-086, C-087, C-088, C-089, C-090, C-092, C-094, C-095, C-097, C-099]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper presents itself as a conceptual exploration and synthesis that frames the convergence of AI and quantum computing as a paradigm shift in financial risk modeling and enterprise decision systems, emphasizing quantum-enhanced AI as a means to tackle high-dimensional optimization and pattern-recognition problems beyond classical capabilities. Its claimed contribution lies in articulating how hybrid AI–quantum models, spanning portfolio optimization, fraud detection, credit risk, and enterprise decision support, could redefine risk modeling, highlighting both transformative potential and the associated ethical, regulatory, and implementation challenges.
    [codes: C-001, C-013, C-016, C-019, C-020, C-068, C-073, C-096, C-097, C-098]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The article is explicitly conceptual and review-oriented, with no original datasets, quantitative benchmarks, or implementation details reported; claimed performance gains for quantum-enhanced models are presented at a high level and not supported by reproducible experiments or metrics in this paper. The discussion of AI and quantum methods is largely theoretical—describing capabilities of ML on nonlinear, high-dimensional data, qubits, entanglement, and standard QML models such as QNNs and QSVMs—as well as generic case-study narratives of portfolio optimization, fraud detection, credit scoring, and risk management without specifying data sources, parameter settings, or evaluation protocols. The authors nonetheless emphasize the need for fairness-aware algorithms, accountability frameworks, and algorithm certification, implicitly acknowledging that rigorous methodological and governance standards will be critical for future empirical work.
    [codes: C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-047, C-050, C-071, C-072, C-079, C-082, C-085]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Several cross-cutting themes extend beyond the core technical and comparative narrative. The authors stress the importance of ethical and regulatory considerations—transparency, fairness, accountability, and bias mitigation—within AI–quantum financial systems, alongside the potential for quantum models to support continuous compliance monitoring and real-time regulatory reporting. They also emphasize the need for cross-disciplinary collaboration among quantum physicists, AI researchers, financial experts, and cybersecurity professionals to develop practical and secure solutions, and note that quantum cloud services already offer a low-barrier environment for experimentation even before hardware matures fully.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-057, C-060, C-075, C-077, C-080, C-082, C-093]

=== PAPER 3f8ab7317ca4 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper studies quantum runtime scaling for several combinatorial optimization problems relevant to finance and data analytics—specifically portfolio optimization, feature selection, clustering, and weighted MaxCut—by reformulating all of them as quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) instances, with portfolio constraints incorporated via penalty terms in the cost function.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-013]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors employ a linear-ramp variant of QAOA in which the optimal parameters exhibit smooth increasing/decreasing patterns and are compressed into a schedule with only two free parameters, implemented as a specific parametrized quantum state. To circumvent costly high-dimensional variational optimization, they propose an extrapolation-based parameter estimation strategy that learns these two parameters on small random subinstances via grid search (with a log-scale parameter grid and a multivariate skew-Gaussian fit) and extrapolates them on a log–log scale to larger instances, then choose the QAOA depth p by minimizing a total-depth runtime metric.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-018, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-027]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Empirically, the extrapolated linear-ramp parameters agree well with those obtained from direct optimization on larger instances, supporting the validity of the extrapolation scheme. For portfolio optimization, the authors report that the quantum runtime scaling exponent is smaller than the classical one and interpret this as an indication of potential quantum scaling advantage, while for feature selection the results suggest a quantum disadvantage relative to classical methods. In clustering, they observe instability with outliers exhibiting extremely large total depths, but show that using universal parameter scaling across instances both improves scaling coefficients and keeps success probabilities for the optimal solution on average above 0.1, which they argue would, if constant, imply algebraic (subexponential) median quantum runtime.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-026, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Quantum runtime scaling is explicitly compared against several classical optimization baselines, including commercial solvers (CPLEX and GUROBI), the MQLib/Burer2002 heuristic, and the Goemans–Williamson algorithm, with classical scaling quantified via the slope of fitted models. The authors ultimately select the MQLib/Burer2002 algorithm as the main classical reference for detailed comparison, against which they report superior scaling for portfolio optimization and inferior performance for feature selection.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-014, C-016, C-017, C-029, C-030]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors caution that their runtime comparisons implicitly assume that suitable LR-QAOA circuit parameters are already known or can be efficiently found, even though the computational effort for their parameter-search and extrapolation procedure itself grows with problem size. They also highlight instability in some applications—notably clustering—where outliers with extremely large total depth values appear, indicating that the extrapolation and parameter-scaling strategies may not behave uniformly well across problem instances.
    [codes: C-031, C-038, C-039]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors argue that to substantiate or refute the observed scaling indications, future studies must cover a broader range of problem sizes beyond N = 28. They additionally call for more elaborate statistical analyses that go beyond median runtime and examine the scaling behavior of multiple runtime percentiles to better characterize performance variability.
    [codes: C-036, C-037]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims as its core contribution an extrapolation-based method that estimates suitable linear-ramp QAOA parameters for larger problem sizes using only optimizations on smaller subproblems, thereby reducing the parameter-search burden while leveraging a two-parameter linear schedule informed by observed parameter patterns. It further positions the work as providing an evaluation of quantum runtime scaling on a simulator across four QUBO-formulated applications and as demonstrating, in the portfolio case, empirically superior scaling compared to classical runtimes, albeit as a potential rather than definitive quantum advantage.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study standardizes all considered problems via QUBO reformulations, including explicit handling of portfolio constraints through penalty terms, and models clustering as a MaxCut problem, which supports comparability across domains. Classical baselines are carefully selected and their scaling quantified by fitting slopes to runtime data, while on the quantum side the authors define runtime in terms of total circuit depth D and empirically validate their parameter extrapolation by comparing predicted parameters to optima from direct grid search on larger instances. They also introduce a universal parameter-scaling scheme and report success probabilities and median runtime implications, indicating attention to both optimization landscapes and performance metrics, though all evidence is obtained on a simulator rather than hardware.
    [codes: C-002, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-013, C-014, C-016, C-018, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-032, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None.
    [codes: —]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it studies QUBO-based optimization and runtime-scaling methods that could be adapted to lending tasks where binary decisions and constrained optimization matter, such as accept/reject or portfolio-style allocation problems. Its main value for this silo is methodological: it shows how to reduce parameter-search burden and how quantum-vs-classical scaling is evaluated, which is directly relevant when assessing whether quantum methods could be practical for credit scoring or default-prediction workflows.
    [silo_relevance_score: medium]
  KEY_POINT 1: QUBO reformulation and penalty handling provide a template for constrained lending decision problems.
    [codes: C-010, C-011  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 2: The extrapolation-based LR-QAOA parameter estimation reduces the need to tune many circuit parameters on large instances.
    [codes: C-001, C-008, C-007  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: The paper’s runtime-scaling comparison against classical baselines is the main evidence framework for judging whether a quantum approach could be useful in lending applications.
    [codes: C-002, C-014, C-016, C-017  dim_source: dimension_4]
  KEY_POINT 4: The feature-selection result is a cautionary signal for credit scoring-style use cases.
    [codes: C-030  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 5: The limitations around parameter-search cost and the assumption that good parameters are already known are especially important for operational lending settings.
    [codes: C-038, C-039, C-031  dim_source: dimension_5]
