=== PAPER 3fe2b056b181 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses optimization of bank credit score cards to improve credit-risk prediction and maximize bank revenue. It formulates the task as a multivariate nonlinear integer programming problem that is transformed into a QUBO model, where binary variables represent threshold choices for credit score cards and the objective function is the bank’s total income. The authors emphasize that quantum optimization is expected to better capture correlations among multiple credit factors (e.g., history, income, debt) to produce more accurate credit scores and risk predictions.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-010, C-012, C-013, C-014]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use a QUBO formulation solved via a quantum annealing algorithm, targeting D-Wave’s quantum annealing hardware designed for combinatorial optimization problems. They describe quantum annealing as a metaheuristic for binary optimization that finds the lowest-energy state by slowly changing one Hamiltonian into another, with a probabilistic acceptance rule that can accept worse solutions during the search. The Hamiltonian includes penalty terms enforcing that each credit score card selects exactly one threshold.
    [codes: C-002, C-014, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-029]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: For the first optimization task (choosing one card and one threshold among 100 cards), the quantum annealing solution selects card 49 with threshold 1, yielding a maximum bank income of 61,172 yuan. For the second task (three designated score cards with one threshold each), the optimal combination is the 8th threshold of the first card, the 1st threshold of the second card, and the 2nd threshold of the third card, with total income of 27,914.8 yuan; the authors summarize this in an optimal strategy table. For the third task (arbitrary three-card selection with one threshold per card), the best solution chooses the 2nd threshold of card 8, the 6th threshold of card 33, and the 3rd threshold of card 49, yielding 43,880.9 yuan, and the authors argue that the relative magnitudes of these results make them “true and reasonable.”
    [codes: C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper aims to compare the feasibility and effectiveness of the quantum annealing–based model with classical algorithms and to explore its difference and advantage, noting that traditional weight calculation in credit scoring often relies on trial and error whereas quantum optimization can quickly find an optimal weight combination. For model testing, the authors apply classical enumeration for the smallest instance and depth-first search for the slightly larger instances, finding that these classical methods produce results consistent with the quantum/model-derived strategies, which they interpret as evidence of the model’s consistency and practicality. They also express an expectation (rather than demonstrated evidence) that quantum annealing will be superior in the credit score card domain.
    [codes: C-009, C-010, C-011, C-025, C-026, C-027]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several limitations of their annealing-based QUBO approach. They note that annealing is a probabilistic global optimization method with inherent randomness, so it may not reliably find the global optimum and can yield only suboptimal solutions. They further state that the accuracy of the QUBO model is limited by the number of available binary qubits, and that when more complex problems are tackled under these hardware constraints, solution accuracy may decrease.
    [codes: C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Looking ahead, the authors state that they expect to prove the superiority of the quantum annealing algorithm for credit score card optimization, indicating that such superiority has not yet been conclusively demonstrated. They also claim that their QUBO-based model can be extended to larger-scale optimization problems, often without redesigning the entire system, suggesting future work on scalability to more complex, higher-dimensional credit scoring settings.
    [codes: C-011, C-030]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is framed as formulating credit score card optimization for bank revenue maximization as a QUBO problem and applying quantum annealing to jointly optimize card and threshold selections, with the intent to demonstrate feasibility and potential superiority over classical trial-and-error weight-setting. The authors further claim that this model is suitable for NP-Hard combinatorial optimization problems in credit scoring and can find better solutions in a short time relative to other algorithms, positioning their approach as a promising new application of quantum annealing in credit risk evaluation.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-028, C-030]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper explicitly constructs an integer programming objective that maximizes bank total income and encodes it with binary variables and Hamiltonian penalties to enforce exactly one threshold per card. For validation, the authors run small-scale instances where classical enumeration (for the simplest case) and depth-first search (for slightly larger cases) are computationally feasible, and they compare these classical results to those from their annealing-based model, reporting consistent strategies and running times within acceptable ranges. On this basis, they argue the model is consistent and practical for the tested scales and assert its suitability for NP-Hard problems, though details on dataset characteristics and broader robustness assessment are limited.
    [codes: C-012, C-013, C-014, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None.
    [codes: —]

=== PAPER 41d2d7bb257a (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit risk prediction, arguing that accurate assessment is essential for financial health, default minimisation, and regulatory compliance, and that current credit models struggle with nonlinear relationships in high-dimensional data and poor recall on the minority (defaulter) class. It formulates the task as a binary loan default classification problem on a real-world credit risk dataset, aiming to improve AUC and minority-class recall via a quantum-assisted model. The work is framed within a broader move from traditional risk models, whose shortcomings were highlighted in the 2008 crisis, toward machine learning and quantum-enhanced approaches in financial risk management.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-033, C-034]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use a hybrid quantum–classical transfer learning model in which a classical neural network compresses 24-dimensional input features into a 4-dimensional latent representation that is then encoded into 4 qubits via angle encoding with RY gates. The quantum component is a parameterized variational circuit comprising an embedding layer, entanglement (e.g., CNOT) layer, and trainable rotation gates; this circuit, together with the classical feature extractor and output layer, is co-trained end-to-end with binary cross-entropy loss using PennyLane’s TorchLayer inside a PyTorch pipeline. Only a selected subset of computationally significant features is encoded into quantum states, addressing data-encoding inefficiency, and all parameters (classical, quantum, and output) are updated jointly via backpropagation through the hybrid architecture.
    [codes: C-019, C-020, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-038, C-039]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The co-trained hybrid QTL model is reported to achieve an AUC of 0.9134 and a test accuracy of 91.17%, with substantially improved recall for the minority defaulter class (0.73) compared to baselines. It demonstrates strong ROC performance and high overall classification accuracy, reaching its best performance at epoch 8, and the authors emphasise that it significantly boosts minority-class detection while maintaining strong metrics on the majority class. An additional empirical finding is that training on the original unbalanced dataset, rather than SMOTE-resampled data, yields higher accuracy, which the authors attribute to overfitting in synthetic samples.
    [codes: C-001, C-005, C-012, C-013, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-035]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The hybrid co-trained QTL model is compared against a standalone QNN baseline and a conventional classical neural network, with the co-trained model claimed to significantly outperform these baselines. Relative to the standalone QNN, the hybrid model shows higher accuracy (0.9117 vs 0.8097), higher AUC (0.9134 vs 0.7446), and much better minority-class recall (0.73 vs 0.16). The standalone QNN, trained directly on raw credit data with limited preprocessing, is said to suffer from poor recall and unstable convergence, while also requiring nearly twice the training time of the hybrid model, leading the authors to describe the hybrid as both more accurate and more time-efficient.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-004, C-005, C-012, C-013, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-050, C-051]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors note several limitations: at the field level, QML is constrained by hardware limits such as qubit count, coherence time, and noise, as well as data encoding inefficiencies, generalization gaps between theory and practice, and scarce benchmarking on real datasets. At the implementation level, they highlight that data encoding inefficiency is a critical barrier and that quantum circuit simulation, especially parameter-shift gradient computation on a CPU-based simulator, is the dominant computational bottleneck. For the standalone QNN, they report poor recall, unstable convergence, high computational demands, and the need to restrict training data to 1,000 samples because of parameter-shift overhead. For the hybrid model, they acknowledge higher computational cost due to joint training of classical and quantum layers and more variable convergence behaviour, which they attribute to non-convex circuit training and high-variance gradients, explicitly stating that this variability calls for careful optimization strategies.
    [codes: C-004, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-037, C-040, C-041, C-036, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-052]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future methodological work is implied in the authors’ observation that convergence fluctuations in the hybrid model and high-variance gradients from parameter-shift rules "highlight the need for careful optimization strategies," suggesting further research into more robust training procedures for co-trained quantum–classical models.
    [codes: C-052, C-049]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The main claimed contribution is a fully co-trained hybrid quantum–classical transfer learning architecture applied to real-world financial credit risk data, where both classical and quantum components are trained simultaneously rather than using a frozen classical feature extractor. The authors argue that this end-to-end co-training enables mutual adaptation between classical representations and the quantum circuit, improves minority-class detection and AUC, and demonstrates that quantum-assisted learning can be feasibly integrated into financial decision pipelines under current simulator and hardware constraints. They further position hybrid architectures as a meaningful near-term path that balances performance gains with existing quantum technology limitations.
    [codes: C-001, C-011, C-014, C-019, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-015, C-012, C-013, C-053]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a public Kaggle credit risk dataset with 24 features and 32,580 instances, applies EDA and Min–Max normalization, and considers SMOTE to address class imbalance, ultimately opting for the unbalanced dataset after observing SMOTE-induced overfitting. The experimental setup includes a stratified 80/20 train–test split for the hybrid model; the standalone QNN baseline is trained on a reduced subset of 1,000 samples due to computational overheads. The hybrid architecture is implemented with PennyLane’s TorchLayer inside a PyTorch pipeline, trained using binary cross-entropy loss and the Adam optimizer, and evaluated with standard metrics including accuracy, ROC/AUC, precision, recall, and F1-score, with convergence behaviour and epoch-level performance explicitly reported.
    [codes: C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-038, C-039, C-032, C-045, C-042, C-043, C-048]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper situates its work in a broader context, noting that transfer learning extends beyond image-based tasks and that modern financial risk management increasingly incorporates machine learning and quantum-enhanced models. It also emphasizes that the proposed hybrid framework, by reducing dimensionality before quantum processing and only encoding selected significant features, offers a practical route to near-term deployment under current quantum technology constraints.
    [codes: C-006, C-009, C-019, C-020, C-021]

=== PAPER 470b798927ef (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper studies quantum optimization for three combinatorial problems reformulated as QUBOs: a constrained portfolio optimization problem with the budget constraint enforced via a quadratic penalty term, a feature selection task that balances feature–target relevance against inter-feature redundancy, and a clustering task reduced to a Max-Cut problem on a dense graph (including applications to financial/credit data). The overarching framing is to understand how QAOA runtime scales with problem size on these QUBO-encoded tasks, with portfolio optimization as the main finance use case.
    [codes: C-010, C-011, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use a linear-ramp variant of QAOA (LR-QAOA) where the many variational parameters of standard QAOA are reduced to two global parameters Δγ and Δβ that define linearly ramped angles across layers, motivated by observed smooth patterns in optimal QAOA parameters and an interpretation of QAOA as a discrete adiabatic schedule. They define the LR-QAOA quantum state and parameterization explicitly, and avoid full variational optimization by fixing these ramp schedules and then optimizing only the circuit depth p via minimizing an estimated total circuit depth D. A key methodological contribution is an extrapolation scheme that learns suitable Δγ, Δβ from optimized solutions on small random sub-instances, iteratively refining them via grid search and skew-Gaussian fitting and then extrapolating their scaling with N under an assumed linear log–log relation.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-009, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-034]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: For portfolio optimization, the authors report that LR-QAOA with extrapolated parameters achieves a smaller fitted exponential runtime scaling exponent (α ≈ 0.22) in total circuit depth than the chosen classical heuristic baseline, and that the success probability of obtaining the optimal solution remains approximately independent of problem size within the studied range, consistent with an earlier conjecture. In contrast, for feature selection the quantum runtime scales worse than classical (≈ 2^0.46N vs ≈ 2^0.2N), and for clustering LR-QAOA also fails to outperform classical baselines (α ≈ 0.25 vs α = 0.061), though the extrapolated parameters generally match those found by full grid search except for some outliers at particular sizes. The paper thus finds problem-dependent behavior, with an indicative scaling advantage only for portfolio optimization in the simulated regime.
    [codes: C-004, C-008, C-031, C-035, C-037, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Quantum runtime is explicitly benchmarked against several classical solvers whose runtimes on the same QUBO instances are empirically fitted to exponential models, with MQLib/Burer2002 chosen as the main comparison baseline because other solvers (CPLEX, Gurobi, Goemans–Williamson) exhibit heterogeneous scaling, discontinuities, or frequent failures to find the optimum. The authors find that different classical methods have markedly different scaling behaviors across portfolio optimization, feature selection, and clustering, and they emphasize that for feature selection and clustering the tested classical methods still scale better than LR-QAOA, whereas for portfolio optimization LR-QAOA’s fitted exponent beats the MQLib/Burer2002 baseline but not all commercial solvers in all regimes.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-013, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-035, C-037, C-039]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges several limitations: some classical solvers like Goemans–Williamson often fail to find the optimum, and commercial solvers may change their search behavior unpredictably, complicating fair scaling comparisons. For the quantum side, the authors note that the extrapolation scheme is less stable for feature selection and exhibits outlier behavior for some clustering sizes, indicating fragility of the learned schedules on certain problem classes. Crucially, they state that the cost of finding optimized circuit parameters is not included in their quantum runtime metric, even though the extrapolation-based analysis still grows with problem size, and they position their study as assessing LR-QAOA potential under the assumption that good parameters are already known or can be found efficiently. They further highlight that their observations regarding success probability and conjectured polynomial scaling are based on relatively small problem sizes (up to N = 28), so the exponential-versus-polynomial scaling question remains unresolved.
    [codes: C-021, C-022, C-023, C-038, C-040, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors call for extending the analysis to larger problem sizes beyond N = 28 to clarify whether LR-QAOA can achieve polynomial-time scaling for some combinatorial optimization problems, explicitly flagging the broader open question of whether quantum optimization can yield polynomial-time solutions in such settings. They also propose that methods based on extrapolation and scaling of circuit parameters with increasing problem size are a promising direction for future research on QAOA parameter setting and should be further developed and tested.
    [codes: C-043, C-047, C-048]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper’s main contribution is the development of an extrapolation-based method to set the two parameters of a linear-ramp QAOA schedule using optimized solutions from small sub-instances, thereby largely avoiding expensive variational optimization while evaluating QAOA runtime scaling on multiple combinatorial problems. Building on observed smooth patterns in optimal QAOA parameters and a prior conjecture about constant optimal-solution probability under fixed linear ramps, the authors claim to systematically address the formerly unresolved problem of how to find suitable linear-ramp parameters and to demonstrate, in simulation, an indicative scaling improvement over a classical heuristic for portfolio optimization.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-005, C-006, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-035]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study defines quantum runtime as the median total circuit depth over all circuits needed to find the optimum, combining QAOA depth and required shots, and optimizes QAOA depth p by minimizing this estimated depth. The parameter extrapolation protocol is described in detail: random sub-instances of increasing size are sampled, grid search over Δγ and Δβ is iteratively refined via fitting a multivariate skew Gaussian to the success-probability landscape, and then the inferred optimal parameters are extrapolated under a linear log–log assumption. The classical benchmarking is also systematically designed, with multiple sampled instances per size, repeated runtime measurements, and robust log2-linear fits to estimate scaling exponents, while noting solver-dependent irregularities and failures. The authors frame their work as an exploration of LR-QAOA’s potential under an idealized assumption that suitable parameters can be efficiently obtained, and conclude that extrapolation and scaling-based parameter strategies appear promising but still require further validation.
    [codes: C-011, C-019, C-020, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-034, C-041, C-042, C-045, C-046, C-047]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None; all extracted codes map to the defined dimensions above.
    [codes: —]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it studies QUBO-based optimization workflows that map naturally onto lending tasks like feature selection for credit scoring and constrained portfolio-style decision problems. Its main value for the silo is methodological: it shows how fixed-schedule QAOA and extrapolated parameter setting might scale on credit-like data, while also showing that the feature-selection-style task does not yet beat classical methods.
    [silo_relevance_score: medium]
  KEY_POINT 1: Feature-selection-style optimization is directly relevant to credit scoring and default prediction because the paper’s QUBO objective balances target relevance against redundancy.
    [codes: C-016, C-010  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The paper’s LR-QAOA parameter-extrapolation method is relevant because it aims to reduce variational overhead when scaling to larger instances.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-009, C-026, C-030  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: The feature-selection benchmark shows no quantum advantage over classical methods, which is a cautionary signal for credit scoring use cases.
    [codes: C-037, C-038, C-031  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: The runtime comparison framework and solver irregularities matter for lending applications because they show that benchmarking against classical baselines can be unstable and solver-dependent.
    [codes: C-012, C-013, C-021, C-022, C-023  dim_source: dimension_4]
  KEY_POINT 5: The paper’s runtime accounting caveat is important for credit_lending deployment because parameter-search cost is excluded from the quantum runtime metric.
    [codes: C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047  dim_source: dimension_5]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-016, C-002, C-003, C-009, C-037, C-038, C-012, C-013, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-044, C-045, C-046

=== PAPER 55496bcdeaa7 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames an unconstrained black-box binary optimization problem, proposing to solve it with a variational quantum algorithm where binary decision variables are mapped to qubits and evaluated via an implicit loss function (C-001, C-008, C-013). Feature selection is chosen as the primary application: selecting a subset of features is treated as an NP-hard combinatorial optimization task whose quality is measured by the performance of a downstream model on a predefined metric, here log-loss (C-002, C-004, C-014, C-019).
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-004, C-008, C-013, C-014, C-019]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors introduce a variational quantum feature selection (VarQFS) method that recasts the black-box binary optimization as a ground-state search over an implicitly defined diagonal Hamiltonian, evaluated by sampling-based loss estimation (C-006, C-008, C-015). They use a Qiskit RealAmplitudes ansatz with linear entanglement and deliberately low depth (d = 0, 1, 2) to remain compatible with MPS simulation and noisy hardware (C-020, C-021, C-023, C-024). Optimization is carried out with an approximate quantum natural gradient optimizer (QN-SPSA), motivated by its connection to imaginary-time evolution and its promise for quantum problems, with overlaps and QFI estimated via compute–uncompute overlap circuits (C-009, C-010, C-011, C-022).
    [codes: C-006, C-008, C-015, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-024, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-022]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Across experiments, the authors report that VarQFS achieves competitive and in some respects better performance than traditional feature selection methods, both on a reduced 20-feature benchmark and the full 59-feature dataset (C-003, C-028, C-034, C-036). On the 20-feature case, MPS-based training reaches a loss extremely close to the globally optimal training score obtained via exhaustive search (C-025, C-026, C-027). In the full-data setting, they observe that higher circuit depths tend to yield better best-sample test scores and that VarQFS can, for at least one seed in every setting, outperform classical baselines on roughly 90% of training instances (C-032, C-034, C-036). They also note that the ibmq montreal hardware run, despite noise and absence of error mitigation, manages to outperform traditional methods in some aspects (C-028, C-037).
    [codes: C-003, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-032, C-034, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The quantum feature selection method is compared against classical baselines such as RFE and RFECV built on logistic regression, with the quality of subsets defined by the resulting model performance on log-loss (C-014, C-019, C-028). On the exhaustively solvable 20-feature problem, VarQFS finds feature subsets with slightly better test scores than RFE/RFECV, and on the full 59-feature dataset the best VarQFS configurations improve over classical baselines in terms of both train and test scores for some seeds and settings (C-025, C-028, C-034, C-036). The authors emphasize that classical methods are mostly greedy heuristics for this NP-hard problem, which leaves room for near-term quantum algorithms to be competitive (C-004, C-005).
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-014, C-019, C-025, C-028, C-034, C-036]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges several limitations. Methodologically, convergence of the variational algorithm relies on choosing an initial state with non-zero overlap with the ground state, which they implement via a nearly uniform state (C-012). In practice, hardware experiments use fewer SPSA/QFI resampling shots than simulations due to limited quantum resources, leading to less smooth convergence (C-029). The authors attribute observed outliers in hardware loss curves to both the stochastic nature of QN-SPSA and physical noise on the quantum backend, and they do not apply any error mitigation, which constrains achievable performance (C-030, C-037). For deeper circuits in the larger-scale simulations, they point out that QN-SPSA’s approximations are more likely to deviate from the true gradient and quantum Fisher information, hampering training at higher depth (C-033). They also report a strong dependence on random seeds in the full-data experiments, indicating significant variability in performance across runs (C-035).
    [codes: C-012, C-029, C-030, C-033, C-035, C-037]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors identify several avenues for future work: more systematic study and tuning of QN-SPSA hyperparameters to improve training stability and performance (C-038), deeper investigation of how entanglement structure in the ansatz affects results (C-039), and extensions of the method to enforce fixed-size feature subsets, e.g., requiring the final selection to contain exactly m features (C-040).
    [codes: C-038, C-039, C-040]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper claims to introduce a variational quantum algorithm tailored for unconstrained black-box binary optimization, instantiated as a feature selection application where the objective is accessed only through sampled evaluations rather than an explicit Hamiltonian construction (C-001, C-002, C-006, C-008, C-015). It further emphasizes the use of a quantum natural gradient–based optimizer (QN-SPSA) motivated by its suitability for quantum problems and its connection to imaginary-time evolution, and demonstrates that this hybrid approach can be competitive with, and sometimes outperform, standard greedy classical feature selection heuristics on a real credit-risk dataset (C-005, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-016, C-028, C-036).
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-006, C-008, C-015, C-005, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-016, C-028, C-036]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The experimental setup uses a publicly available German credit-risk dataset with 1,000 data points and 20 original attributes expanded via one-hot encoding, applying a 50/50 train–test split and checking that train and test subsets have comparable feature correlations to ensure representativeness (C-016, C-017, C-018). Baseline comparisons are clearly specified using logistic regression and log-loss, and the authors employ both MPS simulation with controlled entanglement and a real IBM superconducting processor (ibmq montreal) to assess performance across idealized and noisy settings (C-019, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-024). Empirical loss is explicitly defined via measured shot frequencies, and finite-sample estimation is justified via Hoeffding’s inequality, indicating attention to statistical rigor in how the black-box objective is approximated (C-006, C-007, C-015).
    [codes: C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-024, C-006, C-007, C-015]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: No additional relevant codes fall outside the above dimensions.
    [codes: —]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it applies a quantum feature-selection method to a real credit-risk dataset and evaluates it with logistic-regression log-loss, which is directly aligned with credit scoring and default prediction workflows. Its main value for this silo is showing how a quantum optimizer could search for informative borrower attributes under a black-box performance objective, with comparisons against standard feature-selection baselines.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Quantum feature selection is applied directly to a credit-risk dataset and framed as selecting borrower attributes that improve a downstream classifier.
    [codes: C-002, C-014, C-016, C-019  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The objective is evaluated through logistic regression and log-loss, making the method tightly connected to credit scoring model selection.
    [codes: C-014, C-019, C-015, C-006  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 3: VarQFS is compared against classical feature-selection baselines and can slightly outperform them on test score, suggesting potential value for credit-risk model development.
    [codes: C-003, C-028, C-034, C-036, C-005  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: The experiments use a real credit-worthiness dataset with train/test splitting and representativeness checks, which makes the results more directly applicable to lending data workflows.
    [codes: C-016, C-017, C-018  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 5: The paper highlights scalability and stability limits, including seed dependence, hardware noise, and depth-related approximation issues, which are important for practical credit-lending deployment.
    [codes: C-029, C-030, C-033, C-035, C-037  dim_source: dimension_5]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-002, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-003, C-028, C-034, C-036, C-029, C-030, C-033, C-035, C-037

=== PAPER 620d7dc5208d (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames quantum computing as a promising way to outperform classical computing across multiple financial tasks, highlighting a landscape that includes credit scoring, fraud detection, option pricing, investment recommendation, and dynamic portfolio optimization as motivating use cases for the bank’s quantum program.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-029, C-030, C-040]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The work is conceptual and organizational rather than algorithmically detailed, but it notes experimentation on two quantum computing paradigms—D-Wave quantum annealers and IBM universal gate-based systems—to compare capabilities across platforms, and references a separate paper where the team proposed a variant of the CRA quantum algorithm that enhances risk models and is tested via both classical simulation and quantum processing units. Proof-of-concept projects are selected in domains where quantum computing is projected to offer significant advantages, such as credit risk analysis and portfolio optimization.
    [codes: C-019, C-020, C-033, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Key reported outcomes are organizational and scholarly rather than numerical: the QCC’s experimentation with multiple platforms has yielded a more nuanced understanding of how different quantum technologies can be tailored to specific financial applications, it has produced its first peer-reviewed journal paper on a CRA algorithm variant, and it has learned from hackathon participation that effective quantum projects require parallel domain-expert and technical mentorship. The authors also note that PoC-driven scholarly publications contribute to the broader knowledge base on quantum computing in finance.
    [codes: C-020, C-032, C-034, C-038, C-041]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper does not present direct empirical performance comparisons, but it notes that the CRA algorithm variant was tested through classical simulation as well as on QPUs, and more generally defines potential quantum advantage as either faster achievement of the same outcome or finer solution precision than classical approaches. It also motivates quantum exploration by pointing out limitations in classical solutions for fraud detection (unbalanced datasets) and credit scoring (difficulty in selecting meaningful input features).
    [codes: C-029, C-033, C-040]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge that classical approaches to fraud detection and credit scoring face concrete issues such as highly unbalanced datasets and challenges in choosing meaningful features, which currently constrain performance. At a strategic level, they highlight a major limitation and risk: the QCC must be reaffirmed as a strategic pillar in future industrial plans to avoid having to restart efforts from scratch once quantum devices are widely available as regular services, indicating organizational continuity and commitment as a key constraint.

=== PAPER 64111b3b5d61 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The chapter frames quantum computing as a transformative, fifth‑generation information technology that is expensive, hard to understand, and aimed at achieving "quantum supremacy"—surpassing classical computers on key tasks. Within this broad frame, finance appears as one of several application sectors (e.g., high‑frequency trading and optimization) where computational limits of classical systems motivate interest in quantum methods.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-027, C-029, C-035]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The paper provides an overview of quantum computation principles (qubits and superposition) and architectures, distinguishing adiabatic quantum computers suited for optimization from universal quantum computers with error‑correction. It introduces a hypothetical hybrid architecture combining adiabatic-style qubit operation with a digital, error‑correcting network and notes that quantum software must be probabilistic, with IBM-Q and various open‑source platforms presented as practical interfaces for experimentation. IBM’s Quantum Volume metric is also described as a way to jointly capture qubit count and error tolerance.
    [codes: C-005, C-009, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-037]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The chapter does not report original experimental results, but it repeats several claimed performance outcomes from the literature or industry: that quantum computers "will" calculate exponentially faster than classical machines, Grover’s algorithm offers an exponential search speedup over classical search, and Shor’s algorithm is especially powerful for breaking RSA, with the specific claim that a quantum computer could break 1024‑bit RSA in one day. It also cites work from a Toronto team on quantum Monte Carlo derivative pricing with an O(√N) runtime versus classical O(N), and states that algorithms already account for up to 70% of high‑frequency trading. Additionally, it emphasizes quantum computing’s potential efficacy in biochemistry and materials, including the assertion that current classical computers cannot adequately solve the Schrödinger equation.
    [codes: C-006, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-021, C-022, C-027, C-028, C-033]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Classical methods are portrayed as fundamentally limited in several domains: solving the Schrödinger equation is described as infeasible with current classical computers, and breaking RSA with classical supercomputers is said to require over 10^30 years. In contrast, the authors describe quantum approaches that allegedly offer speedups, such as Grover’s algorithm versus classical search, Shor’s algorithm versus classical factoring, and a cited quantum Monte Carlo derivative pricing algorithm with O(√N) runtime compared to classical O(N).
    [codes: C-015, C-017, C-022, C-028]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The chapter explicitly highlights qubit decoherence and information retention as the central technical obstacles, noting that qubit coherence loss corrupts information and that retaining quantum information remains the biggest challenge. It stresses that maintaining quantum states often requires extremely controlled environments at temperatures around 0.02 Kelvin. Adiabatic quantum computers are said to suffer from decoherence and to lack error‑correction infrastructure, and D‑Wave’s systems are described as controversial, with uncertainty over whether they perform genuinely quantum calculations. The authors also note that current classical computers cannot handle certain quantum‑mechanical calculations, such as Schrödinger’s equation, which they frame as a limitation of the status quo motivating quantum approaches. Finally, blockchain is portrayed as not safe against quantum attacks under existing schemes.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-010, C-022, C-031, C-036, C-038]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future directions center on more capable quantum architectures and applications: universal quantum computers are described as holding greater long‑term promise, and a hybrid adiabatic–digital design is proposed that might deliver notable speedups and be testable in industry within about five years. On the application side, the chapter points to quantum simulation for improved drug discovery, radiology treatment optimization, and more energy‑efficient materials production (e.g., ammonia synthesis). It also anticipates the development of quantum‑resistant cryptographic parameters to secure blockchain and highlights quantum supremacy as a still‑distant milestone, with Google’s 72‑qubit Bristlecone chip cautiously cited as a possible step toward that goal. Weather prediction and other complex systems modeling are similarly framed as future quantum use cases.
    [codes: C-011, C-013, C-014, C-023, C-024, C-026, C-032, C-034, C-035, C-039]

    [codes: C-015, C-016, C-019, C-020, C-028, C-037, C-038, C-039]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The chapter situates quantum computing within a broad ecosystem of applications beyond finance, including AI, biochemistry, radiology, materials production, security, blockchain, and weather forecasting. Overall, it underscores the cross‑cutting importance of optimization and AI linkages when considering quantum technologies in finance and other sectors.

=== PAPER 64beee021f17 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames quantum machine learning as a way to search for potential quantum advantage in realistic classification tasks from finance and vision, focusing on high-dimensional, often imbalanced industrial/financial data such as credit-scoring and MNIST images. It specifically targets non-trivial datasets with many features, many observations, and sometimes unbalanced targets that are challenging for NISQ hardware. The work is conducted under an explicit NISQ-era constraint, motivating architectures that can operate with few qubits while still addressing these practical data characteristics.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-007, C-006]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The core method is a hybrid quantum–classical network that 'squeezes' a variational quantum circuit between a classical front-end and back-end, allowing the classical network to reduce feature dimensionality before quantum encoding so that the number of qubits can be chosen independently of the number of input features and output classes. The authors deliberately adopt a straightforward design for the encoding/variational circuit and distinguish between two training/architecture variants (with vs. without data re-uploading) while using TensorFlow Quantum as the software stack to enable seamless joint training of classical and quantum components. Classical preprocessing networks map high-dimensional data (e.g., many finance features or 784 MNIST pixels) to a smaller set of parameters suitable for angle encoding on a limited number of qubits, and a classical output layer performs final classification.
    [codes: C-036, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-013, C-016, C-017, C-030, C-031, C-019]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Across finance and MNIST experiments, the authors report that the hybrid network achieves an advantage over a purely classical neural network when considering several performance measures, including accuracy and AUC. On the balanced credit-scoring sample, the hybrid approach shows better performance than the classical benchmark, and data re-uploading is reported to be superior to repeating only the variational part of the circuit. On the unbalanced finance sample, differences between quantum variants that are not visible in accuracy emerge in AUC. For MNIST, the QNet is said to learn substantially faster than the classical NNet, and its confusion matrix indicates that it can separate all ten handwritten digits, though the classical model displays less overfitting. The authors also note a practical result that, using TensorFlow Quantum, relatively large circuits (e.g., 10 qubits with 10 layers) can still be simulated on a notebook.
    [codes: C-003, C-024, C-025, C-028, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-018]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study explicitly benchmarks the hybrid QNet against a fully classical neural network constructed with a comparable number of trainable parameters, using both accuracy and AUC as evaluation metrics. On the balanced finance sample, the hybrid model outperforms this classical baseline, and in the unbalanced sample, the authors stress that while accuracy alone appears similar, AUC reveals differences between approaches. On MNIST, the hybrid network is reported to converge faster in training and to reach high training accuracy, but the classical network exhibits less overfitting when comparing train and test performance, suggesting that any hybrid advantage is nuanced and metric-dependent.
    [codes: C-015, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-028, C-032, C-035, C-027]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several limitations. Conceptually, they stress that overfitting remains a concern in quantum machine learning just as in classical settings, and they observe overfitting empirically both on the finance data (when applying trained networks to unseen cases) and on MNIST through a train–test accuracy gap, with the classical benchmark showing less overfitting. They also highlight metric-related limitations: accuracy can be misleading for unbalanced datasets, since a naive classifier assigning all observations to the majority class can already reach high accuracy, making AUC necessary to meaningfully differentiate models. From a scalability perspective, they state that a brute-force QML treatment of MNIST with all 784 pixel features is hardly possible, necessitating dimensionality reduction. Finally, all experiments are performed on simulators in the NISQ era, implying that device noise and hardware constraints are not yet addressed in the reported results.
    [codes: C-004, C-026, C-034, C-035, C-022, C-027, C-029, C-006]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The paper calls for further research on hybrid quantum networks in general, including the need to clarify which observed behaviors are generic properties of the hybrid architecture versus artifacts of particular datasets. The authors specifically identify regularization as an open problem, stating that methods to regularize quantum networks to avoid overfitting must be studied. They also emphasize that future work should investigate the impact of realistic error rates on physical quantum devices, since all current experiments are simulator-based.
    [codes: C-005, C-037, C-038, C-039]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors position their contribution as introducing a hybrid quantum–classical network architecture that interposes a quantum circuit between two classical networks to classify non-trivial, realistic datasets from finance and image recognition under NISQ constraints. They stress the architectural feature that the classical front-end trainably reduces feature dimensionality so that the number of qubits is decoupled from the number of input features and classes, and they systematically compare two quantum variants (with and without data re-uploading) against a parameter-matched classical baseline to explore potential performance advantages of such hybrid designs.
    [codes: C-002, C-036, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-013, C-001]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the work is grounded in the NISQ context and uses TensorFlow Quantum after comparing alternative frameworks, with the explicit rationale that it provides seamless joint training of classical and quantum components and efficient simulation even for circuits with around 10 qubits and 10 layers. The authors design a classical encoding front-end that trainably scales down high-dimensional, potentially imbalanced industrial/finance data to match limited qubit resources, and they explicitly construct classical benchmarks with matched parameter counts for fair comparison. They discuss evaluation choices in detail, arguing that accuracy is misleading for unbalanced datasets and therefore augmenting it with AUC, and they carefully construct both balanced and unbalanced finance samples to study metric behavior under class imbalance. Across experiments, they adopt a straightforward circuit design and standardized training setup (ADAM optimizer, fixed learning rate, cross-entropy loss), which aids clarity but also reflects an exploratory rather than optimization-heavy methodology.
    [codes: C-006, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-015, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-029, C-030, C-019]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None; all verified codes were integrated into the other reporting dimensions.
    [codes: —]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it directly studies credit-scoring classification under both balanced and imbalanced class settings, which is central to default prediction. It also evaluates whether a hybrid quantum-classical model can outperform a matched classical baseline using metrics that matter for lending decisions, especially AUC under class imbalance.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]

    [codes: C-015, C-024, C-003  dim_source: dimension_3]

=== PAPER 6ce12ea7c2bf (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses two machine learning problems: feature selection for credit risk assessment on the German Credit Dataset, framed as selecting a minimal but accurate subset of features and modeled as a QUBO for quantum optimisation, and handwritten digit (3 vs 6) classification on MNIST using quantum neural networks. For credit, the goal is to balance classification performance and feature sparsity in building a lightweight yet accurate scoring model; for images, the goal is to test QNNs on a challenging binary classification task motivated by the importance of image recognition in AI.
    [codes: C-001, C-007, C-008, C-015, C-035, C-013, C-014]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: For credit feature selection, the authors cast the task as a QUBO where binary variables encode feature inclusion or exclusion and use quantum annealing/Ising machines to search for low-energy solutions, with a hybrid annealing strategy that extracts and solves sub-QUBOs under specific penalty settings. For image classification, they construct a QNN pipeline that includes downsampling and binarization, FRQI and compressed FRQI quantum image encoding implemented via recursive multi-qubit gate decompositions, and two variational QNN architectures (CRADL with alternating XX/ZZ layers and CRAML with additional mixed gates) trained and evaluated via cross-validation against classical neural networks.
    [codes: C-002, C-009, C-011, C-012, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-023, C-024, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-005, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-041, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: In the credit case study, quantum-annealed QUBO feature selection yields a model that chooses only two features ('guarantor' and 'residence duration') yet attains 70% overall classification accuracy with strong performance on the majority class, which the authors interpret as a high-precision, minimal-feature credit scoring model. In the image case study, QNNs based on CRADL/CRAML and FRQI/compressed FRQI demonstrate that quantum models can effectively handle high-dimensional image data, with compressed FRQI reducing qubit usage without significant loss in performance and deeper compressed QNNs on 16×16 inputs approaching the accuracy of classical neural networks; the authors emphasize that quantum models start training more slowly but their accuracy improves over time and can close much of the performance gap. They also argue that compressed FRQI improves QNN scalability while preserving image information and that mixed gates in CRAML enhance entanglement, helping the largest QNN handle 16×16 images.
    [codes: C-003, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-040, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-058]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors note that modeling feature selection as QUBO allows solving with both classical numerical methods and quantum computing, and they state that the superiority of the QUBO approach versus other feature selection methods is dataset-dependent, without claiming a general advantage. For image classification, they explicitly compare QNNs (CRADL/CRAML) to classical neural networks, reporting that classical 2‑layer networks learn faster and reach high accuracy quickly on 8×8 images, while QNNs initially learn more slowly but eventually achieve performance comparable to classical NNs, particularly for the 42‑layer compressed QNN on 16×16 images.
    [codes: C-009, C-010, C-045, C-047, C-050, C-051, C-053]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: For the credit experiment, the authors acknowledge poor performance on the minority (high-risk) class, attributing this to severe class imbalance and the simplicity of the two-feature model. They critique existing sub-QUBO extraction methods as heuristic with no solid theoretical foundation and argue that fixed sub-QUBO selections prevent guaranteeing global optimality of the original QUBO. For the QNN image experiments, they recognize that the number of encoding gates grows exponentially with image size, justifying this only in the context of classical simulation, and admit that current quantum annealers and QNN hardware have limited qubit counts that restrict scalability to larger datasets such as full MNIST. They also highlight that computing gradients for QNNs is computationally expensive, slowing training compared to classical neural networks.
    [codes: C-025, C-026, C-034, C-042, C-055, C-056]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include improving sub-QUBO extraction strategies, potentially with parallel processing of sub-QUBOs on Ising machines, to better approximate globally optimal feature subsets and enhance scalability for larger datasets in feature selection. On the QNN side, the authors propose introducing higher-order multi-qubit gates to capture more complex image features and optimizing quantum encoding circuits to reduce gate counts and speed up training. They also plan to extend their QUBO and QNN frameworks to broader domains such as fraud detection and medical image classification, and to study how limited qubits and gates in QNNs might act as implicit regularizers that improve generalization.
    [codes: C-059, C-060, C-061, C-062, C-063]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper presents a collaborative framework combining quantum optimisation (via QUBO and quantum annealing) and quantum neural networks, using two case studies—credit feature selection and handwritten digit classification—to demonstrate quantum computing's potential utility in practical machine learning tasks, while stressing the need to balance resource usage and model efficacy. It positions QNNs with FRQI/compressed FRQI encodings and CRADL/CRAML architectures as capable of handling relatively high-dimensional image data with qubit-efficient encodings, and frames the QUBO-based feature selection as a way to derive lightweight credit scoring models that maintain reasonable precision with very few features, though without claiming formal quantum advantage.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-005, C-006, C-011, C-012, C-014, C-040, C-051, C-052, C-057, C-058]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the credit experiment uses a well-known public benchmark (German Credit Dataset), with clear feature preprocessing (one-hot encoding of categorical fields, standardization, and Random Forest importance filtering) and explicit QUBO parameter settings; performance is evaluated via a logistic regression classifier with reported accuracy and per-class metrics. The image experiments use a standard MNIST subset (digits 3 and 6), detail their preprocessing (downsampling, normalization, binarization), quantum encodings (FRQI and compressed FRQI with explicit gate-construction strategies), and QNN architectures (CRADL/CRAML), and adopt 10-fold cross-validation with hinge loss while qualitatively comparing learning dynamics between quantum and classical models. The authors also explicitly discuss theoretical underpinnings such as mapping NP problems to Ising models and reflect on the heuristic nature and lack of theory for some sub-QUBO extraction methods, indicating some awareness of methodological limitations.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-015, C-016, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-022, C-024, C-025, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-041, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Some codes elaborate conceptual motivations and interpretations that cut across the other dimensions, such as viewing quantum computing as providing new acceleration mechanisms for combinatorial optimisation through superposition, entanglement, and tunneling, and offering domain interpretations of the selected credit features (guarantor as secondary repayment source; longer residence as stability proxy). The authors also reiterate a broader perspective that quantum computing can be a useful tool for practical machine learning, provided resource expenditures are balanced against model performance.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-013, C-031, C-032, C-057]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to credit_lending because it studies credit risk feature selection on the German Credit Dataset using a QUBO/quantum annealing approach. Its main value for the silo is showing how a quantum optimization workflow can produce a very small feature set for credit scoring, while also exposing limitations around class imbalance and heuristic sub-QUBO extraction.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]

    [codes: C-031, C-032  dim_source: dimension_9]

=== PAPER 6fe2aeb67e66 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames quantum computing as a near‑ to medium‑term tool for improving hard problems in finance—especially portfolio optimization, pricing and risk simulations, and selected ML applications—while simultaneously addressing urgent security risks from potential quantum attacks on cryptography. It emphasizes that quantum will not remake finance overnight but that firms should already be preparing through pilots and starting migration to post‑quantum cryptography to mitigate harvest‑now, decrypt‑later risk.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-006, C-012, C-013, C-022, C-023, C-030]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors focus on hybrid quantum–classical approaches, particularly variational quantum algorithms such as QAOA and VQE for constrained portfolio and discrete trading/hedging optimization, quantum‑style Monte Carlo methods for pricing, risk, and stress testing, and cautious early quantum ML where data encoding is tractable. They highlight VQE’s role in mapping some portfolio choices into quantum form and recommend a vendor‑agnostic stack with emulation/HPC plus orchestration of HPC and quantum access to support these methods.
    [codes: C-002, C-007, C-013, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-026, C-029, C-037, C-018]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: As a conceptual/practitioner brief, the paper does not report new quantitative experimental results; instead, it lays out expected benefit patterns. It suggests that near‑term quantum methods may sharpen portfolio optimization and scenario analysis for CIOs and portfolio managers and could yield faster, more reliable risk metrics for CROs and MRM teams, assuming strong governance and appropriate benchmarking.
    [codes: C-006, C-025, C-027]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper insists that any quantum pilots be benchmarked rigorously against strong classical baselines, comparing solution quality, constraint feasibility, and wall‑clock time end‑to‑end. For portfolio optimization, quantum hybrids (QAOA/VQE) should be tested on real portfolios against the firm’s best classical tools, and for quantum‑style Monte Carlo the number of samples, accuracy, and total time including setup must be compared to classical Monte Carlo.
    [codes: C-004, C-015, C-019, C-020, C-038]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors emphasize that fully reliable, large‑scale, fault‑tolerant quantum computers remain a distant aspiration and that the current NISQ era features small, error‑prone chips which favor short circuits and hybrid methods; fault tolerance is explicitly described as years away. They stress that current value is limited to small‑scale techniques on noisy devices, that noisy outputs require tight governance, and that speed improvements are meaningless if optimization constraints are violated or returns deteriorate. They also caution that quantum ML must proceed carefully because the cost of loading data can offset any accuracy gains, and they note that quantum key distribution, while powerful, is niche due to cost and infrastructure constraints.
    [codes: C-004, C-008, C-014, C-021, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-039]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future‑oriented directions include assessing quantum Monte Carlo for liquidity and interest‑rate stress tests by comparing runtime and accuracy, and funding small hybrid pilots now as a way to buy option value for future scaling as hardware improves. The paper also implicitly points to ongoing benchmarking of VQAs, QML, and quantum Monte Carlo as a continuing research agenda for heads of quant and researchers.
    [codes: C-026, C-030, C-032]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The brief positions its contribution as a practitioner‑oriented roadmap rather than a technical breakthrough: it offers a clear reality check on what NISQ‑era hardware can and cannot do, an actionable roadmap for small hybrid pilots in optimization, simulations, and ML, a security playbook for starting post‑quantum cryptography and handling harvest‑now/decrypt‑later risk, and concrete role‑based guidance for CIOs, CROs, CISOs, CTOs, quants, and strategy owners on how to design stacks, benchmark algorithms, and plan ROI‑oriented pilots.
    [codes: C-001, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-010, C-011, C-014, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the authors prescribe disciplined experimental and governance practices rather than report their own experiments. They call for side‑by‑side A/B tests against strong classical baselines, end‑to‑end wall‑clock measurements that include encoding, transpilation, and error‑mitigation overhead, and tracking of solution quality, constraint violations, P&L/risk impacts, and accuracy. Robustness should be checked via variance across shots and random seeds and stability under noise and hyperparameter changes, with pipelines kept reproducible and error‑mitigation steps logged. They emphasize small, clean datasets for QML experiments, clear validation expectations and data‑pipeline requirements so model risk and audit can sign off, and the use of a vendor‑agnostic stack with emulation/HPC plus orchestrated HPC/quantum access, overseen by a small cross‑functional team.
    [codes: C-004, C-015, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-024, C-029, C-031, C-038, C-039, C-040]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Several cross‑cutting strategic and security points do not fit neatly into the other dimensions: the paper stresses that beginning a shift to post‑quantum cryptography is a primary security objective because PQC schemes are designed to resist quantum attacks, and that firms should audit encryption, prioritize data at risk of harvest‑now/decrypt‑later exposure, and align migration and governance controls with NIST’s PQC timelines. It also underlines that QKD is powerful but should be reserved for ultra‑secure, niche links, and that early capability‑building via pilots has strategic option value for organizations.
    [codes: C-003, C-010, C-012, C-016, C-023, C-028, C-031, C-032, C-035, C-036]

=== PAPER 71966c8a9dc7 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses how big data analytics can transform financial risk management by enabling faster, more accurate, and more proactive management of market, credit, liquidity, fraud, and operational risks, compared with traditional slow, reactive, and internally focused approaches. It frames the core problem as leveraging diverse, high‑velocity data and machine learning to move from reactive, report-based risk management to real-time, data-driven prediction and prevention of risks, while supporting regulatory requirements. This problem is not formulated for quantum computation; quantum is only mentioned as a possible future accelerator for optimization and model training.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-014, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-028, C-029, C-038, C-039, C-059, C-060, C-016]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The paper does not implement or specify any concrete quantum algorithm; it only speculates that quantum computing applied to optimization and mathematical problems may in future speed up tasks such as portfolio optimization, insurance pricing, and training large machine learning models in risk management. No details on quantum circuits, hybrid schemes, hardware, or complexity are provided.
    [codes: C-005, C-054]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The main reported results are conceptual and case-based: the authors argue that big data analytics and machine learning make risk management faster, more accurate, and more proactive across market, credit, liquidity, and fraud risks, shifting institutions from reactive to data-driven approaches and improving real-time detection and prevention. They highlight quantified industry case studies, including a real-time fraud detection system at an African digital bank that blocked fraud within two seconds, saving $4.5 million and reducing complaints by 65%, and a global firm’s real-time liquidity prediction system that anticipated liquidity issues 48 hours ahead and saved $12 million during a crisis. Overall, the study concludes that big data is changing how financial risks are managed, enabling rapid fraud detection, deeper insights, personalized risk profiling, improved pricing, and enhanced regulatory compliance.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-009, C-014, C-015, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-039, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-059, C-060]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper contrasts traditional risk assessment methods—based on slow, batch processing of internal structured data and assumptions that the future resembles the past—with big-data-driven approaches that ingest large, fast, and diverse internal and external datasets, including unstructured sources such as social media and geospatial data. It notes that classical models like logistic regression remain important, interpretable benchmarks for credit scoring and regulatory compliance but have difficulty capturing complex nonlinear relationships, whereas machine learning methods (decision trees and ensembles, neural networks, XGBoost, LightGBM, deep learning for fraud) exploit richer data and complex patterns to improve accuracy, default prediction, and fraud detection, albeit with interpretability trade-offs.
    [codes: C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors emphasize multiple limitations and implementation challenges: data quality and integration issues can generate false alerts or missed risk signals, and data drift over time can degrade machine learning models if not monitored and retrained. They stress that complex, black-box models like deep neural networks are difficult to interpret, complicating regulatory compliance—especially where regulations such as GDPR give individuals rights to explanations for automated credit or fraud decisions. Cybersecurity and data privacy requirements, including heavy encryption, can slow real-time systems and hurt decision latency. Organizationally, there is a serious shortage of skilled professionals in data science, AI, and risk analytics, and many institutions face outdated legacy systems that struggle with real-time analysis. The paper also flags legal and ethical concerns around the use of alternative data (privacy, consent, provenance, bias), and more generally notes that big data risk management must balance innovation with governance, transparency, ethics, and robust controls to avoid financial and reputational harm.
    [codes: C-004, C-034, C-036, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-058, C-061, C-063]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The future directions discussed center on emerging technologies and evolving practices rather than concrete research roadmaps: the authors propose that quantum computing could in future accelerate optimization, pricing, and large-scale model training; blockchain and distributed ledgers could strengthen traceability and auditability of risk data and model decisions; and explainable AI (e.g., SHAP, LIME, rule-based and additive models) will play a growing role in making complex models more transparent, fair, and error-resistant. They also foresee an increasing role for alternative and non-traditional data sources—such as satellite imagery, web and location data, and ESG scores—for fast risk and investment signals, while acknowledging the need for stronger legal, ethical, and governance frameworks around their use.
    [codes: C-005, C-054, C-055, C-056, C-057]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions its contribution as a synthetic review and integrative analysis of how big data, machine learning, and real-time analytics are transforming financial risk management across multiple risk types, data sources, and use cases, combined with a discussion of implementation challenges and governance issues. It claims to show that big data shifts risk management from reactive to proactive, real-time, and data-driven practices, backed by concrete industry case studies quantifying the impact of real-time fraud detection and liquidity prediction. Additionally, it highlights as a contribution the forward-looking integration of trends such as quantum computing, blockchain, explainable AI, and alternative data into the future landscape of financial risk management, and argues for a balanced approach that couples innovation with strong governance, transparency, ethics, and regulatory compliance.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-014, C-019, C-020, C-039, C-046, C-047, C-059, C-060, C-005, C-054, C-055, C-056, C-057, C-004, C-061, C-063]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the work is a conceptual and review-style study rather than an empirical quantum or algorithmic implementation: it synthesizes prior literature and industry reports on big data, machine learning model classes, data sources, and real-time architectures for financial risk management. The paper discusses a typical real-time system architecture with ingestion (Kafka/Kinesis), stream processing (Spark Streaming/Flink), storage, and analytics/ML layers, and cites case studies with headline savings figures, but does not provide experimental protocols, datasets, or reproducible benchmarks for the reported systems. Its quantitative elements come from secondary case-study reports rather than controlled experiments; issues of dataset quality, data drift, and integration are acknowledged conceptually rather than addressed via empirical validation.
    [codes: C-018, C-023, C-031, C-037, C-038, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-063]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper also provides definitional and contextual material: it defines big data in finance via the 4Vs (volume, velocity, variety, veracity) and as encompassing market, customer, social media, and economic data; clarifies financial risk as the possibility that actual outcomes deviate from expected ones, leading to loss; outlines key risk categories (market, credit, liquidity); and emphasizes the centrality of risk management for asset protection and systemic stability. It notes that the 2008 financial crisis spurred stricter regulatory standards such as Basel III, and that big data can support regulatory reporting (e.g., Basel III, MiFID II, GDPR) by enabling storage, auditability, and reporting. It also discusses the use of social media/news for sentiment and rumor detection, transaction logs for fraud and AML risk, and alternative data for forecasting economic activity, as well as the broader impact of big data on resilience and efficiency in finance.

=== PAPER 71dc25d5ac6a (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses the problem of estimating financial crashes and distress propagation in a network of small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) that are customers of a Turkish bank, motivated by the need for accurate and timely crash estimation to preserve stability and mitigate risk. It formulates this as a nonlinear, quadratically constrained quadratic optimization problem on an SME network, capturing domino-style contagion effects and multiple equilibrium points in firms’ market values and failures.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-005, C-011, C-035, C-036, C-048, C-050]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors use D-Wave’s hybrid constrained quadratic model (CQM) solvers to tackle the QCQP formulation, arguing that CQM is a good fit due to constraints, high complexity, and integer decision variables. Quantum computing is used within this hybrid framework to explore the solution space and obtain decision variables indicating firms’ likelihood of financial distress, with repeated runs necessitated by randomness in the quantum processing mechanism and the presence of multiple equilibrium points.
    [codes: C-004, C-020, C-022, C-030, C-031, C-034, C-038, C-042]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On 2019 financial data, the model identifies 90.04 million TL of non-performing loans out of 107 million TL and reports an overall prediction accuracy of about 91.2%, with quarterly accuracy figures also reported. Using D-Wave’s hybrid solver, the authors state they can obtain a feasible solution in roughly 7 seconds of solve time, and they present concrete examples of failure propagation between firms in the network, such as firm D’s failure increasing its own and another firm’s unpaid debt.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-010, C-039, C-045, C-049]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper claims that classical computing solutions to this type of crash-estimation problem would be computationally infeasible, citing an estimate of 13.7 billion years of runtime for classical methods on similar problems. In contrast, the hybrid quantum approach reportedly solves the formulated optimization problem in seconds, though the extreme classical runtime figure is drawn from prior literature rather than measured directly in this study.
    [codes: C-006, C-012, C-019, C-039]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several limitations: inadequate and incomplete financial information and inter-firm relations in the available data constrain the quality and feasibility of the constructed networks and require post-processing of equilibrium points. The hybrid solver exhibits sensitivity to solve time: shorter-than-recommended times yield infeasible solutions with constraint violations, while longer runtimes do not improve results beyond obtaining feasible solutions. They also note the stochasticity of the quantum processing mechanism and multiple equilibrium points, necessitating repeated runs, and argue that standard machine learning metrics like precision and recall are not meaningful for evaluating their model, which restricts the evaluation toolkit.
    [codes: C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-050, C-051]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include improving data quality by more frequent and complete collection of companies’ trial balances, expanding the network to include large companies alongside SMEs for more realistic projections, and enhancing the statistical modeling by involving domain experts and incorporating more diverse financial documents beyond trial balances.
    [codes: C-053, C-054, C-055]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors position their work as a pioneering, interdisciplinary demonstration of the applicability of quantum computing to one of the most challenging problems in finance—predicting financial crashes in complex SME networks—and suggest that combining quantum optimization with financial network modeling has the potential to improve the robustness and reliability of crash predictions for practitioners and policymakers.
    [codes: C-013, C-015, C-016]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper constructs an SME network from proprietary bank financial records, selecting nodes based on database analysis and classifying customers into three risk types (including NPL and watchlist categories) defined by payment delay thresholds. It introduces detailed model parameters such as crossholding ratios and self-holdings, binary failure indicators, and integer-restricted market values, with an equilibrium-based objective and constraints linking failures to value thresholds. For evaluation, the authors reject classical ML metrics in favor of real unpaid debt as the primary metric, focusing on end-of-year quarters because they provide more comprehensive financial statements and validating model predictions against unpaid debts observed in subsequent quarters of 2020. They also use a dependency matrix to visualize and interpret cascade effects, and repeat each solver configuration multiple times due to stochasticity and multiple equilibria.
    [codes: C-024, C-025, C-026, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047, C-048]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The related-work discussion emphasizes prior findings on how integration and diversification interact in financial networks, how small investment changes can affect overall stability, and that estimating post-shock failures is computationally intractable even with full information. The authors also cite earlier proposals and experiments using quantum annealers for cascades and other finance tasks such as portfolio optimization, arbitrage detection, credit scoring, and Monte Carlo, positioning their work within a broader emerging field of quantum finance.
    [codes: C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it models default/distress propagation across borrower networks and evaluates predictions against unpaid debt, which is directly aligned with default prediction and credit risk monitoring. Its strongest value for the silo is the network-based treatment of borrower interdependence and the use of real loan-loss outcomes rather than generic classification metrics.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Network-based distress prediction can support borrower default monitoring when firms are linked through financial dependencies.
    [codes: C-001, C-005, C-048, C-049  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The model uses unpaid debt / non-performing loan outcomes as the evaluation target, which is closer to credit risk than standard ML classification metrics.
    [codes: C-008, C-010, C-044, C-043  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 3: Borrower risk categories are defined using payment-delay thresholds, making the setup directly interpretable as a credit-risk segmentation problem.
    [codes: C-025, C-026  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 4: The paper’s network construction from bank customer records and inter-firm ownership/holding parameters is relevant to lender-side portfolio analysis.
    [codes: C-024, C-032, C-033  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 5: The reported limitations around incomplete financial information and the need for repeated runs matter for deployment in lending settings.
