=== PAPER 980d054544f5 (type: central) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses bank credit risk management by optimizing credit scoring card thresholds to balance approval volume, default risk, and resulting income. It formulates two QUBO optimization problems: selecting a single credit scoring card and threshold, and selecting a three-card threshold combination, both aiming to maximize final bank revenue as a function of interest income and bad-debt losses, subject to implicit pass-rate / bad-debt trade-offs.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-007, C-008, C-015, C-021, C-022, C-024]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors formulate the credit scoring card optimization as QUBO problems that can, in principle, run on quantum hardware and claim millisecond-level acceleration and more efficient optimization via quantum computing. The single-card QUBO is solved with a classical simulated annealing algorithm, while the three-card combination QUBO is solved using a D-Wave quantum annealing algorithm (and pyqubo to simulate the annealing process), with constraints embedded via quadratic penalty terms and variable substitutions to obtain an unconstrained QUBO Hamiltonian.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-009, C-010, C-016, C-022, C-023, C-026, C-027, C-029, C-030, C-031]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: For the single-card problem, the simulated annealing QUBO solution yields a minimum-energy configuration corresponding to a maximum bank revenue of 61,172 CNY, identifying the 49th card at threshold level 1 as optimal. For the three-card combination problem, the quantum-annealing-based QUBO solution identifies a specific threshold configuration (including the eighth threshold for the first card) that maximizes bank revenue at 27,914.8 CNY. The authors also report that during annealing the objective energy generally decreases, and empirically find a strong positive correlation (0.71) between pass rate and bad-debt rate with similar trend dynamics and specified value ranges.
    [codes: C-012, C-013, C-014, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper notes that prior work in credit scoring card optimization mainly employs machine learning methods but does not provide any direct empirical comparison against such classical approaches. For the single-card QUBO, the authors explicitly treat it as a small-scale optimization solvable by typical classical algorithms like simulated annealing, but they do not benchmark quantum annealing against classical baselines or report any speedup measurements.
    [codes: C-004, C-009, C-017]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: A key modeling limitation acknowledged is the need to choose a sufficiently large penalty parameter P in the quadratic penalty function to ensure that encoded constraints (such as one-threshold-per-card and auxiliary-variable consistency) are properly enforced in the unconstrained QUBO; the authors explicitly state that P must be large enough for the penalty term to be effectively equivalent to the original constraints and that the problem becomes unconstrained only under this condition.
    [codes: C-028, C-029, C-027]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Not addressed in this paper.
    [codes: —]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors position their main contribution as establishing suitable QUBO models that convert bank credit scoring card threshold optimization—both for a single card and for a three-card combination—into forms solvable by annealing-based algorithms, and then using these formulations to determine the card/threshold configurations that maximize bank income. They present the explicit development of a single-card QUBO solved via simulated annealing and a multi-card QUBO solved via D-Wave quantum annealing as core contributions beyond prior machine-learning-based approaches.
    [codes: C-004, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper begins with data preprocessing and binary coding to map card thresholds into binary variables before expressing the objective in matrix QUBO form. For the three-card problem, it assumes simplified rules for aggregating pass rates (multiplication) and bad-debt rates (averaging) across cards and enforces the constraint that only one threshold is chosen per card via binary one-hot encodings and quadratic penalty functions. It further notes that because the single-card case is small scale, standard optimization algorithms can be used, while the combination case is formulated with cubic-term decomposition and penalty-based constraint handling to fit the QUBO framework. The authors also qualitatively describe quantum annealing as exploring all possible inputs in parallel and use pyqubo to simulate this process, but they do not discuss code release or detailed reproducibility aspects beyond these modeling choices.
    [codes: C-011, C-016, C-017, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Several contextual elements complement the main analysis: the authors empirically observe that pass rates mainly fluctuate between 0.7–0.9 while bad-debt rates range from 0–0.15, and that these series share similar trends; they reiterate in the conclusions the specific single-card (49th card, threshold 1) and three-card threshold combinations found to maximize bank income. They also emphasize that combining several different credit scoring cards is often operationally necessary for optimal risk control strategy in practice.
    [codes: C-013, C-014, C-020, C-021, C-032, C-034, C-035]

=== PAPER b0f18b08964e (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames the problem as improving financial risk management in increasingly complex and data‑intensive markets, where traditional, model‑based and statistical risk techniques struggle with scale, high‑dimensional optimization, real‑time analysis, and simplifying assumptions that can distort risk estimates. It positions quantum computing as a theoretically transformative tool to address these computational and modeling limitations across portfolio risk, stress testing, derivative valuation, and credit risk assessment.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-005, C-018, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: As a theoretical review, the paper discusses several families of quantum approaches rather than implementing a specific algorithm: quantum Monte Carlo methods for more efficient simulations of complex financial systems, quantum annealing for combinatorial and portfolio optimization, and quantum machine learning for pattern recognition and classification in large risk datasets. It also notes the foundational role of qubits, superposition, and entanglement for parallel computation, and highlights quantum‑resistant cryptography as a related security application.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-007, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-017, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-039]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The paper does not report empirical or quantitative results; instead it advances theoretical implications. It argues that, in principle, quantum computing can offer exponential speed‑ups for certain financial risk management problems, improve computational efficiency and scalability, enable more efficient portfolio optimization and stress testing, and allow more accurate derivative pricing and credit risk modeling. It also notes recent advances in hardware (more qubits, better coherence) as evidence of progressing feasibility, while treating all performance gains as prospective rather than demonstrated within this study.
    [codes: C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-049]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors contrast quantum approaches conceptually with classical methods, claiming that classical computing struggles with vast data volumes, complex and high‑dimensional optimization, and real‑time risk analysis, and that traditional models may be biased due to simplifying assumptions. In contrast, they suggest that quantum computers—via superposition, entanglement, and parallelism—could solve some of these tasks exponentially faster, scale more effectively, explore many portfolio configurations simultaneously, and simulate complex financial systems more efficiently than classical Monte Carlo and optimization algorithms.
    [codes: C-005, C-011, C-013, C-022, C-023, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-033, C-034, C-036, C-049]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges multiple limitations and barriers. On the classical side, it notes scalability issues with large datasets, difficulty handling high‑dimensional and nonlinear optimization, constraints on real‑time analysis, and potential bias from simplifying model assumptions. On the quantum side, it recognises current technical challenges in scalability, qubit stability and coherence, error rates, and the immaturity of hardware. It also highlights broader adoption barriers: a shortage of professionals who combine quantum and finance expertise, evolving and uncertain regulation around market stability, privacy, and cybersecurity, and high costs and limited accessibility of quantum infrastructure. The authors repeatedly stress that these technical, regulatory, and resource challenges must be overcome before quantum computing can be widely adopted in financial risk management.
    [codes: C-009, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-050]

They also emphasise the need for interdisciplinary collaboration between quantum computing and finance communities, as well as the design of curricula and training programs that bridge quantum physics and financial mathematics to alleviate the skills shortage.

  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions itself as a theoretical review that synthesises the fundamentals of quantum computing with core domains of financial risk management, arguing that quantum computing is potentially transformative for risk assessment and mitigation strategies across portfolio, derivative, credit, and stress‑testing contexts. Its contribution is primarily conceptual: articulating how quantum uncertainty and quantum algorithms could reshape risk paradigms and security considerations in financial markets, while systematically outlining associated challenges and implications.

  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The work is explicitly theoretical and review‑oriented, with no original empirical implementation, datasets, or quantitative evaluation of algorithms; consequently, issues of experimental design, reproducibility, and data quality are not addressed. The discussion of quantum hardware progress and algorithmic potential is descriptive and citation‑based rather than based on experiments conducted by the authors.
    [codes: C-003, C-015, C-016, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper provides foundational explanations of core quantum principles—qubits, superposition, entanglement, and error correction—and standard classifications and definitions of financial risk types (market, credit, operational), to orient readers who may be unfamiliar with these domains. It also discusses how quantum computing introduces a new paradigm of uncertainty and raises both opportunities and threats for security, including quantum‑resistant cryptography and broader considerations of cryptography and data privacy in financial systems.

=== PAPER b524b2fc9631 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper tackles the problem of estimating full loan-level loss/risk distributions, rather than just point estimates, to enable true account-level pricing and decisioning in retail lending. It frames this within a competing-risks cash-flow and survival modelling setup that incorporates multiple sources of uncertainty (including lifecycle and macro-environment effects) to obtain a probability distribution for individual-loan default and attrition outcomes.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-007, C-016, C-017, C-019]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors consider quantum Monte Carlo methods based on quantum amplitude estimation (QAE) to estimate loan-level tail risk more sample-efficiently than classical Monte Carlo, using amplitude encoding of probability density functions into qubit registers. They explain how distributions are encoded via binary representations in qubit superposition, rely on entanglement to reuse sampled values across calculations, and decompose the quantum algorithm into three components while referring to existing quantum Monte Carlo / QAE work for acceleration of risk-estimation tasks.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-022, C-023]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Classically, Monte Carlo simulations of loan-level loss distributions for example accounts fit very well to lognormal distributions; normal fits are rejected in favour of lognormal, and once this shape is assumed, only tens of simulations are needed to estimate tail risk instead of thousands. With 50 simulations, the classical method computes an account’s risk distribution in about 1.9 seconds, which the authors note is sufficient for real-time use, and they also report empirical patterns from the APC decomposition (e.g., default peaks around the 2001 and 2009 recessions, payoff dynamics). On the quantum side, they reiterate the theoretical QAE result that the accuracy achieved with M classical Monte Carlo samples can be achieved with O(M) operations yielding O(1/M) error scaling, giving a quadratic sample-complexity improvement over the classical O(1/√M) convergence, but do not report hardware experiments.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-021, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-039]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper develops a classical Monte Carlo method as the main practical solution for estimating loan-level loss distributions and explicitly contrasts its O(1/√M) convergence with the theoretically faster O(1/M) error scaling of QAE-based quantum Monte Carlo. However, because the simulated distributions are well-approximated by lognormal, the authors show that only tens of classical simulations are required to estimate mean, variance, and tail risk, leading to runtimes of about 1.9 seconds per account and arguing that this is already fast enough for real-time use, thereby undercutting the near-term practical need for quantum speedup despite the theoretical quadratic advantage.
    [codes: C-002, C-009, C-023, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-039]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several limitations of their quantum approach and modelling assumptions. For quantum computing, they note that quantum registers cannot be copied, requiring entanglement workarounds, and that current hardware noise may render present-day results largely unreliable even as hardware improves. They emphasise that efficient loading of distributions into quantum registers is only possible when an efficient classical integration algorithm exists, and that the full competing-risks quantum formulation scales poorly in qubits (best-case 4Nm qubits and 4N registers), far exceeding likely commercial qubit counts in the next few years. They explicitly state that the full competing-risks approach will require additional quantum algorithm development to become feasible, and that including a full yield model and additional cash-flow components may break the lognormal or beta distribution fit, undermining both the classical and proposed quantum shortcuts that assume a simple closed-form distribution for the final loss distribution.
    [codes: C-005, C-012, C-033, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-040]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The paper suggests that if quantum computing can make some of the estimation processes fast enough for real-time use, it may change lending practices, but this is framed as a longer-term possibility. As a nearer-term direction, the authors propose focusing on simplified, single-risk models with unconditional distributions as the likely initial application of quantum computing to account-level decisioning. They also suggest exploring quantum algorithms that exploit assumed closed-form distribution families (e.g., lognormal or generic beta) for the final loss distribution, which would allow QAE to estimate parameters rather than tail points directly, potentially reducing quantum resource requirements for more realistic competing-risks settings.
    [codes: C-010, C-032, C-038]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors present a combined classical–quantum analysis of loan-level risk-distribution estimation: they develop and empirically validate a classical Monte Carlo framework for competing-risks loan-level loss distributions, including demonstrating robust lognormal fits that dramatically reduce required simulations, and they map this problem to quantum Monte Carlo/QAE, outlining how distributions and survival components can be encoded in qubit registers and entangled for reuse. Their contribution is positioned as identifying where quantum amplitude estimation could in principle offer a quadratic sample-complexity advantage for loan-level tail-risk estimation, while also showing that, under current modelling assumptions and lognormal fit, classical Monte Carlo already delivers real-time performance.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-004, C-009, C-014, C-016, C-018, C-020, C-022, C-023, C-028, C-029, C-031, C-039]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study is grounded in a real-world public dataset from Freddie Mac, focusing on 30-year fixed-rate conforming mortgages with rich account-level variables, and it constructs a multihorizon competing-risks survival framework based on APC decomposition of lifecycle and environment components. The authors estimate separate PD and PA models via panel logistic regression at each horizon, propagate uncertainty by including 95% confidence intervals on coefficients and environment functions, and model environmental dynamics using a second-order mean-reverting Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process. Classical Monte Carlo then samples conditional PD and PA from these distributions, tabulates lifetime default and payoff densities, and tests distributional assumptions by comparing lognormal and normal fits, including goodness-of-fit testing and discussion of simulation counts and runtimes; no actual quantum hardware or code implementation is reported, and the quantum analysis remains theoretical.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-009, C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-031]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Not addressed in this paper
    [codes: —]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to credit_lending because it focuses on account-level default/attrition uncertainty for loan pricing and decisioning, using mortgage data and competing-risks survival models. It also evaluates whether quantum Monte Carlo/QAE could speed up loan-risk estimation, but shows that classical Monte Carlo is already fast enough under the paper’s assumptions.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Account-level uncertainty estimation is framed as necessary for true loan-level pricing and decisioning.
    [codes: C-001, C-006  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The empirical setup is built on mortgage loan data and models default and payoff as competing risks.
    [codes: C-015, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019  dim_source: dimension_8]
  KEY_POINT 3: Classical Monte Carlo produces loan-level loss distributions that fit lognormal well, making tail-risk estimation feasible with only tens of simulations.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-039  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: Quantum amplitude estimation is presented as a theoretical quadratic speedup for estimating loan-risk distributions.
    [codes: C-011, C-013, C-014, C-022, C-023  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 5: The paper also highlights near-term limits of the quantum approach for full competing-risks lending models.
    [codes: C-005, C-012, C-033, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-040  dim_source: dimension_5]

=== PAPER b9aa74e4bc0d (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper addresses credit risk analysis, specifically credit score classification to support lending decisions, by comparing classical and quantum machine learning models on a structured credit scoring dataset. Credit risk modelling is framed as a core tool for informed lending in finance, with traditional models ranging from statistical to deep learning approaches but facing challenges on complex, high-dimensional data. Quantum machine learning is proposed as a way to better capture intricate patterns and relationships that classical models might miss, potentially improving the effectiveness of credit score classification.
    [codes: C-001, C-007, C-010, C-011, C-012, C-013, C-014]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study uses Quantum Support Vector Classifiers (QSVCs) built on quantum kernels derived from different quantum feature maps, including ZZFeatureMap, PauliFeatureMap, and a combined ZZPauliMap that incorporates both single-qubit rotations and entangling ZZ interactions to capture individual and pairwise feature interactions. Quantum feature maps are described as the bridge between classical credit data and quantum algorithms, allowing encoding into a higher-dimensional Hilbert space before QSVC training. The QSVCs are trained and validated with stratified k‑fold cross-validation and hyperparameter tuning via grid search to optimize performance, and model decisions are later probed using LIME for interpretability.
    [codes: C-005, C-015, C-016, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-032, C-039]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The classical SVC baseline achieves around 79% accuracy on the credit scoring task before cross-validation and hyperparameter tuning. Initial QSVC models with individual feature maps perform lower, but when using the hybrid ZZPauliMap together with tuning, the QSVC test accuracy is reported as 78.7% and then 84.8%, with the hybrid map outperforming the individual ZZFeatureMap and PauliFeatureMap. The authors conclude that integrating KPCA with advanced combinational quantum feature maps significantly enhances the model’s ability to capture intricate patterns, and that quantum machine learning shows promising potential in financial applications with quantum models delivering performance competitive with SVCs.
    [codes: C-017, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-040, C-046, C-047]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The classical benchmark is a Support Vector Classifier (SVC) trained on a train–test split, achieving approximately 79% accuracy before cross-validation and hyperparameter tuning, and around 78.7% after these procedures. QSVCs using individual feature maps initially underperform this baseline, but the hybrid ZZPauliMap-based QSVC is reported to reach up to 84.8% accuracy, surpassing the tuned classical SVC on this dataset. Overall, the authors state that quantum models can be at least competitive with traditional SVCs in credit score classification, and in the best configuration may yield higher accuracy.
    [codes: C-004, C-008, C-017, C-031, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-040, C-047]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors emphasize that current quantum hardware is still nascent, with limited qubit coherence times, high error rates, and scalability challenges, which constrain practical deployment of QSVC-based credit risk models. They also note that hybrid quantum–classical integration introduces additional computational burden, especially problematic for latency-sensitive applications like high-frequency trading or real-time credit scoring. Furthermore, they highlight the substantial computational overhead associated with tuning complex quantum feature maps such as ZZPauliMap, implying significant resource requirements. Because of these factors, they argue that financial institutions must carefully evaluate the cost–benefit trade-off before adopting quantum machine learning models in production.
    [codes: C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work is directed toward exploring a broader range of quantum learning algorithms beyond QSVCs, including Quantum Neural Networks and Quantum Bayesian Networks for credit risk modelling. The authors also state that addressing scalability challenges and optimizing quantum models for larger, more complex datasets will be a priority in subsequent research.
    [codes: C-048, C-049, C-050]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The contribution is positioned as an empirical study that integrates classical and quantum machine learning for credit score classification, combining KPCA-based dimensionality reduction with multiple quantum feature maps and QSVC, and then applying LIME to enhance explainability of the quantum model’s decisions. The work aims to advance understanding of how quantum feature maps and QSVCs can improve both performance and interpretability in credit risk analysis, demonstrating that quantum models can provide competitive classification performance within a transparent, finance-appropriate framework.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-007, C-015, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-031, C-040, C-046, C-047]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study applies a fairly standard supervised learning pipeline: categorical credit features are label encoded and numerical features standardized, with pair plots used in exploratory data analysis to inspect relationships, correlations, trends, and potential interactions and outliers. The dataset is split into training and test sets for SVC evaluation, and KPCA is employed for dimensionality reduction while preserving nonlinear structure. On the quantum side, quantum kernels are built from specified feature maps, and stratified k‑fold cross-validation with grid search is used to mitigate overfitting and tune hyperparameters, followed by LIME-based local perturbation analysis with aggregated feature importances to explain model decisions, which is emphasized as crucial in financial contexts that demand transparency. However, the paper does not provide detailed dataset split ratios, quantum resource counts, or implementation-level reproducibility artifacts such as code, which must be inferred as absent.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-040, C-041]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper reiterates that credit risk modelling underpins informed decision-making in lending and that classical SVCs are a standard choice but face performance, scalability, and high‑dimensionality challenges that motivate investigation of more expressive models. Quantum models are analytically described as operating in higher-dimensional Hilbert spaces, which is used by the authors to interpret their empirical findings.
    [codes: C-008, C-009, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-039]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to credit_lending because it studies credit score classification as a decision aid for lending, comparing classical and quantum models on a structured credit dataset. Its main value for this silo is showing whether quantum feature maps and QSVCs can match or exceed standard SVC performance while remaining interpretable enough for loan approval and default-risk decisions.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: The task is explicitly credit score classification for lending decisions, making the study directly applicable to loan approval and default-risk screening.
    [codes: C-001, C-012, C-031  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The quantum pipeline uses QSVCs with ZZFeatureMap, PauliFeatureMap, and ZZPauliMap, which is relevant because these encodings are tested as alternative classifiers for credit-risk prediction.
    [codes: C-005, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: The best reported quantum setup (ZZPauliMap with tuning) reaches 84.8% accuracy and outperforms the classical SVC baseline, suggesting potential value for credit scoring models used in lending workflows.
    [codes: C-036, C-037, C-038, C-047  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: Interpretability is emphasized through LIME, which matters for lending because credit decisions often need local explanations for individual applicants.
    [codes: C-006, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-041  dim_source: dimension_7]
  KEY_POINT 5: The paper flags practical deployment constraints—nascent quantum hardware, added computational burden, and tuning overhead—which are especially important for credit_lending systems that may need fast, scalable scoring.
    [codes: C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-050  dim_source: dimension_5]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-001, C-005, C-006, C-012, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-047, C-050

=== PAPER bbd82caa4d1f (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames the problem as classical business intelligence systems in marketing and finance struggling with the rising complexity and real‑time nature of consumer and financial market data, exceeding classical processing capabilities and motivating exploration of quantum computing for predictive analytics, segmentation, pricing and risk modeling. It highlights the absence of an integrated framework that connects academic quantum computing advances with practical behavioral and predictive business applications, and positions quantum computing as a transformative technology for business intelligence across marketing, finance, supply chains and pricing strategies.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-010, C-012, C-014, C-016, C-039, C-045]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: As a review, the paper synthesizes several quantum and hybrid methods rather than implementing a single algorithm, emphasizing QSVM, QAOA, quantum-enhanced neural networks (including GEQDNN), VQE-based models, quantum annealing with QUBO, quantum walk models, Quantum NLP, QNNs, and Quantum Amplitude Estimation for Monte Carlo acceleration. It conceptually organizes these into clusters: foundational quantum technologies (e.g., Quantum Computing, QML, QSVM, Quantum NLP), customer analytics and behavior modeling, and optimization-focused methods (portfolio optimization, Monte Carlo, QUBO, supply chain).
    [codes: C-005, C-013, C-015, C-036, C-037, C-040, C-041, C-047, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-059]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: At the synthesis level, the authors report that quantum and hybrid models significantly improve efficiency and precision in marketing intelligence, sentiment analysis, portfolio optimization, risk modeling, and dynamic pricing, and they identify cross‑sector decision support gains. Cited studies on sentiment analysis show high classification performance, including RGWO‑GEQDNN achieving 96.44% and LSTM‑VQC achieving 92.59% accuracy, while quantum walk models reveal correlations between emotional responses and box‑office revenue. In financial applications, the review highlights theoretical quadratic speedup of QAE over classical Monte Carlo and reports that quantum portfolio optimization has demonstrated speedup and scalability for thousands of assets. Meta‑analytic outcome metrics indicate that quantum models tend to achieve higher accuracy (0.88–0.96 vs. 0.60–0.80 for classical methods), 30–50% reductions in training and inference time for business analytics using VQE and QSVM, and small but positive effect sizes (0.08–0.15) in favor of quantum approaches. Thematic and bibliometric analyses show rising publication trends post‑2019, dominance of sentiment analysis and QML themes, and a three‑cluster conceptual network tying foundational quantum tech, customer analytics, and optimization problems.
    [codes: C-003, C-013, C-015, C-018, C-023, C-024, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-037, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-046, C-047, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055, C-059, C-070]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: Across the reviewed studies, the authors state that quantum and hybrid methods generally outperform classical comparators in accuracy, speed, robustness, and scalability, but these are mostly reported as author claims from primary studies rather than standardized benchmarks. They emphasize that QAE offers a quadratic speedup in time complexity compared with classical Monte Carlo, that quantum amplitude estimation accelerates classical simulation and reduces error, and that quantum models can cut training and inference time by 30–50% versus classical approaches in business analytics tasks. The meta‑analytic synthesis reports higher average accuracy scores for quantum models (0.88–0.96) relative to classical methods (0.60–0.80) and small positive effect sizes (0.08–0.15), suggesting modest but consistent advantage.
    [codes: C-035, C-050, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper stresses several limitations of the evidence base and technology. At the hardware level, it notes the embryonic nature of quantum computing, where many studies rely on simulations because practical devices are limited and issues of noise and decoherence are not properly accounted for. Methodologically, the review warns of selection bias arising from dependence on a limited expert panel and regional data, as well as conceptual bias from overgeneralizing use cases without empirical testing and from not modeling real‑world noise sources. Data‑wise, most models rely on restricted, often English-only datasets that do not support multilingual or real‑time applications, which constrains external validity. For practice and deployment, the authors flag that integrating quantum computing requires significant infrastructure investment and that existing governance and regulatory frameworks are not yet adequate to address privacy, fairness, and broader ethical risks.
    [codes: C-007, C-030, C-038, C-057, C-058, C-062, C-063, C-064, C-066]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors call for future work to build multilingual and low‑resource quantum NLP models, expand dataset diversity, and validate quantum systems in live operational environments rather than simulations only. They recommend developing standardized benchmarks and validation protocols that simulate real‑world, uncontrolled conditions, including cross‑platform quantum benchmarking across different hardware providers. Strategically, they outline a phased roadmap from theory to simulated proofs of concept, cross‑sector pilots, and finally scalable business applications, with high‑urgency priorities in real‑time financial forecasting and multilingual analytics. They also propose near‑term piloting of quantum‑enhanced sentiment analysis in marketing departments and stress embedding ethical and regulatory considerations, as well as pursuing a broader strategic integration plan for global quantum business intelligence by 2030 leading to a quantum‑literate, globally integrated economy.
    [codes: C-008, C-060, C-065, C-067, C-068, C-069, C-070, C-071, C-072]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper presents itself as a PRISMA‑guided systematic literature review and meta‑analysis that integrates bibliometric analysis, thematic clustering, and strategic foresight to construct an integrated roadmap for applying quantum computing in marketing and finance. It claims novelty in demonstrating, at a synthesis level, that hybrid quantum‑classical systems and specific methods such as QSVM, QAOA, and quantum‑enhanced neural networks are emerging as primary tools for real‑time, emotionally intelligent, adaptive decision systems, and in framing quantum computing as a strategic capability and enabler of next‑generation, quantum‑resilient business intelligence rather than just a computational innovation.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-004, C-005, C-017, C-039, C-059, C-069, C-072, C-073]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the review follows PRISMA and Kitchenham’s three‑phase process, combining database searches (Google Scholar, Scopus) with defined search strings, a 2014–2025 time window, citation chaining, and explicit inclusion criteria focused on peer‑reviewed English‑language marketing‑related QC papers, ultimately narrowing 874 records to a corpus of 26 studies. It augments the SLR with bibliometric, thematic, and conceptual mapping analyses, including keyword frequency and semantic clustering, and meta‑analytic aggregation of outcome metrics and effect sizes. At the same time, the authors acknowledge potential selection and conceptual biases, limited geographic representation, reliance on simulated or small‑scale experiments due to immature hardware, and lack of standardized benchmarks, all of which constrain reproducibility and generalizability.
    [codes: C-002, C-017, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-046, C-055, C-062, C-063, C-065, C-071]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Several codes provide conceptual or contextual background beyond the core review dimensions. The paper explains the technical basis of potential speedups by noting that qubits can process multiple states in parallel, and it identifies global research leadership in quantum computing in the US, China, India, and Germany with strong private‑sector investment. It also stresses the need for robust financial regulatory and data governance frameworks, including privacy protections, as quantum capabilities advance, and reinforces a view of quantum logic as a route to more scalable predictive analytics and of QC as a strategic capability that ushers in a new, quantum‑resilient era of business intelligence.
    [codes: C-011, C-018, C-041, C-056, C-059, C-061, C-072, C-073]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it highlights quantum methods for credit scorecard optimization, faster risk estimation, and scalable predictive modeling. Its strongest value for this silo is the discussion of quantum annealing/QUBO for credit scoring, QAE for faster financial estimation, and the broader limitations around data quality, benchmarking, and deployment readiness that directly affect lending use cases.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Quantum annealing and QUBO are explicitly linked to optimizing credit scorecards.
    [codes: C-036  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 2: Quantum amplitude estimation (QAE) is presented as a way to speed up Monte Carlo-style financial estimation.
    [codes: C-015, C-035, C-051  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 3: The review reports improved accuracy, speed, and robustness for quantum models versus classical comparators.
    [codes: C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055  dim_source: dimension_4]
  KEY_POINT 4: The paper flags major deployment constraints: limited datasets, lack of real-time support, and unresolved noise/decoherence.
    [codes: C-064, C-066, C-068  dim_source: dimension_5]
  KEY_POINT 5: The future-work roadmap emphasizes standardized benchmarks, live operational validation, and real-time financial forecasting.
    [codes: C-065, C-068, C-070, C-071  dim_source: dimension_6]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-036, C-015, C-035, C-051, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055, C-064, C-066, C-068, C-065, C-070, C-071

=== PAPER c408224c2f59 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets automation of two key financial decision tasks—credit card fraud detection and loan eligibility prediction—both of which have become more challenging due to growing transaction volumes and digital financial activity. The authors frame these as supervised binary classification problems where accurate prediction is hindered by scalability, overfitting, high computational cost in classical ML models, and persistent feature selection challenges in DNN-based financial models. They motivate a quantum approach as a way to process high-dimensional financial data more efficiently while improving optimization and prediction quality.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-007, C-009, C-010, C-011]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The proposed method is a Quantum Feature Deep Neural Network (QFDNN) that combines a UU†-based feature encoding block with a variational quantum circuit (VQC). The UU† module encodes PCA-reduced financial features into quantum states by computing inner products between centroid and test states and extracting similarity from the amplitude of the |0⟩⊗n computational basis state using multiple-qubit amplitude encoding with controlled and anti-controlled U gates; this scheme is iteratively extended to higher qubits and is claimed to encode 2^N−1 features using only N qubits with logarithmic qubit scaling. The variational part consists of parameterized Ry and RZ layers (with entangling gates implied) optimized with the COBYLA optimizer using a loss defined as 1 − accuracy, and the overall architecture is explicitly evaluated under six Kraus-operator-based noise channels (bit-flip, bit-phase flip, phase flip, depolarizing, amplitude damping, phase damping).
    [codes: C-003, C-013, C-014, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the credit card fraud (CCF) dataset, QFDNN reportedly achieves an accuracy of 0.822 with near-perfect precision of 0.9524, and an ablation study shows accuracies of 0.726 for the UU† block alone and 0.786 for the variational block alone. On the loan prediction (LP) dataset, QFDNN attains a peak accuracy of 0.744 with an F1 score of 0.8247, which the authors describe as indicating a robust balance of precision and recall. The model is also tested against six noise models: it is most sensitive to bit-flip and bit-phase flip errors (accuracy dropping to around 0.366–0.372), shows near-constant accuracy under phase-flip noise, and exhibits moderate degradation under amplitude damping and depolarizing noise. The authors further claim that, compared with prior quantum models, QFDNN achieves similar tasks with fewer qubits, highlighting resource efficiency.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-016, C-017, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors benchmark QFDNN against several standard classical ML models, including logistic regression, SVM, ANN, random forest, KNN, and gradient boosting. On the CCF dataset, these classical models achieve nearly perfect scores of 1.000 across all metrics, substantially outperforming QFDNN’s reported accuracy of 0.822, albeit with QFDNN showing high precision. On the LP dataset, classical models again achieve higher accuracies (e.g., around 0.85 for LR and SVM) and F1 scores than QFDNN’s accuracy of 0.744, although the paper emphasizes QFDNN’s relatively strong balance between precision and recall. The comparison is framed primarily around accuracy and related metrics rather than runtime, and the authors additionally stress that their quantum model uses fewer qubits than other quantum baselines rather than outperforming classical models.
    [codes: C-002, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-039, C-040, C-044]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges that the chosen loss function (1 − accuracy) is effectively non-differentiable in practice, which motivates the use of the derivative-free COBYLA optimizer and implies limitations for gradient-based training strategies. The authors also explicitly state that the training process "takes comparatively more time," indicating a practical performance limitation despite the resource-efficient encoding claims. Additionally, they note that existing quantum models are vulnerable to noise, motivating their design; although they claim improved robustness, the significant performance drops observed under certain noise types further underscore that noise remains a challenge.
    [codes: C-012, C-045, C-046]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include exploring alternative optimizers such as Adam or gradient descent in conjunction with different, more suitable cost functions to overcome current optimization and training-time limitations. The authors also propose extending QFDNN to broader financial tasks, explicitly mentioning portfolio optimization and risk assessment, and suggest hardware-specific optimizations to better align the method with real quantum devices.
    [codes: C-047, C-048]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors claim to introduce a novel, resource-efficient, multi-feature and noise-resilient Quantum Feature Deep Neural Network (QFDNN) tailored for financial applications such as fraud detection and loan prediction. Central to their novelty claim are the UU†-based feature encoding combined with a variational quantum circuit that together aim to reduce overfitting and computational overhead, an exponential feature compression scheme that purportedly encodes 2^N−1 features using only N qubits with logarithmic qubit requirements, and empirical demonstrations of robustness under six different noise models. They further position QFDNN as more qubit-efficient than existing quantum models while still achieving competitive accuracies on two financial datasets.
    [codes: C-003, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-017, C-023, C-044]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses two public financial datasets (credit card fraud and loan prediction) that are preprocessed via principal component analysis to reduce dimensionality to seven features and feature scaling to ensure consistency; for the fraud dataset the 'Amount' and 'Time' features are dropped before PCA. Class imbalance is addressed using SMOTE oversampling, and both datasets are truncated to smaller, simulator-compatible sample sizes. The experimental setup trains QFDNN for 10 iterations, which the authors state is sufficient for stable convergence, and includes ablation studies that separately evaluate the UU† and variational components. Noise is modeled rigorously using Kraus operators across six standard channels. However, all experiments are conducted on a simulator (no hardware details or code availability are provided), and classical baselines are reported but without runtime comparisons.
    [codes: C-016, C-018, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-038]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper situates its work within the broader context of "social financial technology," stating an intention to enhance trust and security in financial systems by leveraging advanced automation, and it emphasizes that deep neural networks are already powerful tools for complex financial analysis, with quantum computing presented as a way to build on these capabilities. It also reiterates general claims that quantum models like quantum deep neural networks can offer faster optimization and more accurate predictions for high-dimensional financial data.
    [codes: C-006, C-008, C-010, C-011]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it directly studies loan eligibility prediction alongside a fraud task, using a quantum model aimed at improving binary decision-making under feature-selection and scalability constraints. Its most silo-relevant value is the loan-prediction result, the preprocessing/feature-compression pipeline, and the comparison against classical baselines for a lending decision setting.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: The paper directly targets loan eligibility prediction as a supervised binary classification task.
    [codes: C-001, C-007, C-039  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: The model is designed to address feature-selection and scalability issues that are especially important in lending data.
    [codes: C-002, C-009, C-013, C-014, C-023  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 3: On the loan prediction dataset, QFDNN reports a peak accuracy of 0.744 and F1 of 0.8247.
    [codes: C-004, C-016, C-039, C-040  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 4: The study benchmarks against classical models, which achieve higher loan-prediction performance than QFDNN.
    [codes: C-034, C-039, C-040  dim_source: dimension_4]
  KEY_POINT 5: The preprocessing pipeline reduces the loan dataset to seven PCA features and uses SMOTE to handle imbalance.
    [codes: C-018, C-032, C-033  dim_source: dimension_8]
  PRIMARY_CODES: C-001, C-007, C-009, C-013, C-014, C-023, C-032, C-033, C-039, C-040, C-034

=== PAPER c434305b37fb (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit risk analysis within a Gaussian Conditional-Independence (GCI) framework, focusing on loading standard Gaussian latent factors and mapping them to conditional default probabilities for a single asset. The authors concentrate on the uncertainty-model sub-circuit that is common to both VaR and CVaR estimation, encoding the conditional default probability as the chance of measuring |1⟩ on an asset qubit with rotation angles depending affinely on the latent factor. This is framed as constructing quantum circuits that can reproduce distributions relevant to credit risk models on current NISQ hardware.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-009, C-012, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The approach is a hardware-aware, variational, gate-based method that prepares discrete Gaussian distributions on small superconducting QPUs and plugs them into a GCI credit-risk sub-circuit. The Gaussian loading ansatz consists of single-qubit Ry rotations followed by entangling gates, whose parameters are variationally trained via parameter-shift gradients and Adam to match a target histogram, with analytic angle relations derived to enforce symmetric bell-shaped outputs. Implementation is tightly coupled to the Quantware Contralto-D sQPU: native CZ gates, specific qubit triplets, pulse-level schedules, and hardware-aware transpilation (including SABRE routing, subcircuit replacement, and pulse-level retuning) are used to minimize depth, respect connectivity, and tune rotation angles as device-specific hyperparameters.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-010, C-016, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-047]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The authors report that their hardware-aware variational circuits can synthesize two- and three-qubit Gaussian-like distributions whose probability amplitudes follow periodic trends with respect to rotation angles, allowing identification of optimal angle settings for specific qubit pairs and triplets. They claim that the method and hardware scale well from two to three qubits, and that analytic angle relations provide practical guidance for shaping symmetric, bell-like histograms on today’s superconducting QPUs. For the integrated GCI credit-risk sub-circuit, they find experimentally that certain Ry angles control the relative heights of the Gaussian lobes, identify an angle combination that best matches simulated output, and observe that the 95% VaR conclusion at a given loss level is consistent across simulator, hardware-ready, and experimental outputs, supporting a proof-of-concept viability claim for quantum adaptation in this financial context.
    [codes: C-005, C-008, C-021, C-022, C-029, C-030, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-039, C-040, C-042]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper primarily compares hardware runs against noiseless quantum simulations and classical GCI outputs rather than against full classical Monte Carlo baselines. For the GCI sub-circuit, they state that the best-tuned hardware-ready circuit yields output distributions and VaR estimates that align with those from the simulated transpiled circuit and a classical baseline, concluding that the simulator cannot directly reproduce experimental behavior without modeling hardware noise. They also emphasize that standard transpilation and noiseless simulation, while useful references, ignore device-specific noise, in contrast to their hardware-informed retuning.
    [codes: C-040, C-042, C-043, C-044]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge several limitations: current NISQ hardware has few addressable qubits, high gate errors, and decoherence, necessitating very small proof-of-concept circuits. They note that SPAM and other noise sources cause asymmetries in the experimentally prepared distributions even when the analytical target is symmetric, and that optimal rotation angles are highly dependent on the specific qubit pair or triplet, limiting parameter portability. They explicitly state that noiseless simulators and standard transpilation flows do not capture device noise or sensitivity to particular error sources, and that no readout error mitigation was applied in their experiments, which they present as a methodological caveat.
    [codes: C-006, C-031, C-032, C-038, C-043, C-044, C-045]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include integrating readout error mitigation and error-amplification techniques into their quantum algorithm implementation pipeline, and developing or using quantum compilers that more fully adapt circuit structures and parameterizations to specific QPUs, thereby incorporating noise-awareness beyond basic transpilation.
    [codes: C-046, C-048]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors position their work as the first experimental investigation and implementation of a key Gaussian-loading sub-circuit within a QAE-based credit risk analysis algorithm on a superconducting QPU, serving as a small-scale, finance-inspired proof-of-concept. They also claim that their study bridges a gap between quantum algorithm specialists and hardware engineers by combining financial modeling (GCI) with pulse-level, hardware-aware transpilation and parameter tuning tailored to a specific superconducting processor.
    [codes: C-005, C-008, C-011]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study is explicitly hardware-aware: circuits are compiled to minimize depth and connectivity violations, then further adapted at the pulse level on a specified Contralto-D sQPU with documented native gates and DRAG-based single-qubit pulses. The team performs benchmarking of coherence and fidelity parameters immediately before experiments, selects specific qubits and parking strategies, and uses a combination of Qiskit’s preset pass manager, SABRE routing, and manual subcircuit replacement based on hardware tests. The uncertainty model sub-circuit is chosen because it is common to both VaR and CVaR, and the work derives analytic angle conditions for symmetric distributions to guide shallow ansatz design. At the same time, reproducibility is somewhat constrained by device-specific tuning, the lack of readout mitigation, and the absence of real market datasets, as the experiments rely on synthetic Gaussians and a stylized GCI model.
    [codes: C-003, C-006, C-009, C-010, C-017, C-018, C-019, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-045, C-047]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Superconducting QPUs are described as a particularly relevant platform for this type of NISQ-era financial application, and the work emphasizes benchmarking and device characterization as an important cross-cutting practice alongside algorithm design.
    [codes: C-007, C-028]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it implements and tests a quantum sub-circuit for Gaussian latent-factor loading in a credit risk model, which is directly tied to default probability estimation. Its main value for lending is as a proof-of-concept for quantum-assisted default prediction and VaR-style loss assessment, but it remains small-scale and hardware-specific.

=== PAPER c56a3ba7d912 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper tackles a real‑world, imbalanced credit‑risk task: predicting whether credit card clients will default, formulated as a binary classification problem on a public benchmark dataset that has been widely used in prior studies. The financial problem is explicitly framed as testing a hybrid quantum–classical pipeline for an imbalanced classification setting, where the reservoir layer is intended to expand feature dimensionality to capture nonlinear dependencies relevant for default prediction.
    [codes: C-001, C-004, C-012, C-013, C-025]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors implement a Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) layer on QuEra’s Aquila neutral‑atom analog simulator, embedded within an otherwise classical machine‑learning routine. They test two encoding schemes into the neutral‑atom reservoir—position encoding, where inter‑atomic spacings encode features, and local detuning encoding, where feature values map directly to local detuning amplitudes—and extract time‑series features as expectation values of single‑ and two‑qubit Z operators across a small number of timesteps chosen to respect coherence times. A classically dequantized reservoir is also constructed as a direct classical counterpart to the QRC layer.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-009, C-010, C-011, C-015, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-030, C-031, C-032]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: In noiseless emulation, the QRC‑based hybrid pipeline achieves F1‑scores that are competitive with a deep neural network and with a fully classical pipeline, especially for statevector emulations, while requiring only training of simple output classifiers. Performance saturates once a few hundred Hamiltonian evolutions (shots) are used, and hardware experiments show that increasing measurements beyond roughly 200–240 shots per sample brings little additional correlation benefit. On real Aquila hardware, classification performance is consistently worse than in emulation due to noise and decoherence, with correlations degrading at later timesteps, but detuning encoding generally performs as well as or better than position encoding, and performance improves on the larger hardware subsets as classifiers can better learn to disregard machine‑induced noise. Across classifiers, the authors report that QRC plus simple classifiers can reach performance comparable to more complex neural networks, while training time trends depend on resampling strategy, with some classical methods (notably SVM) incurring prohibitive training times.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-048, C-050, C-051]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The study deliberately benchmarks the hybrid QRC pipeline against two classical baselines: a fully classical pipeline that applies traditional classifiers directly to preprocessed features, and a Classical Reservoir Computing (CRC) variant that replaces the QRC layer with a mathematically equivalent classical reservoir. In noiseless emulation, QRC’s predictive performance (F1‑score) is described as competitive with the fully classical pipeline and with a deep neural network benchmark, while also offering shorter training times and lower computational resource requirements than the DNN when paired with simple classifiers. The authors also observe that SVM, as a classical baseline within their pipeline, suffers from prohibitive training times relative to other classical classifiers, underscoring the appeal of combining QRC with simpler models.
    [codes: C-001, C-018, C-019, C-037, C-040, C-042, C-050, C-051]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors emphasize that hardware noise and decoherence on the neutral‑atom platform significantly degrade performance relative to noiseless emulation, with clear deterioration of correlations at later timesteps. They also note that increasing measurement counts beyond about 200 shots does not materially improve correlation, implying practical limits to statistical mitigation of noise. A major scalability limitation is the finite atom‑register size on Aquila, which constrains both how much input data can be encoded and the achievable sampling rate. Due to limited quantum resources, they cannot process the full dataset on hardware and instead resort to nested smaller subsets. Methodologically, they caution that accuracy is an unsuitable, potentially misleading metric for their imbalanced problem and therefore avoid it. Finally, they report that certain classical models within their pipeline, such as SVM, have prohibitively high training times, which constrains their practicality as baselines.
    [codes: C-005, C-021, C-036, C-042, C-043, C-045, C-046, C-052]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future work, the authors propose exploring gate‑based (digital) implementations of Quantum Reservoir Computing on the same credit‑default dataset, suggesting that digital QRC could enable methods more specifically tailored to the dataset than what is possible on the analog neutral‑atom platform.
    [codes: C-053, C-054]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions its main contribution as defining and benchmarking a hybrid quantum–classical reservoir‑computing pipeline—implemented on a neutral‑atom analog platform with position and local‑detuning encodings—for a realistic, imbalanced credit‑card default prediction task, and showing that in noiseless emulation this QRC layer combined with simple classifiers can match the performance of more complex deep neural networks at substantially lower training cost. The authors further argue that the flexibility, scalability characteristics, and hybrid nature of QRC make it a promising near‑term approach compared to QML schemes where learning is performed solely in the quantum module.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-007, C-014, C-015, C-025, C-040, C-049, C-050, C-051]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the authors motivate their focus on reservoir computing as a way to reduce training complexity by restricting learning to a simple output layer and leveraging a fixed high‑dimensional dynamical system. They select a widely cited, publicly available credit‑card dataset to support reproducibility and apply a detailed preprocessing pipeline: data cleaning that removes inconsistent samples, one‑hot encoding for categorical variables, scaling, PCA to retain 11 components explaining ~99% of variance, and hierarchical clustering to aggregate 24 original features into 12 groups before the reservoir expands dimensionality again. Because of limited quantum resources, they construct nested smaller datasets for hardware runs. They carefully treat class imbalance with both over‑ and under‑sampling methods applied only to the training set, select hyperparameters using validation‑set F1‑score instead of accuracy to avoid misleading assessments, and include multiple classical classifiers (including a DNN) for benchmarking. Timing parameters of the quantum evolution are chosen to respect coherence constraints, and the number of measurements is empirically tuned by correlation analysis to an optimal measurement count.
    [codes: C-008, C-009, C-013, C-014, C-017, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-031, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-041, C-046, C-047]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors note that position encoding, by requiring an additional qubit, yields a higher‑dimensional reservoir feature space than detuning encoding, which may influence representational capacity. They also observe that increasing dataset size in hardware experiments helps classical classifiers learn to recognize and discard machine‑induced noise, and they interpret the hybrid design—where learning occurs mainly in the classical stage—as particularly advantageous under current NISQ‑era constraints.
    [codes: C-030, C-048, C-049]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to credit_lending because it studies credit card default prediction, an imbalanced binary classification problem central to credit scoring and lending risk decisions. Its main value for the silo is the comparison of a hybrid quantum-classical reservoir pipeline against classical baselines under realistic preprocessing, imbalance handling, and hardware-noise constraints.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]

    [codes: C-013, C-017, C-020, C-022, C-023, C-024  dim_source: dimension_8]

=== PAPER c60694a1e2ed (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames credit risk assessment as a core input to contemporary financial decision-making and argues that existing classical machine learning approaches, while improving predictiveness, are increasingly constrained by scalability when applied to high-dimensional financial datasets with thousands of features and millions of entities that generate exponential computational loads. Quantum computing is proposed as a potential solution to these computational barriers in credit risk modeling and related risk processes.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-004, C-005]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors conceptually survey several quantum machine learning techniques for credit risk, including quantum principal component analysis for dimensionality reduction, quantum support vector machines using quantum kernel machinery for classification, quantum linear systems algorithms (HHL) for regression and scoring, amplitude estimation for speeding Monte Carlo-style risk analysis, and quantum neural networks based on parameterized quantum circuits for pattern recognition. They emphasize hybrid quantum–classical architectures, where computationally intensive subroutines (e.g., variational quantum circuits, quantum kernels) are executed on quantum hardware while data preparation and optimization remain classical, and they note that quantum kernels and feature maps may enhance credit analysis once classical data are encoded into quantum states.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-017, C-018, C-021, C-028]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: The paper does not report new empirical or quantitative results but summarizes theoretical performance claims from the literature: quantum amplitude estimation can in theory offer quadratic speedups in Monte Carlo-style risk estimation, reducing precision scaling from O(1/ε²) to O(1/ε); quantum support vector machines may discover complex nonlinear interactions among credit variables and, for certain well-structured problems, theoretically improve scalability to O(log n); and quantum risk analysis and portfolio optimization methods have been shown in theory to scale better than classical approaches for high-dimensional, nonlinear financial data. The authors further claim that quantum machine learning could improve credit scoring by extracting subtle patterns from diverse data and that quantum kernels may enhance credit analysis and, potentially, more inclusive credit provision.
    [codes: C-010, C-014, C-016, C-021, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-029, C-030, C-022]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors contrast quantum and classical approaches by noting that classical machine learning has already improved predictive performance in credit risk but runs into scalability issues on large, high-dimensional datasets, whereas quantum methods are posited to alleviate some of these burdens. They highlight that classical SVMs generally offer greater interpretability through explicit visualization of support vectors and decision boundaries, while QSVMs trade off some interpretability for the potential to implicitly map data into exponentially larger feature spaces and theoretically reduce training complexity from polynomial to logarithmic time in specific structured cases. They further state that quantum risk analysis and portfolio optimization, via amplitude estimation and related methods, have been theoretically shown to scale better than classical Monte Carlo-based techniques in terms of precision-dependent complexity.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-004, C-008, C-015, C-016, C-024, C-025]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper emphasizes several limitations and caveats. On the hardware side, current devices are in the NISQ regime, with decoherence and noise posing particular challenges for long-running financial algorithms, and hardware constraints are described as the main obstacle to practical implementation. On the data and algorithmic side, encoding classical credit data into quantum states is identified as a fundamental challenge, and the O(n) gate cost of loading n features can become a preprocessing bottleneck that may negate putative quantum speedups. The authors also point out that widely used quantum software frameworks such as Qiskit and PennyLane, while helpful, impose significant constraints on financial applications. Finally, they implicitly acknowledge that most advantages discussed are still theoretical, contingent on overcoming these hardware, encoding, and software limitations.
    [codes: C-003, C-011, C-019, C-020, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-016, C-024]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: For future directions, the authors point to the development and refinement of hybrid quantum–classical solutions and variational quantum algorithms as particularly promising in the near term, enabled in part by industry–academic partnerships and prototype implementations that are accelerating progress toward realizing theoretical quantum advantage. They suggest that quantum kernels and alternative credit scoring methods could support more inclusive lending by better discriminating risk among previously marginalized borrowers and that modular adoption strategies, starting with quantum solutions for specific computational bottlenecks in risk processes, can gradually expand to more holistic applications. In the long run, they envision risk assessment systems that are potentially real-time, explainable, and more inclusive, contingent on advances in hardware and methods.
    [codes: C-012, C-022, C-027, C-028, C-031, C-036, C-037]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The paper positions itself as a forward-looking conceptual review that synthesizes foundations of quantum machine learning and maps them specifically onto credit risk assessment and related financial risk tasks. Its distinctive contribution is to link concrete QML primitives—such as QSVMs, QNNs, quantum PCA, HHL, and amplitude estimation—to credit classification, scoring, portfolio optimization, and inclusive lending scenarios, while arguing that high-dimensional, nonlinear credit datasets are particularly promising for benefiting from quantum processing and that benchmarking should jointly evaluate predictive performance and computational resources.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-013, C-017, C-021, C-023, C-026, C-029, C-030, C-022, C-031, C-037]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: The article is conceptual and does not present original experiments, datasets, or quantitative benchmarks; instead, it argues that any future evaluation of quantum versus classical credit risk models should employ carefully constructed benchmarking frameworks that jointly consider predictive accuracy and computational resource requirements. It also stresses that hybrid systems should be designed so that computationally intensive subroutines are offloaded to quantum processors, while classical systems handle data preparation and optimization, implying a need for clear methodological partitioning in future empirical studies.
    [codes: C-010, C-018, C-023, C-024, C-025]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: Quantum cloud services are noted as a practical transition mechanism, enabling financial institutions to access quantum computing through familiar interfaces while they begin with modular approaches that target specific computational bottlenecks in risk processes. These infrastructural and rollout considerations complement the methodological and hardware-focused discussions elsewhere in the paper.
    [codes: C-035, C-036]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it maps quantum machine learning methods directly onto credit scoring, default classification, and alternative lending decisions, while also highlighting the practical limits that currently block deployment. Its main value for this silo is the discussion of how quantum kernels, QSVMs, and hybrid workflows could affect borrower risk assessment and potentially expand access to credit.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: The paper directly frames credit risk assessment as the basis for lending decisions and proposes quantum computing as a response to scaling limits in large credit datasets.
    [codes: C-001, C-003, C-004, C-005  dim_source: dimension_1]
  KEY_POINT 2: QSVMs and quantum feature methods are presented as tools for credit classification and credit analysis, with claims that they can capture complex nonlinear borrower interactions.
    [codes: C-013, C-014, C-021, C-030  dim_source: dimension_2]
