=== PAPER f88bb4cc0c22 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper frames the overarching problem as how machine learning, including quantum machine learning, can transform financial decision‑making across tasks such as fraud detection, AML, algorithmic trading, forecasting, credit scoring, portfolio optimization, risk assessment, and option pricing, while overcoming computational constraints that limit classical ML in complex, high‑dimensional financial settings. QML is specifically framed as integrating quantum‑computation principles with intelligent financial systems to exploit superposition, entanglement, and quantum parallelism to address these classical limits.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-004, C-007, C-011, C-013, C-033, C-039, C-041, C-043]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: As a systematic review, the paper surveys multiple quantum approaches rather than implementing a single method: QSVM/QSVC with quantum kernels and feature maps, QNNs and variational quantum classifiers, quantum clustering, QAOA for combinatorial portfolio optimization, QAE and quantum Monte Carlo for option pricing and market simulations, QGAN and QCBM generative models, and QRL for high‑frequency trading. It also discusses a generic QML workflow in finance where classical financial data are first encoded into quantum states, often via quantum feature maps and encodings within hybrid quantum‑classical architectures, supported by emerging QML tools and frameworks that integrate quantum algorithms into AI workflows.
    [codes: C-004, C-022, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-034, C-039, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-054]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: At review level, the authors report that QML algorithms exhibit advantages across hardware dependency, accuracy, training efficiency, data‑handling capacity, and computational speed compared with classical ML. They cite specific quantitative results such as QSVC with ZFeatureMap achieving an F1‑score of 0.98 on fraud detection, quantum anomaly detection gaining over 10% in average precision over classical kernels, QNNs reaching 97.63% accuracy versus 91.20% for classical networks in credit default prediction, QAE offering quadratic speedup over classical Monte Carlo for option pricing, QGAN/QCBM improving forecast accuracy and lowering uncertainty, QRL agents outperforming classical RL in accuracy and flexibility, and a hybrid quantum‑classical credit risk model achieving 83.47% and 81.12% accuracy for different loan products. They also state that quantum clustering is more efficient than classical clustering for organizing financial data and that QML techniques are increasingly preferred in credit risk modeling because of their high‑dimensional processing capacity.
    [codes: C-023, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-039, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-046, C-047]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The review systematically contrasts QML with classical ML, arguing that QML may surpass classical algorithms by exploiting superposition, entanglement, and quantum parallelism, and stating that QML algorithms show clear advantages in accuracy, training efficiency, data handling, and computational speed versus their classical counterparts. Specific comparisons include claims that QSVM can classify complex financial data such as fraud and credit risk more accurately and faster than classical SVM; QNNs are reported to outperform classical neural networks in credit default prediction (97.63% vs. 91.20% accuracy); quantum clustering is described as more efficient than classical clustering; quantum anomaly detection is said to outperform RBF kernels by over 10% in average precision; QRL agents are claimed to outperform classical RL agents in accuracy and flexibility; and QAE is presented as providing a quadratic speedup over classical Monte Carlo for option pricing. At the same time, the authors emphasize that many of these advantages are theoretical or demonstrated in limited settings and that classical ML remains widely used but constrained by domain‑specific issues in finance.
    [codes: C-003, C-019, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-037, C-039, C-041, C-043, C-046, C-049, C-059]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge numerous limitations for both classical ML and QML in financial services. For classical ML, they highlight challenges such as the ineffectiveness of traditional rule‑based fraud detection against evolving fraud techniques, scalability and adaptability issues in traditional AML methods, overfitting, model generalization and data accessibility problems in algorithmic trading, and the black‑box nature of ML credit scoring models that complicates explanations to customers and regulators; they further state that CML in finance faces constraints inhibiting its deployment in mission‑critical systems. For quantum computing and QML, they emphasize NISQ‑era hardware constraints including low qubit counts, high noise, and operational errors, as well as broader implementation obstacles in real‑world QC. Specifically for QML, they stress that despite theoretical advantages, practical application in finance is hindered by obstacles such as hardware and data issues, immaturity of the field, gaps in algorithmic optimization and scalability, and unresolved problems around noise and decoherence, lack of scalable quantum circuits for large financial datasets, and absent evaluation and benchmarking standards. They also note that algorithmic trading and HFT remain latency constrained by current hardware and decoherence times, that encoding classical financial data for quantum processing is a critical research bottleneck, and that deploying AI‑driven models—classical and quantum—into live financial environments requires overcoming significant real‑world obstacles.
    [codes: C-008, C-010, C-012, C-015, C-018, C-021, C-040, C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051, C-055, C-057]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The paper outlines several future directions, including advancing explainable and ethical AI in classical ML to establish trust, fairness, and privacy in automated financial decisions, and developing explainable quantum AI frameworks for transparent, fair, and compliant financial decision‑making. For QML, the authors argue that progress will depend heavily on the evolution and integration of quantum hardware with ML, enabling innovative tools for portfolio optimization, fraud detection, and risk analysis. They advocate hybrid quantum‑classical architectures that combine strengths of both paradigms as a key near‑term path, emphasize the need to resolve quantum data‑encoding bottlenecks, and call for research on noise‑resilient, scalable circuits and standardized benchmarking. They also propose a roadmap for industry adoption that includes pilot projects on real‑time risk analytics and quantum‑aware fraud detection, and suggest extending QML to actuarial calculations and complex derivative tasks via quantum parallelism.
    [codes: C-038, C-052, C-053, C-054, C-056, C-057, C-060]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors position their contribution as a comparative and integrative systematic review of classical ML and QML in the financial sector that not only surveys applications such as QSVMs, QNNs, and quantum clustering but also analyzes their relative advantages and challenges. They further claim novelty in proposing a new framework for assessing the suitability of QML in practical financial applications and in articulating QML as a new frontier that can potentially surpass classical algorithms by exploiting quantum phenomena to overcome classical ML limitations, including in mission‑critical financial systems.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-006, C-022, C-024, C-033, C-049, C-059]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper is a review article rather than an empirical implementation study, synthesizing results from multiple primary sources across fraud detection, AML, portfolio optimization, trading, risk assessment, and option pricing. It provides a high‑level QML workflow for financial applications, highlighting that traditional financial data must first be encoded into quantum states and discussing various QML tools and frameworks that democratize access and ease integration into AI workflows. However, the review‑level nature means experimental details such as hardware configurations, noise models, and dataset preprocessing are generally not specified, and the authors themselves note the lack of standardized evaluation and benchmarking for QML as an open problem, which limits comparability and reproducibility across reported studies.
    [codes: C-005, C-028, C-033, C-034, C-047, C-055, C-057]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper includes background exposition on quantum computing fundamentals, explaining how classical bits represent distinct 0 or 1 states while qubits leverage superposition to explore many states in parallel, and emphasizing that the power of quantum computation is underpinned by superposition, entanglement, and interference. It also reiterates in the conclusion that QML can, in principle, overcome classical ML limitations by exploiting these quantum effects, and notes that ML already plays a broad role in handling diverse financial problems and enabling real‑time fraud anomaly detection and adaptive AML through clustering and classification.
    [codes: C-009, C-011, C-014, C-016, C-017, C-019, C-020, C-052, C-059]
[SILO OVERLAY]

It also highlights the main deployment barriers for lending use cases: explainability, data encoding, noise, and benchmarking.

=== PAPER f96f14134a21 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets prediction of bond recovery rates—defined as the bond price 30 days after default—for use in risk assessment, portfolio allocation, pricing, and credit risk management, emphasizing that recovery rates are highly variable, often bimodal, and poorly captured by the common constant-recovery assumption around 40%. The authors argue that traditional statistical models struggle with the complex nonlinear relationships in these data and that their setting involves a particularly challenging 256-feature, heterogeneous financial dataset with a continuous recovery-rate target. They also note that the literature on recovery-rate modeling is relatively limited despite the variable and empirically broad distribution of recoveries.
    [codes: C-001, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-016, C-017, C-022, C-023]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study proposes a hybrid quantum-classical neural network in which a classical preprocessing layer feeds into a Quantum Neural Network that uses Amplitude Encoding to embed 256 classical features into the amplitudes of an 8‑qubit quantum state, exploiting the unitarity constraint of parametrized quantum circuits and the exponential compression of qubits. The architecture follows a four‑step pipeline, employs a strongly entangling PQC built from single‑qubit rotations and CNOTs with range‑1 connectivity, and measures Pauli‑Z expectation values which are passed to a classical output layer. The authors emphasize that amplitude encoding requires only n = log2 N qubits for N features, enabling them to encode all features simultaneously in a compact state while keeping the number of trainable parameters logarithmic in the input dimension.
    [codes: C-003, C-014, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-044, C-045]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: On the 1,725‑observation recovery‑rate dataset, the amplitude‑encoding hybrid QML model achieves an RMSE of 0.228, which the authors report as significantly lower than the 0.246 RMSE of the classical FNN and the 0.242 RMSE of the angle‑encoding QML baseline, and they state that it also requires fewer epochs and exhibits smaller RMSE variance. The recovery‑rate distribution in the data is broad and bimodal, with mean 48%, median 42%, and standard deviation 0.33, and the authors argue that the relationships between features and recovery rates are highly nonlinear. They also report that noisy‑simulator experiments and preliminary noisy conditions indicate that the hybrid quantum‑classical architecture can maintain the accuracy and robustness of recovery‑rate forecasts close to the noiseless case.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-022, C-023, C-041, C-046, C-007]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The amplitude‑encoding QML model is reported to outperform a classical feedforward neural network, with lower average RMSE, fewer required epochs, and smaller standard deviation of RMSE, and both quantum models (amplitude and angle encoding) are said to generalize better than the FNN. Compared to XGBoost, the authors state that their amplitude‑encoding QML model is competitive: XGBoost attains a slightly lower cross‑validation RMSE, but with higher variance such that it is not statistically significantly better across most epochs according to a Diebold–Mariano test, and in the conclusion they characterize the comparison as not demonstrating clearly superior performance for either side. They also situate their QML approach relative to broader ML practice by emphasizing that machine learning methods are well‑suited to capturing nonlinear, heterogeneous relationships that traditional statistical models cannot and by using FNN and ensemble tree methods as core baselines.
    [codes: C-005, C-006, C-018, C-036, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-046, C-047, C-038]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors explicitly acknowledge that all QML models are implemented and evaluated on quantum simulators rather than real hardware, which they highlight as a major limitation, and they further state that direct runtime comparisons between quantum and classical models are meaningless under these simulation conditions. They note that while amplitude encoding is qubit‑efficient, it leads to deeper, more complex circuits that are more sensitive to noise on real devices, and that the requirement for the feature vector length to be a power of two can necessitate padding or zero‑filling, potentially distorting inputs when this condition is not met. More generally, the paper concedes that its conclusions about potential quantum benefits are contingent on overcoming these hardware and encoding constraints in future implementations.
    [codes: C-048, C-049, C-050, C-051]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include exploring more robust and efficient quantum model implementations and alternative encodings to mitigate the depth, noise sensitivity, and padding issues associated with amplitude encoding. The authors also propose deploying QNNs on real quantum devices, which they say will require advances in circuit optimization and efficient gradient calculation, and extending the study from forecasting accuracy to assessing the economic value of QML‑based recovery forecasts in profitable trading strategies. Additional suggested avenues are testing whether QML models maintain performance across different market regimes or macroeconomic cycles and broadening the framework from standalone recovery‑rate prediction to more integrated credit‑risk applications.
    [codes: C-052, C-053, C-054, C-055, C-056]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The authors position their work as contributing to both QML and financial risk modeling by introducing a hybrid quantum neural network with amplitude encoding tailored to a high‑dimensional, heterogeneous recovery‑rate prediction problem where classical ML models are prone to overfitting. They emphasize three main contributions (though not all are detailed in the coded excerpts), including leveraging unitary quantum layers as a form of norm‑preserving regularization, exploiting amplitude encoding’s exponential compression to handle 256 features with only eight qubits and logarithmically many parameters, and empirically demonstrating improved accuracy, stability, and generalization over classical FNNs and angle‑encoding QML models on a realistic credit‑risk dataset. They also situate their work within an emerging literature on QML in finance and credit risk, suggesting that recovery‑rate modeling is a relatively underexplored application area.
    [codes: C-002, C-003, C-011, C-012, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-019, C-033, C-034, C-040, C-041, C-045, C-046]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the paper uses a proprietary global recovery‑rate dataset of 1,725 observations from 1996–2023 with 256 engineered features spanning firm characteristics, bond attributes, and macro indicators, and it defines recovery as the bond price 30 days after default. Data preprocessing includes z‑score normalization of continuous features using training‑set statistics only, binary mapping for categorical variables, and no imputation or PCA because the selected variables contain no missing values. The experimental design comprises a hybrid architecture with a clearly specified four‑step pipeline, comparison against FNN, angle‑encoding QML, orthogonal‑network controls, and XGBoost, and uses four‑fold cross‑validation and leave‑one‑out CV with RMSE as the main metric plus Diebold–Mariano tests to assess statistical significance; they also report that the quantum unitarity implies bounded spectral norms and prevents gradient explosion, which they interpret as supporting stability and generalization. However, all quantum results are simulator‑based, and runtime comparisons to classical models are explicitly treated as not meaningful under these conditions.
    [codes: C-004, C-011, C-018, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-031, C-033, C-034, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-042, C-048, C-049]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The authors discuss that amplitude encoding allows the quantum model to represent all input features simultaneously in a compact quantum state, and they argue that the number of trainable parameters in the amplitude‑encoded QNN scales logarithmically with the number of input features, which they view as a key efficiency advantage. They also highlight more generally that QNNs can exploit the exponential growth of Hilbert space to represent sparse high‑dimensional financial data with fewer parameters and that orthogonality and unitarity constraints act as local isometries with bounded spectral norms, which they interpret as a kind of regularization benefiting generalization.
    [codes: C-012, C-033, C-044, C-045]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it tackles a core credit-risk task: predicting post-default recovery, which directly affects loss estimation, loan pricing, and default-risk management. Its main value for this silo is the comparison of a quantum-classical model against standard ML baselines on a heterogeneous credit-risk dataset, though the evidence is simulator-based and not yet deployable on real lending systems.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: Recovery prediction is directly tied to credit risk management and pricing decisions.
    [codes: C-001, C-008, C-009, C-021  dim_source: dimension_1]

    [codes: C-004, C-016, C-020, C-021, C-025  dim_source: dimension_8]

    [codes: C-005, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-046  dim_source: dimension_3]

    [codes: C-006, C-042, C-043, C-047  dim_source: dimension_4]
  KEY_POINT 5: The main limitations are simulator-only evaluation and hardware-sensitive amplitude encoding, which constrain near-term lending deployment.

=== PAPER f9e5b6ee139d (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets binary classification problems in financial services, particularly fraud detection and credit/marketing tasks, motivated by the expectation that quantum kernels could improve predictive model quality and unlock profitable near-term quantum utility in this industry. A central technical challenge is that selecting a single suitable quantum kernel for a given classical financial dataset is difficult, and single-kernel models can suffer from exponential concentration as feature dimensions grow, which degrades trainability and ROC-AUC performance.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-003, C-012, C-022]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The authors propose a hybrid quantum multiple kernel learning (QMKL) approach in which several quantum kernels are combined into a single kernel with nonnegative weights optimized for each dataset and task. A classical solver determines these kernel weights using alignment-based strategies—kernel-target alignment with SDP, centered alignment, and an iterative projection-based alignment—to avoid direct quantum circuit optimization while still learning a suitable quantum-enhanced kernel. The method uses n-qubit feature maps that encode n-dimensional data, with a tunable scaling parameter α controlling kernel bandwidth and similarity, and incorporates projected kernels derived iteratively to improve robustness; for hardware, the projected method is ultimately chosen because of its empirical consistency. The hardware pipeline further relies on randomized compiling and pulse-efficient transpilation to reduce two-qubit gate overhead, together with measurement error mitigation via calibration matrices over all computational basis states.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-006, C-010, C-011, C-013, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-018, C-019, C-036]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: Across three financial datasets, the QMKL approach generally improves classification performance: on the HSBC fraud dataset the best model is the projected quantum MKL with simple averaging (PQ-MKL AVE), while alignment-based weighting yields a clear advantage only for fidelity kernels on that dataset. As the feature dimension increases, single-kernel models experience performance drops linked to exponential concentration, whereas MKL can overcome this issue and stabilize kernel variance and mean, improving ROC-AUC on two of the three datasets. On German Credit, quantum models are reported as comparable to the best classical model across feature dimensions, and on Bank Marketing the projected MKL with projection-based alignment (PQ-MKL PROJ) gives the best performance. In hardware studies, error mitigation substantially boosts agreement with simulations and enables scaling kernel-based QML up to 20 qubits, making this one of the larger QML implementations on real devices; projected kernels show more robust slopes and R² scores than fidelity kernels under noise, and MKL models are more robust than single-kernel learners, though some inconsistencies remain in specific settings such as MKL SDP at 16 qubits.
    [codes: C-007, C-008, C-009, C-020, C-021, C-022, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-034, C-035]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The authors compare their quantum methods against classical baselines and report that, for the German Credit dataset, the quantum models are comparable to the classical model, achieving equal or better performance across all feature dimensions. More broadly, they state that QMKL improves ROC-AUC scores on two out of three datasets, implying that in those cases the quantum or hybrid MKL configurations match or surpass the best classical alternatives, while acknowledging that gains are dataset-dependent.
    [codes: C-025, C-034, C-037]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The paper acknowledges several important limitations. First, choosing a single suitable quantum kernel for a given financial dataset is intrinsically challenging, and single-kernel models suffer from exponential concentration as feature dimensions grow, which inhibits model trainability and leads to ROC-AUC degradation. On real hardware, without the error mitigation pipeline the approach becomes unusable beyond about 4 qubits, underscoring the dependence on mitigation techniques; even with mitigation, some hardware results are inconsistent, such as an outlier behavior for MKL SDP at 16 qubits. The authors also note that performance gains vary by dataset, indicating that QMKL does not uniformly outperform classical or single-kernel baselines across financial tasks.
    [codes: C-003, C-012, C-022, C-030, C-033, C-037]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: The authors state that further improvements to QMKL are required for it to offer truly meaningful benefits in financial classification, specifically calling for extensions to larger feature spaces and datasets as the next step in assessing and enhancing its practical impact.
    [codes: C-038]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The central contribution claimed is a hybrid quantum multiple kernel learning framework that learns nonnegative weights over a family of quantum (and hybrid quantum–classical) kernels using classical alignment-based optimization, thereby improving classification quality over single-kernel approaches. They further claim that this design avoids the difficulties of variational quantum circuit optimization while helping to mitigate challenges of running quantum kernel methods on real hardware, and that the pulse-transpiled, mitigated implementation constitutes one of the larger QML demonstrations on hardware to date and a practical way to stabilize kernel statistics against exponential concentration.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-006, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-013, C-015, C-024, C-032, C-035]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the work frames financial services as a promising domain for near-term quantum utility through improved kernel-based predictive models, and systematically studies how the kernel bandwidth parameter α affects state similarity and trainability, including its role in exponential concentration. The kernel-weight learning relies on standard MKL alignment objectives (SDP-based kernel-target alignment, centered alignment, and iterative projection-based alignment), with the projection-based method iteratively constructing well-aligned kernels that were deemed consistent enough to be selected for hardware tests. On hardware, the authors deploy a multi-layer mitigation and compilation pipeline—pulse-efficient transpilation to reduce two-qubit error overhead and measurement error mitigation based on full calibration matrices—before evaluating robustness of kernel statistics (variance, mean, regression slopes, and R²) and ROC-AUC under noise, which supports their claims about the practicality of running QMKL at up to 16–20 qubits.
    [codes: C-001, C-006, C-008, C-011, C-012, C-014, C-015, C-016, C-018, C-019, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-035, C-036]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: None; all verified codes were integrated into the other reporting dimensions.
    [codes: —]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is relevant to credit_lending because it studies binary financial classification on the German Credit dataset and reports that quantum/hybrid MKL can match or improve performance relative to classical baselines. The main credit-lending takeaway is that kernel choice and feature-dimension scaling materially affect discrimination quality, which is directly tied to credit scoring and default prediction.
    [silo_relevance_score: high]
  KEY_POINT 1: German Credit results are competitive with the classical model across feature dimensions.
    [codes: C-025, C-034  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 2: Single-kernel models degrade as feature dimensions grow, while MKL can overcome exponential concentration and stabilize performance.
    [codes: C-012, C-022, C-024, C-035  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 3: Kernel selection is difficult, and the paper’s alignment-based QMKL is designed to learn kernel weights automatically.
    [codes: C-003, C-004, C-005, C-014, C-015  dim_source: dimension_2]
  KEY_POINT 4: The paper reports that QMKL improves ROC-AUC on two of three financial datasets, indicating dataset-dependent gains rather than universal improvement.
    [codes: C-034, C-037  dim_source: dimension_3]
  KEY_POINT 5: Hardware results show that mitigation and MKL improve robustness, but without mitigation the approach becomes unusable beyond 4 qubits.
    [codes: C-028, C-030, C-032, C-033  dim_source: dimension_3]

=== PAPER ff36740b3b52 (type: central+overlay) ===
[CENTRAL MEMO]
  DIM_1 PROBLEM_FRAMING: The paper targets credit risk assessment in the banking sector, framing it as a predictive modeling task where creditworthiness is predicted separately for different loan types (row-type dependent predictive analysis) to capture heterogeneous risk profiles across agriculture and personal loans. Traditional methods are presented as insufficient to capture the complexities and rapid changes in modern financial markets, motivating a more granular, loan-type-specific approach to portfolio risk and vulnerability analysis.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-006, C-007, C-004, C-029]
  DIM_2 QUANTUM_METHOD: The study implements a hybrid quantum-classical deep neural network (HyQuC-DeepNN-RTDPA) that embeds PCA-compressed classical features into quantum states via angle (used in experiments) or amplitude embedding, applies entangling gates to create correlations, and then couples these quantum layers with classical dense layers for classification. The pipeline includes row-type-specific preprocessing, SMOTE-based data augmentation, quantum encoding, entangling circuits, and integration of the quantum layer with deep neural network layers, with hyperparameters tuned via grid search on a quantum simulator constrained to 5 qubits/components.
    [codes: C-005, C-018, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-022, C-028, C-036, C-020, C-021, C-025, C-055]
  DIM_3 KEY_RESULTS: For personal loans, the hybrid model achieves an overall accuracy of about 0.83 with high precision for the majority Standard class (≈0.996), but very low precision (≈0.12) and recall (≈0.25) for the Sub Standard class, and a Cohen’s kappa around 0.33, indicating limited agreement beyond chance under imbalance; training accuracy (~0.67) is notably lower than validation/test accuracy (~0.83. For agriculture loans, the model attains roughly 0.81 accuracy and a Cohen’s kappa of about 0.46, with ROC AUC values highest for the Standard class (≈0.86) and lowest for Sub Standard, again reflecting difficulty in minority-class prediction. The authors argue that, within these constraints, the HyQuC-DeepNN-RTDPA architecture effectively identifies complex patterns in applicant profiles and supports credit risk analysis, while ROC/AUC and kappa analyses highlight differences in class separability and only moderate agreement beyond chance.
    [codes: C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-047]
  DIM_4 CLASSICAL_COMPARISON: The paper positions the hybrid quantum-classical model as a response to the limitations of traditional credit risk methods and classical deep learning, emphasizing that the hybrid design combines quantum computational efficiency with deep neural networks to address complex, row-type-specific risk patterns. However, the reported experiments do not provide explicit quantitative comparisons against classical baselines; claims about improved convergence, subtle correlation detection, and potential to overcome traditional model limitations remain qualitative.
    [codes: C-004, C-005, C-013, C-015, C-016, C-020, C-048]
  DIM_5 STATED_LIMITATIONS: The authors acknowledge multiple limitations: quantum algorithms and QML remain immature and resource-intensive, with adoption constrained by scalability and high hardware costs and current quantum hardware unable to scale effectively. On the modeling side, deep learning’s dependence on large labeled datasets is noted, and PCA-based dimensionality reduction, particularly the enforced reduction to five principal components due to simulator limits, may have discarded critical information and relies on linear, orthogonality assumptions that may not hold in complex financial data. Only angle embedding was tested, leaving alternative encodings unexplored. Class imbalance persists despite SMOTE, potentially biasing predictions and making overall accuracy misleading, especially for minority classes and specific subclasses such as Sub Standard and Loss. Hyperparameters of the quantum model may not be fully optimized, and interpretability is highlighted as a major challenge relative to classical models. Computationally, limited local hardware (16 GB RAM, 8-core CPU) and simulator constraints prevented more intensive analyses such as computing confidence intervals, further restricting robustness assessments and, together with the use of a single dataset and specific loan types, limiting generalizability of the findings.
    [codes: C-011, C-012, C-016, C-036, C-051, C-052, C-054, C-055, C-057, C-059, C-060, C-062, C-063, C-064, C-041, C-040, C-044]
  DIM_6 FUTURE_WORK: Future work directions include leveraging cloud or high-performance computing resources to overcome current computational bottlenecks and enable more comprehensive analyses, including confidence intervals and larger or deeper quantum circuits. Methodologically, the authors suggest exploring alternative quantum embeddings such as amplitude and superposition encoding, assessing additional data-balancing strategies beyond the SMOTE variant used, and considering nonlinear dimensionality-reduction techniques like t-SNE, KPCA, Isomap, or autoencoders. They also propose adapting interpretability tools such as SHAP and LIME to quantum models and indicate that the HyQuC-DeepNN-RTDPA architecture could be extended to related predictive tasks in financial services, including fraud detection, with broader adoption contingent on resolving the identified technical and methodological challenges.
    [codes: C-053, C-056, C-058, C-065, C-061, C-049, C-067]
  DIM_7 NOVELTY_CLAIM: The core contribution claimed is a systematic hybrid quantum-classical deep learning framework, HyQuC-DeepNN-RTDPA, specifically designed for row-type dependent predictive analysis in credit risk, which integrates quantum deep learning techniques, RTDPA, and data augmentation within a single pipeline. The authors argue that prior work on hybrid quantum-classical credit risk models has largely neglected RTDPA and that their approach uniquely tailors models to distinct loan categories, enabling more granular risk assessments and potentially leveraging quantum properties such as superposition and entanglement for enhanced convergence and detection of subtle feature interactions.
    [codes: C-001, C-002, C-007, C-008, C-009, C-010, C-013, C-014, C-020, C-048, C-066]
  DIM_8 METHODOLOGICAL_NOTES: Methodologically, the study uses a proprietary bank dataset partitioned into agriculture and personal loans, with explicit row-type-specific preprocessing that drops irrelevant features (e.g., dryland/wetland for personal loans), removes variables with over 70% missing values, performs PCA-based feature reduction, and then further truncates to the first five components due to simulator limits. Rows are segregated by loan subcategory, preprocessed individually, and then augmented via SMOTE to address class imbalance, though the authors recognize that imbalance remains and that accuracy can be misleading; they therefore report richer metrics including precision, recall, F1, ROC AUC, and Cohen’s kappa, and comment on discrepancies between train and validation/test accuracy as possible overfitting. Hyperparameters are tuned using grid search, but the authors admit that the quantum-side hyperparameters may not be exhaustively optimized. The entire implementation runs on a classical machine with limited RAM and CPU, and quantum experiments are performed on a simulator, contributing to constrained analysis (e.g., no confidence intervals) and raising reproducibility challenges given the proprietary data and absence of public code.
    [codes: C-022, C-023, C-024, C-025, C-026, C-027, C-028, C-029, C-030, C-031, C-032, C-033, C-035, C-036, C-037, C-038, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-042, C-043, C-045, C-046, C-051, C-052, C-059]
  DIM_9 MISCELLANEOUS: The paper includes broader conceptual discussions of deep learning’s capacity to autonomously learn hierarchical feature representations, the basic principles of quantum computing (superposition, entanglement) and their role in parallel information processing, and a high-level argument that resource and scalability challenges currently limit practical QML deployment in real-world financial systems. It also makes interpretive claims that quantum-inspired data augmentation can improve robustness and fairness and that the hybrid architecture could be relevant to a wider class of predictive economic tasks, framed under an ‘economic impact’ narrative rather than strictly technical evaluation.
    [codes: C-015, C-017, C-019, C-037, C-048, C-050]
[SILO OVERLAY]
  SILO_RELEVANCE: This paper is directly relevant to credit_lending because it targets credit risk assessment and loan-category-specific default/creditworthiness prediction. Its main value for the silo is the row-type dependent modeling of different loan types, plus the discussion of class imbalance, minority-class detection, and interpretability constraints that matter for practical lending decisions.

  PRIMARY_CODES: C-001, C-002, C-007, C-010, C-029, C-031, C-039, C-040, C-041, C-043, C-044, C-045, C-046, C-022, C-037, C-038, C-057, C-036, C-051, C-052, C-059, C-060, C-061, C-062, C-063, C-066

## Task

Read every evidence block carefully. Identify **descriptive themes** — patterns of claim, method, result, or limitation that recur across multiple papers in this batch. Descriptive themes answer *"What does the literature say about this silo?"* They describe patterns, they do not yet interpret them.

Themes must emerge **inductively** from the evidence. Do NOT force themes to fit pre-existing categories.

## Output Format

Return a JSON array of themes. ONLY the JSON array — no markdown, no prose.

```json
[
  {
    "theme_id": "DT-CL-B01-001",
    "theme_label": "<short descriptive name, 5-10 words>",
    "description": "<2-3 sentence description of what this theme captures>",
    "supporting_papers": ["<paper_id_1>", "<paper_id_2>", "<paper_id_3>"],
    "support_code_ids": {
      "<paper_id_1>": ["C-001", "C-007"],
      "<paper_id_2>": ["C-003"],
      "<paper_id_3>": ["C-011", "C-014"]
    },
    "evidence_summary": "<2-4 sentences: what the cited codes/memos actually say that constitutes this theme>",
    "dimension_cluster": "<which memo dimensions (1-9) or overlay sections contribute most; use free-form tags e.g. 'dim_2,dim_3' or 'overlay_key_points'>"
  }
]
```

## Hard rules

1. **Minimum support**: every theme must cite ≥ 3 distinct papers from this batch. Single-paper findings are NOT themes — omit them.
2. **Per-paper grounding**: for every paper in `supporting_papers`, you MUST provide at least one code ID in `support_code_ids`. That code ID MUST appear in that paper's evidence block (central memo support_codes or overlay codes). Do not invent code IDs.
3. **No phantom papers**: every paper_id in `supporting_papers` must be a paper in this batch.
4. **Theme ID format**: `DT-CL-B01-NNN` where NNN is the 3-digit theme index within this batch (001, 002, ...). No other format.
5. **Anti-anchoring**: do NOT use Phase 1 taxonomy codes (any of PD-01..PD-10, SA-01..SA-11) or their labels as theme names. Themes must emerge from the evidence, not from a pre-existing classification. Example: do NOT produce a theme called "Portfolio Optimisation" (that is just the silo name); DO produce "QAOA underperforms classical solvers on real-market constraints" if that is what multiple papers claim.
6. **Describe, do not interpret**: descriptive themes report what papers SAY; they do not yet explain WHY or what it MEANS. (Interpretation is B2.)
7. **Output JSON only** — no markdown fences, no commentary, no reasoning traces in the output.
